of the number of cuts; choosing the stopping scale from the actual H avoids accumulating its
defect along the masks. The resulting strict improvement contradicts extremality and proves the
proposition.

4.5   Planar incidences and classical labels of zero cost
We next exclude simultaneous zero quadratic and linear cost in the classical cylinder model.
We first formulate the planar incidence input so that its application to conditional probability
measures is explicit.

Theorem 4.7 (Discretized planar Furstenberg estimate). Let 0 < a ≤ 1, 0 < b ≤ 2, and ω > 0.
There are σ > 0 and ∆0 > 0 with the following property. Work in fixed bounded coordinate charts
for points and affine lines in the plane. Suppose that a probability measure ν on lines satisfies

                                    ν(B(ℓ, r)) ≤ ∆−σ rb     (∆ ≤ r ≤ 1).

For every line in its support, suppose that a probability measure µℓ on a set Pℓ within distance
C∆ of that line satisfies, uniformly in ℓ,

                                   µℓ (B(x, r)) ≤ ∆−σ ra     (∆ ≤ r ≤ 1).

If 0 < ∆ < ∆0 , then

                                ≥ ∆−F (a,b)+ω ,
                  [
                       Pℓ                          F (a, b) = a + min{1, b, (a + b)/2}.       (RW)
                   ℓ        ∆

Here | · |∆ denotes covering number; the constants may depend on the fixed charts and on C.

Proof. The separated-set version is the point–line dual of the discretized Furstenberg theorem of
Ren and Wang [25, Theorem 4.1]. We explain the passage from probabilities, which is the only
additional point needed here. Choose a′ < a and b′ < b sufficiently close to a, b for the prescribed
final loss. Take the slack σ in the hypothesis smaller than both min(a − a′ , b − b′ )/2 and one
quarter of the slack allowed by the cited separated-set theorem at a′ , b′ . Choose the additional
sampling loss below another quarter of that slack. Replace line parameters by representatives
of their ∆-grid cells. Points incident to a line remain within O(∆) of its representative. On



                                                     50
                             ′
the finite grid sample ⌈∆−b ⌉ line parameters independently from ν. For a dyadic ball of radius
r ≥ ∆, the expected number of samples is at most
                                             ′                    ′
                                     2∆−σ−b rb ≤ 2∆−σ (r/∆)b .

For any prescribed small additional power loss, binomial tails bound all these counts simultane-
                                  ′
ously by that power times (r/∆)b . Indeed there are only polynomially many grid balls to consider,
whereas the probability of exceeding a sufficiently large small-power multiple of the displayed
expectation and 1 decays faster than every fixed power of ∆. This also bounds multiplicities in a
single cell. The probability of one line grid cell is O(∆b−σ ), so the expected fraction of samples
                                    ′
repeating an earlier cell is O(∆b−b −σ ). Discard these repetitions at a small-power cardinality
cost. Perform the same grid sampling on each retained line with exponent a′ < a; its point-cell
                                                                                     ′
probabilities are O(∆a−σ ). The expected fraction of point repetitions is O(∆a−a −σ ). The number
of lines is polynomial, so the binomial count bounds hold simultaneously; by averaging, discard
any small fraction of lines with excessive point repetitions. Equalize cardinalities at a further
small-power cost. The resulting sets satisfy the nonconcentration and cardinality hypotheses of
the separated-set theorem. Its conclusion, continuity of F , and choices of a′ , b′ sufficiently close to
a, b give (RW). Small changes of width and projection of the point supports onto representative
lines change covering numbers only by bounded factors. All small losses are chosen before ∆
tends to zero.

   For a finite or countable discrete name U , side information D, and 0 < ρ < 1, put

                                                    − log P(U | D)
                                     iρ (U | D) =                  ,
                                                       log(1/ρ)

evaluated at the sampled value of U . Conditional probabilities are measurable versions on the
probability spaces of the cylinder construction.

Lemma 4.8 (Likelihood and list comparisons). For arbitrary additional side information D′
and ϵ > 0,
                             iρ (U | D) ≥ iρ (U | D, D′ ) − ϵ                        (LP)
outside a set of probability at most ρϵ . If I(U ; D′ | D) = o(log(1/ρ)), the difference of these
scores tends to zero in probability. More precisely, if the sampled U belongs on an event E to a
list L(V, D) of at most ρ−b names, then

                           P E ∩ {iρ (U | D) > iρ (V | D) + b + ϵ} ≤ ρϵ .
                                                                       


If V instead belongs to a list indexed by (U, D), reverse U, V in this comparison. Thus a change
of discrete names by such lists costs at most b + ϵ in the indicated direction. The exceptional
probability is measured on E under the original law; no conditioning on E is implicit. Finally,
restricting the law to an event of probability bounded below preserves any fixed finite collection of
normalized scores up to an error tending to zero in probability under the restricted law.

Proof. Write p(u | D) and q(u | D, D′ ) for the two conditional masses. Conditioning on (D, D′ )
and summing over u gives

                                 P{q(U | D, D′ ) < ρϵ p(U | D)} ≤ ρϵ .



                                                    51
This is (LP). If Z = log(q(U | D, D′ )/p(U | D)), the same argument gives P(Z < −t) ≤ e−t
and hence EZ− ≤ 1. Since EZ = I(U ; D′ | D), Markov’s inequality applied to Z+ proves the
mutual-information assertion.
   For lists, conditional on the known data, the total probability of names in a list of size L that
each have conditional probability less than ρϵ /L is at most ρϵ . Combine this observation with
the chain rule for conditional masses and the fact that P(U | D) ≥ P(U, V | D) at the sampled
names. The argument still applies if only values attained on the specified event are listed.
   Let E be the restricting event, with P(E) ≥ c > 0. The new conditional mass is

                                                              P(E | U, D)
                                P(U | D, E) = P(U | D)                    .
                                                               P(E | D)

Both factors in the ratio are at most 1. Under the restricted law, the probability that either is
smaller than ρϵ is at most 2ρϵ /c, by integrating the corresponding conditional probability of E.
Thus the normalized logarithm of the ratio tends to zero in probability. This also proves the
assertion with nonatomic side information.

Lemma 4.9 (Conditional planar incidence bootstrap). Fix a, g ∈ (0, 1] and ξ > 0. There are
a finite collection of pairs 0 < z ′ ≤ z ≤ 1 and a tolerance χ > 0 with the following property.
Let (Q, L, D) vary with ρ ↓ 0, where Q and L lie in fixed bounded point and line charts and are
incident up to O(ρ). Subscripts denote grid names of width ρz . If, simultaneously on the chosen
pairs, with probability tending to one,

                     iρ (Qz ′ | Lz , D) ≥ az ′ − χ,        iρ (Lz ′ | Qz , D) ≥ gz ′ − χ,      (cond)

then, with probability tending to one,

                                 iρ (Q1 | D) ≥ a + min{1, a + g} − ξ.                            (inc)

The same statement holds at any fixed output depth, with the target multiplied by that depth. The
side information and conditional law may vary along the sequence.

Proof. We first justify one bootstrap step. Suppose the unconditional line scores, conditional
only on D, are at least yz ′ − χ at a sufficiently fine finite grid of depths z ′ between 0 and a fixed
z > 0. We claim that the point score at depth z is at least (F (a, y) − ω)z in probability, when
the input tolerance and mesh are sufficiently small in terms of ω and z.
    Otherwise pass to a subsequence on which violation has probability at least a fixed c > 0. A
violating point belongs to a cell of conditional mass greater than ρ(F (a,y)−ω)z ; for each D there
are fewer than ρ−(F (a,y)−ω)z such cells. Intersect this event with all the good input-score events.
Choose a value of D for which this intersection has conditional probability bounded below, say
by c/2. Retain fine line cells on which the conditional probability of the intersection is at least
c/4. Their total retained witness mass is bounded below in terms of c. Restrict the original line
probability to these witnesses and normalize. Any occupied coarser line cell contains a good
                                                                   ′
witness, so its original mass satisfies the required bound ρyz −χ ; restriction and normalization
cost only a constant depending on c.
    In each retained fine line cell, restrict its conditional point probability to witnesses and
normalize. Each occupied coarser point cell contains a witness to the first inequality of (cond),
                                                    ′
so its mass is bounded by a constant times ρaz −χ . Cover Euclidean balls by boundedly many
grid cells. Between successive queried scales use the next larger queried cells; a depth mesh of

                                                      52
size ϑ costs at most ρ−2ϑ in the ball estimates. At the coarsest end the trivial probability bound
gives the same small-power loss. Thus, in units ∆ = ρz , the retained line and point measures
meet Theorem 4.7 with exponents y, a and arbitrarily small nonconcentration slack. Replace
each fine line cell by a representative line; its witnesses move by O(∆). Equation (RW), with a
loss less than ωz, contradicts the upper bound on the number of popular point cells. This proves
the step. Point–line duality proves the symmetric step.
    By (LP), the starting marginal bounds are x = a and y = g, up to arbitrarily small losses.
The two steps iterate
                                 x ←− F (a, y),       y ←− F (g, x).
Use the preceding values on both right-hand sides, so the sequences are increasing and bounded.
At their fixed point, y > a and x > g. For example, y ≤ a would give x = a + y ≥ 2y and then
F (g, x) > y, a contradiction. Consequently the fixed-point equations reduce to

                   x = a + min{1, (a + y)/2},          y = g + min{1, (g + x)/2}.

This map is a contraction in the maximum norm. Its fixed point is (2a + g, a + 2g) when a + g ≤ 1,
and (1 + a, 1 + g) when a + g ≥ 1. Thus finitely many iterations suffice to reach the strict target
in (inc). Choose the finite grids and tolerances backwards from the final iteration. The argument
for output depth z uses ρz as basic scale and is identical after this change of units.

    We now return to a classical cylinder extremal configuration. Assume that both minimal
shear costs vanish, normalize the terminal widths to r1 = f1 = 1, and retain the notation of the
cylinder construction:

             Γ = d + (1 − p)/2,    p ≥ 1,    0 < d ≤ 1,        Ea = es−da ,        κa = κspa .

The assigned terminal law is P1 . All following statements use the ordered limits and finite
meshes of that construction. In particular, ordinary regularizations are inserted in forward
order, their total energies and regularity constants obey the displayed benchmarks up to s−o(1) ,
the documented terminal count is at most s−o(1) E1 /κ1 , and terminal assigned mass is at least
so(1) E1 .
    Let xc , nc be the unit-bin indices of the exact velocities X, N and set

                        A = (X − xc , N − nc ),        V (A) = (A1 , A21 , A2 ).

Use boosted spatial coordinates

                             (Bt − txc , Yt − 2xc Bt + tx2c , Dt − tnc ).

Their velocity is V (A), and A lies in a fixed bounded square. For 0 ≤ a ≤ 1, define the nested

Lemma 4.10 (Geometric and probabilistic bounds for the boosted names). Suppose Γ > η/2.
The preceding configuration has the following properties, simultaneously on every fixed finite list
of depth queries.

                                                  53
  1. A terminal label determines C(1) to s−o(1) possibilities. After deterministic flattening of
     the relative scores of C(a) given C(0), their limiting profile D(a) is nondecreasing and
     Lipschitz, with D(0) = 0 and D(1) ≤ p + d.

  2. For 0 < v < 1 and 0 ≤ a′ ≤ 1, the typical score of A(v) given either its terminal label
     or C(a′ ) is at least (3 − p)v in log(1/s) units. Every fixed strict deficit has power-small
     probability.

  3. For fixed 0 < a < a + zh ≤ 1, the time name at width sa+zh , conditional on the exact
     particle and C(a), has typical score at least dzh in log(1/s) units, with the same meaning
     of strict deficit.

  4. Fix K = 3, a > 0, and h > 0 with a + Kh < 1. For every ϵ > 0, uniformly over measurable
     choices of an affine plane ΠC(a+Kh) ⊂ R3 ,

                               P1 {dist(V (A), ΠC(a+Kh) ) ≤ sϵh } ≤ sc+o(1)

      for some c > 0 depending on the fixed parameters and strict gap.

Proof. Zero shear cost and Taylor expansion on a terminal test imply unsheared velocity widths
s−o(1) and spatial widths s1−o(1) after a bounded velocity boost. The possible unit boost bins
therefore cost only s−o(1) per label. This proves the first assertion about C(1). Conversely, for
each fixed value c0 of C(0), take the union of the root predecessors of all terminal samples
with C(0) = c0 . This whole union lies in only boundedly many root time/test pairs of bounded
weight: A and terminal time are bounded, so exact backflow places the initial boosted positions
in a bounded box. Consequently a list of at most sτ e/κ values of C(0) has predecessor mass at
most Csτ e, for every fixed τ > 0. Rarity transfer (Sp) makes the corresponding terminal event
power-small. Apply this to the list of atoms with probability greater than s−τ κ/e. This is the
popular-list argument of Lemma 3.23, and gives the typical lower score log1/s (e/κ) − o(1) for
C(0) without counting terminal times separately. The documented count and the label-to-name
list give the typical upper score log1/s (E1 /κ1 ) + o(1) for C(1). Their difference is p + d. Apply
forward deterministic flattening at final readout on successively finer finite lists. The relative
branching bound and Lemma 4.8 imply that all limiting increments lie between 0 and 4(b − a).
Dense-list extension gives the asserted profile and endpoint bound.
     For the angular assertion, ordinary-regularize at j = 1 − v. Fix a terminal label and an
angular sv -cell. In duration units of the j-interval, terminal spatial uncertainty is sv−o(1) in
each of the three boosted spatial coordinates. Backflow through a duration at most 1 adds
uncertainty O(sv ) horizontally and in the normal position. After tilting by the angular-cell center,
the velocity curvature contributes O(s2v ) in the last base coordinate, while terminal position
uncertainty there is still sv−o(1) . Consequently s−v−o(1) full tests with r = f = sv−o(1) cover all
predecessors. Each has weight s4v−o(1) , so their total mass is at most

                                  s−o(1) κj s3v = s−o(1) κ1 s(3−p)v .

Classical masks are pointwise contractions and do not enlarge this mass. Labels of read mass
below sτ κ1 have total probability at most sτ −o(1) by the count. On the remaining labels the
conditional angular atom bound follows, with arbitrarily small τ . A label predicts C(a′ ) with a
subpower list. Testing popular angular atoms given C(a′ ), and pulling these lists back to labels,
gives the same bound by Lemma 4.8.

                                                  54
    For the time assertion apply Lemma 3.22, with the exact particle as common side information
and C(a) as target. At each of the cuts a and a + zh, use the current time and boosted-position
bins at the common width sa as relay names. Bounded boosted velocity moves position by
only O(sa ) throughout the depth-a interval. Thus the earlier row predicts the later relay with
bounded ambiguity, and that relay predicts C(a) with bounded ambiguity. The lemma and (Sp)
exclude lists of fewer than s−dzh+τ fine time names for any fixed τ > 0. Testing the atoms that
would violate the stated score gives the assertion. Approximating depths on the finite cut mesh
costs only the ordered vanishing exponent errors.
    It remains to prove plane avoidance. Write the plane condition as

                      |αA1 + βA21 + γA2 + c0 | ≲ sϵh ,         α2 + β 2 + γ 2 = 1.

Proposition 4.11 (Exclusion of classical zero costs). A classical cylinder extremal configuration
with Γ > η/2 cannot have both minimal shear costs equal to zero.
Proof. Suppose such a configuration exists and use Lemma 4.10. Put m = 3 − p. The relation
between p, d, Γ gives m = 2 − 2d + 2Γ > 0, while p ≥ 1 gives m ≤ 2. The profile supplied by
that lemma satisfies D(1) ≤ p + d. We will prove D′ (a) ≥ 1 + 3d at almost every interior depth,
contradicting p + d = 1 + 3d − 2Γ. At a short gap, conditional sampling and planar incidences
will force information in a two-dimensional projection of the spacetime position. Recovering the
two omitted coordinates from time uncertainty gives the required derivative bound.
    We first identify depth parameters at which conditioning changes angular information negligi-
bly on a short depth interval. For each dyadic h > 0, take subsequential limits, simultaneously
on dense depth lists, of
                                               H(A(Kh) | C(a))
                                  fh (a) = lim                 .

                        I(A(Kh) ; C(a + Kh) | C(a))

                                  h log(1/s)
in the ordered limiting sense. Fix such an a at which D is also differentiable.

in probability.

To see this, sample the vectors successively. Uniform conditional plane avoidance from Lemma 4.10

using measurable orthonormal coordinates. Write gij (Ak ) for the projective direction of πij e(Ak ).

                                             iρ (gij (Ak ))z | C ′ , L̸=k

at these depths. There are finitely many colors: scores outside [0, 2] can be assigned one overflow

M sufficiently large that the finite Ramsey theorem [24, Theorem B, p. 267] provides four indices
all of whose triples have the same color. Only this monochromatic four-set conclusion for a finite
coloring of triples is used here.
    We verify the recovery estimate used with this monochromatic set. For its largest index k,
choose the smallest index i and the other two indices j, ℓ. A direction for πij e(Ak ) specifies the
three-dimensional linear space span(ei , ej , e(Ak )); the other direction specifies span(ei , eℓ , e(Ak )).
The determinant bound implies that these spaces meet transversely in span(ei , e(Ak )), with
inverse norms at most ρ−O(ϵ) . Thus two direction cells of width ρz constrain any bounded
candidate with first coordinate 1 to within O(ρz−O(ϵ) ) of the affine line through ei , e(Ak ). On
this line, parameterized by (1 − t)ei + te(Ak ), the equation “third coordinate equals the square
of the second coordinate” has residual

The roots 0, 1 are simple with derivative bounded below by a power ρO(ϵ) . Consequently the
candidate angular parameter lies in a bounded number of balls of radius ρz−O(ϵ) . This is a list of
at most ρ−O(ϵ) cells at the queried width.
    It follows from the angular lower bound and list comparison that the joint score of these two
directions is at least mz − O(ϵ) − o(1), on transverse samples outside a vanishing exceptional set.

                                                          57
The sum of their individual scores is at least the joint score minus o(1) by (LP) and the chain
rule. Their colors agree, so each individual score is at least (m/2)z − O(ϵ) − o(1), simultaneously
at all query depths. Since there are only finitely many index choices, one fixed triple i < j < k
has these properties on an event of probability bounded below independently of ρ. Restrict to
that event. By Lemma 4.8 all previously obtained finite score bounds remain valid in probability,
with vanishing additional losses. One can further restrict to a fixed bounded slope chart after
one of finitely many fixed rotations, retaining positive probability.
    Set D∗ = (C, L̸=k ) and define
                           Q = πij O,     L = πij (0, Wk ) + Rπij e(Ak ).
These form a point–line incidence to accuracy O(ρK ) in bounded charts. We check (cond) with
a = d and g = m/2. After adjoining particle k, the line is known. Its projected speed is at least
                                                       ′
ρO(ϵ) , by transversality, so a point cell of width ρz permits at most ρ−O(ϵ) time cells of that
width. The conditional time bound and (LP) prove the first inequality of (cond). For the second,
adjoin C ′ . The point name is then known, and a line cell specifies its direction to boundedly
many direction cells. The score bound obtained from the monochromatic triple and (LP) prove
the required line score. All tolerances can be made smaller than the tolerance in Lemma 4.9.
Hence
                             iρ (Q1 | D∗ ) ≥ d + min{1, d + m/2} − o(1)
with an arbitrarily small prescribed strict loss.
    We must also recover the two coordinates removed by πij . Complete the quotient coordinates
to an orthonormal basis using first the unit vector along the part of ej perpendicular to ei ,
and then the unit vector along ei . Given particle j and D∗ , the quotient point Q1 has only
boundedly many possibilities: its velocity component is annihilated up to O(ρK ), because Lj is
known. The next coordinate has speed at least ρO(ϵ) and therefore reveals θj to a list of ρ−O(ϵ)
cells. Its score conditional on Q1 , D∗ is at least d − O(ϵ) by the time bound, the bounded-list
version of the chain rule, and (LP). For the last coordinate adjoin particle i. Both preceding
coordinates are predictable with bounded ambiguity, whereas the last coordinate reveals θi with
the same accuracy. This contributes another d − O(ϵ). The chain rule, bounded changes between
orthonormal coordinate grids, and (LP) to remove the pins give
               iρ (O1 | C) ≥ 3d + min{1, d + m/2} − arbitrarily small strict loss.
    The order of choices is as follows. First choose the target strict loss and the finite incidence
iterations and query grids. Next choose the color accuracy, transversality tolerance, and input
score tolerances sufficiently small. Then fix a finite Ramsey number M . Only after these choices
let dyadic h ↓ 0 at the selected a, taking all preceding scale and mesh limits first for each h. The
stationary information loss then tends to zero, and every event restriction after this information
comparison has probability bounded below by a constant depending on M , not on the inner
scale.
    Given C, the name O1 and the refinement C(a + h) determine each other with bounded
ambiguity. Thus its limiting score in log(1/ρ) units is (D(a + h) − D(a))/h, which tends to D′ (a).
Since d + m/2 = 1 + Γ > 1, the preceding inequality gives D′ (a) ≥ 1 + 3d for almost every a.
Integrating and using Lemma 4.10 gives
                              1 + 3d ≤ D(1) ≤ p + d = 1 + 3d − 2Γ,
a contradiction. This proves the proposition.

                                                58
5     Canonical names and conditional observations
This section constructs the information variables used in the positive-cost cylinder argument.
There are two distinct uses of probability. Canonical entropies concern the assigned law of a
single experiment. Independent observations and the walks constructed below are auxiliary
experiments, conditioned on a specified common side variable. We shall keep their laws separate.
    Throughout, we work on an affine extremal configuration satisfying (F), with ordinary
regularity imposed before the documented gates. Thus

                               r1 = f1 = 1,      p + d = 1 + 3d − 2Γ.

In the hybrid case the growth exponent is strictly larger than the classical comparison exponent,
so that (Pl) is available. The limiting conventions of the framework apply: every depth gap,
finite query list, and operator stack is fixed before the preceding approximation errors tend to
zero. In particular, an error denoted by o(h) at a tangent gap h is obtained by taking those
earlier limits first.

5.1     The canonical coordinates
For a real number or vector z, let [z]b be its absolute dyadic cell at width comparable to sb , or
the fixed representative of that cell when a coordinate is required. The convention applies also
to negative b. We also write X (v) for the angular cell [X]v . Let ta be the left endpoint of the
depth-a ancestor of the terminal time. Approximate gates may be used, with their errors taken
to zero before any subsequent gap is reduced.
    In the classical model, y0 denotes the exact particle, including all exact auxiliary indices. A
positive-time label on its realized path is determined by y0 and the corresponding interval start.
The label at the final assignment is still sampled. In the hybrid model the base coordinates are
exact; the sampled normal coordinates are extended backward as a classical ray only for the
purpose of defining names. Comparisons of this artificial ray with physical rows will always use
(Pl).
    A canonical name is a finite record of a ray in a specified shear frame. It stores the rounded
coefficients of that frame, then the base coordinates and the residual normal coordinates. For
example, a linear shear with recorded coefficient L replaces normal velocity and position by
N − LX and Dta − LBta . Their bins describe a cell in that known frame; they do not specify
an exact particle. The displays below choose which curvature coefficients to retain, and the
templates choose the precision of the entries.
    Finite partitions and their conditional entropies across resolutions also underlie the local
entropy methods of Hochman–Shmerkin [13, Section 4]. Here the records must additionally
retain the time gate and the changing shear.
Definition 5.1 (Curvature displays). Fix one of the following displays on an entire parameter
patch.
    (i) In the quadratic equality case, write

                     b(a) = b2 (a) = c(a − 1),     b1 (a) = (c − J)(a − 1),     c > 0,

       and put P ⋄ = P1 (F ). If c > 1, let Ra , ka be the rounded values of β1 (ta ), K1 , at depths
       b(a), b(a) − a, respectively, and extend Ra with slope ka from ta . If c ≤ 1, round only
       β ⋄ = β1 (T1 ) at depth b(a), and set ka = 0.

                                                 59
 (ii) In the pure linear case c2 = 0 < c1 , set Ra = ka = 0, b1 (a) = c1 (a − 1), and take P ⋄ to
      be the linearized terminal coefficient at the center of its own base test. Earlier tests are
      replaced by their enclosing linear tests from (F), with vanishing exponent loss.

 (iii) In the strict case c2 = 1, q < 1, fix an endpoint D ∈ (0, 1]; hybrid applications use D = 1.
       Use the encountered label λD , put P ⋄ = PD (F ), and first record

                                             k̄ = [KD ]b2 (D)−D .

      Subtract the common shear whose curvature trajectory is (k̄t, k̄), with zero other coefficients.
      All subsequent coordinates in this display are the residual coordinates. Set

                                    b(a) = b2 (D) + a − D         (a ≤ D),

      round the residual βD (TD ) at depth b(a) to obtain Ra , and set ka = 0. The actual derivative
      exponent remains b1 (a) = (q − J)(a − 1). For D < 1 the law remains the final classical
      assigned law; its encountered D-label supplies the data.
Write P for the specified derivative, after the global subtraction in case (iii).
   The horizontal resolution is v ≥ 0. We use one of the templates
                           template         l(a, v)           w(a, v)
                           quadratic       b(a) + v          b(a) + 2v
                            clipped   min{b(a) + v, b1 (a)} l(a, v) + v
                          pure linear        b1 (a)         b1 (a) + v.

The clipped template is used only when b′1 > 0. All derivatives of b, or of b1 when it occurs as an
active branch, are positive. An explicitly indicated enlargement parameter g ≥ 0 changes only
the normal phase widths from w, a + w to w − g, a + w − g. Unless indicated otherwise, g = 0.
Definition 5.2 (Canonical name). The name C(a, v) records k̄ when present, ta , Ra , ka , and

                                    L = [P − 2Ra X + 2ka Bta ]l .

It also records
                          [X]v ,     [Bta ]a+v ,        [Yta − 2[X]v Bta ]a+2v .
Finally it records the residual normal velocity and position at ta , at depths w − g, a + w − g,
after subtracting the shear
                            N 7−→ N − LX − Ra X 2 − ka (Yta − 2XBta ),
                          Dta 7−→ Dta − LBta − Ra Yta + ka Bt2a .
Here Ra in the displayed expressions is its value at ta .
    All names are used on significant polynomial support. The cutoff convention permits us
to remove negligible mass and enlarge a fixed polynomial support on each inner setup. Thus
finite names have the cardinality bounds needed for likelihood and entropy arguments. Exact
side variables need not have finite range. Conditional kernels can be taken on the standard
Borel codes of Lemma 3.19. Completing-path tests below are projections of the specified Borel
finite-path relations. Before another path test uses a completed-measurable row restriction, we
take its Borel version under that stage’s active row law as in the lemma.

                                                   60
Lemma 5.3 (Refinement and comparison). Fix a template and g. If both a and v increase, the
finer canonical name determines the coarser one up to bounded lists. The relative branching
exponent is bounded by a constant times the sum of the two depth changes. At approximated
gates the lists may have size s−o(1) . Names at nearby parameters admit mutual comparison lists
with exponent tending to zero with the parameter displacement.
Proof. The time cells are nested. For base coordinates, backflow uses Bt = Bt′ + (t − t′ )X,
Yt = Yt′ + (t − t′ )X 2 . After subtracting the tilt 2[X]v Bt , the unresolved contribution of the time
displacement is quadratic in the unresolved angular increment. Its sizes are therefore sa+v and
sa+2v . Changing the angular cell center produces its known linear correction and an error of the
latter size. These facts give both the base lists and their relative branching bounds.
    Compare two curvature displays in the coarse time units. Their differences have sizes O(sb(a) )
and O(sb(a)−a ). For a nonconstant display, extrapolation backward from the finer time preserves
these bounds because b′ − 1 ≥ 0. Constant displays require no slope comparison. After both
displays have been guessed, the unresolved part of their derivative difference has size at most
sb(a)+v , which is no larger than sl . Thus the derivative cells have bounded comparison lists.
    For completeness, the normal estimate uses the difference of the two shears, rather than
either shear separately. Its derivative on the actual base ray is O(sl ), since both displayed
derivatives approximate P . Its curvature difference has size O(sb(a) ) in the coarse time units.
On the unresolved base cell, Taylor’s identity bounds its velocity uncertainty by
                                    O(sl+v + sb(a)+2v ) = O(sw ),
and its position uncertainty by sa times this quantity. These estimates are stronger when the
normal widths are enlarged. Shear subtraction commutes with backflow. This proves the lists
for the residual coordinates even when the absolute shear or absolute position is large. The
same calculations with unequal resolutions give at most s−C(|∆a|+|∆v|)−o(1) choices. Comparison
through a common coarser pair proves the final assertion.

5.2   Entropy bounds and label prediction
We request deterministic final-readout profiles for the finite names used below. Let H(a, v) denote
the limiting entropy of C(a, v), divided by log(1/s), and put
              D0 = lim log1/s (e/κ),      W (a, v) = (3 + η)v + (1 − η)(w(a, v) − g).
Proposition 5.4 (Canonical entropy and endpoint recovery). On its domain of use, the excess
satisfies
                    E(a, v) = H(a, v) − D0 − (p + d)a − W (a, v) ≥ 0.              (canon)
For hybrid names this assertion is used only when g = 0, w < 0, and at their terminal limits by
continuity. The following equality and prediction statements also hold, with lists of size s−o(1) .
  (i) For D = 1, g = 0, equality holds at (1, 0). In the classical equality and pure linear cases
      with b′1 > 0, equality holds at v = u(a). In a classical strict family ending at D, it holds at
      (D, u(D)). At these equality points use the template with l = b + v = b1 , or the pure linear
      template.
 (ii) In a strict family every row connected from i < D to D, and its label, predicts k̄. After its
      subtraction,
                        |Ki | ≤ sb2 (D)−D−o(1) , |βD (TD ) − βi (Ti )| ≤ sb(i)−o(1) .

                                                  61
(iii) In an equality or pure linear family, suppose J > 0, a < j < 1, v = u(j) > 0, and

                                              l(a, v) ≤ min b1 .
                                                        [j,1]

     Then λj predicts C(a, v) on every completing path; in the hybrid case require w < 0.

 (iv) In the classical cases with b′1 > 0, fix 0 < a < j. If v − u(j) ≥ 0 is sufficiently small
      compared with j − a and l(a, v) ≤ b1 (j), then (λj , X (v) ) predicts C(a, v). The strict version
      uses j = D. There λD alone suffices at v = u(D); this last assertion also holds for hybrid
      D = 1 and w < 0, without the condition b′1 > 0.

No equality is asserted at an arbitrary point where min[a,1] b1 < b1 (a).

Proof. Fix a canonical name at depth a. On its ancestor rows the base coordinates lie in the
displayed base cell; after the displayed shear the two normal coordinates lie in cells of widths
sw−g , sa+w−g . Conversion between ta and the actual gate time has the same precision because
the residual velocity is known to that precision. Thus those rows are covered by a small list of
regularity tests of weight sW , in a small list of time intervals. Their benchmark mass is at most
spa+W −o(1) κ. Division by the assigned-law benchmark s−da e, and the sparse-list consequence of
(Sp), exclude cells whose typical probability is larger than (κ/e)s(p+d)a+W −o(1) . This is (canon).
In the hybrid comparison the artificial ray differs from the ancestor row by at most s−o(1) in
normal velocity and sa−o(1) in position, by (Pl). Since the base is identical, all shears preserve
this additive comparison. It fits the required widths when w < 0. The classical virtual endpoint
uses the same sparse-list argument on the final law.
     For the upper bounds at the stated equality points, the reached label determines the name to
a small list. Curvature comparisons are those of (F). If c ≤ 1, omitting the slope of the curvature
trajectory costs at most the own curvature width in its own duration units. The derivative
discrepancy along an actual ray is bounded by smin b1 −o(1) on the connecting interval. Once the
curvature cuts are guessed, recentering over the label’s base cell adds at most sb2 (a)+u(a)−o(1) to
that derivative. The Taylor calculation in Lemma 5.3 then gives the normal widths. In a strict
family the residual own slope is bounded by sb2 (D)−D−o(1) , so its omission is harmless at D. The
documented label count supplies the reverse entropy inequality.
     Assertion (ii) follows directly from (A). The slope comparison on [i, D] predicts the global
bin and gives the first bound. Multiplying this residual slope bound by the earlier duration gives
the artificial depth b(i). The coefficient comparison itself is finer, since min[i,D] b2 > b(i), and
gives the second bound.
     For (iii) and (iv), first guess the earlier curvature cuts. Backflow of the later base test, with
X when required, gives the earlier base widths: its uncertainties sj+u(j) and sj+2u(j) fit when
   (v)

the right excess v − u(j) is small compared with j − a. At zero excess the inequalities are weak
and the allowed small lists suffice. At the later time the unresolved angular part of the relative
derivative has size at most sb(a)+v−o(1) ; the unresolved position part of a curvature slope has the
additional later-time factor. The remaining derivative discrepancy fits by the stated condition on
b1 . Taylor’s identity now gives the normal comparison, using n(j) ≥ w(a, v), with strict slack for
a small positive excess.
     When a curvature slope was omitted, perform this comparison at Tj , not across the whole
earlier duration. The omitted slope has size sb2 (j)−j−o(1) ; its contribution from unresolved own
positions to the derivative is sb2 (j)+u(j)−o(1) . It fits the later normal widths. The unresolved
angular increment multiplies only the curvature difference, of size sb(a)−o(1) . Having compared

                                                  62
velocity and position at Tj , backflow gives the earlier position assertion. These bounds depend
only on the later label and the allowed common base region, so the lists are uniform over all
completing paths of that label.

5.3   Deterministic profiles and their traces
The final-readout flattening is performed on growing finite lists of queries, after the ordinary
gates have been prepared in forward order. Requests may include canonical names, angular
bins, mixed time and coefficient bins, and exact side data such as y0 . Each retained class has
exponent-zero fraction by the count constraint and minimality of p; subsequent final restrictions
preserve the earlier deterministic profiles. A virtual endpoint chosen after preliminary profiles
is fixed before requesting its additional profiles. Its exact side variable is common over that
request; no interpolation across unrelated virtual labels is used. The same procedure applies to
one intermediate label selected after the preliminary profiles: only the final readout is refined
again.
     For any fixed finite query list and any fixed positive tolerance, the profile assertions hold
off power-small sets. Indeed the flattening fixes the preselection log probabilities; likelihood
comparison bounds the change under an exponent-zero restriction, and list counting controls
exceptionally small probabilities. Consequently such deterministic profiles persist under a later
restriction of fraction so(h) , with errors vanishing in h-units after the earlier limits. This fact
does not assert stability of an arbitrary mutual-information budget under that restriction.
     Let
                                                  − log P(X (x) | C(a, v))
                                U (x; a, v) = lim
                                                         log(1/s)
denote the deterministic angular score. It also equals the limiting conditional entropy. It is
nonnegative, nondecreasing and 1-Lipschitz in x, and vanishes at x = v. Lemma 5.3 gives
Lipschitz dependence on (a, v). Its increments in x decrease when either conditioning depth
increases.
Lemma 5.5 (Angular trace and time stationarity). At almost every interior (a, v), and at almost
every v for each fixed admissible a, there is m(a, v) ∈ [0, 1] such that

                        U (v + p′ h; a, v) = m(a, v)p′ h + o(h)   (p′ ≥ 0).                   (ang)

The trace has a version nonincreasing in a at fixed v. Moreover, at almost every interior root,
and at almost every time on an admissible fixed-v boundary interval,

                         U (v + Gh; a, v) − U (v + Gh; a + Gh, v) = o(h)                       (ng)

for every fixed G, as h ↓ 0.
Proof. At a fixed a, take derivatives in x for a countable dense set of conditioning depths. Their
values lie in [0, 1], and are nonincreasing as the conditioning depth increases. Monotone envelopes
therefore define the trace from conditioning depths below x. For x ∈ [v, v + h], its derivative
at conditioning depth v lies between that trace and the derivative at depth x − ε, provided
h < ε. At common Lebesgue points, integration over [v, v + h], followed by h ↓ 0 and then ε ↓ 0,
makes the two bounds agree. This proves (ang). Countable envelopes and continuity recover
the original profiles. Time monotonicity follows from the corresponding monotonicity of every
difference quotient.

                                                 63
    For fixed dyadic h, the function a 7→ U (v + Gh; a, v) is nonincreasing and bounded by Gh.
The integral of its decrement over a translation of length Gh is consequently OG (h2 ) on a compact
interval. Divide by h and sum over dyadic h. The resulting summable nonnegative functions tend
to zero almost everywhere. Nesting bounds an arbitrary smaller gap by a comparable dyadic gap.
The same proof can be integrated in v, giving the interior statement. For a fraction-so(h) final
restriction, first transfer the deterministic scores. Chain rule and bounded relative name capacities
then recover the normalized entropy difference; direct conditioning of the old information budget
is not required.

Lemma 5.6 (Mixed coefficient trace). Let V∗ be an absolute scalar parameter and S a fixed side
variable. Flatten
                              H(x, z) = lim Hs ([V∗ ]x | S, tz ).
The x-derivatives lie in [0, 1] and decrease with z. On an affine graph x = B(z) of slope c′ > 0,
there are almost-everywhere traces 0 ≤ ν− (z) ≤ ν(z) ≤ 1. More precisely, at almost every a, fix
offsets α1 ≤ α2 and β for which the following queries are admissible, and put I = [α1 , α2 ]. Then

                     H(B(a) + α2 h, a + βh) − H(B(a) + α1 h, a + βh)
                         = h ν− (a)|I ∩ (−∞, c′ β)| + ν(a)|I ∩ (c′ β, ∞)| + o(h).
                                                                               


The coefficient-depth interval is split at x = B(a + βh); the displayed lengths are in units of h.
The larger trace is taken from earlier conditioning times at a fixed coefficient depth.

Proof. Use monotone envelopes of the derivatives for countably many time depths. At a point of
the graph, squeeze the integrand on each side between its trace and its value with time moved
by a fixed ε to that side. Lebesgue differentiation in x, the positive affine change of variable
x = B(z), and then ε ↓ 0 prove the assertion. Joint and conditional scores agree with these
deterministic entropy increments by chain rule and flattening. This argument uses no regularity
of a coefficient as a function of the exact particle.

    For later classical applications define p∗ (a) to be the supremum of depths with zero limiting
conditional score for the raw final derivative P1 (F ), given y0 , ta . Use the raw final linear coefficient
in the pure linear case. Then p∗ is nondecreasing; if b′1 > 0, p∗ (a) ≥ b1 (a), since the earlier
owned label and (A) predict that accuracy. Below p∗ , typical values admit lists of arbitrarily
small exponent; strictly above it their deterministic score is positive. At a virtual endpoint
with b′1 > 0, raw PD (F ) and P1 (F ) differ by at most sb1 (D)−o(1) , so these conclusions transfer at
strictly coarser query depths.

Proposition 5.7 (Memory consequences). Put k0 = 1 − d and
                                                                   (
                                                                       (q, q − J),   curvature families,
    Lraw = max{1, (1 + Q)/2, 1 + Crate },          (Q, Crate ) =
                                                                       (0, c1 ),     pure linear.

Whenever the left label comparison in Proposition 5.4(iii) applies at v = u(j), the angular trace
satisfies
                                         k0 + 2Γ + η(Lraw − 1)
                               m(a, v) ≥                         .
                                                  Lraw




                                                    64
Fix a classical depth j, and let λj denote the random label encountered there on the final assigned
law. Define Rj (v) = lim Hs (X (v) | λj ), with conditional entropy averaged over that label random
variable. Then Rj (u(j)) = 0, and

                                    Rj (u(j) + z)   k0 + 2Γ + η(Lraw − 1)
                          lim inf                 ≥                       .                       (mr)
                            z↓0           z                 Lraw
These lower bounds hold on the same flattened law at every tested boundary prefix whenever λj
predicts the boundary name; they are not assertions after conditioning on each individual label
value. Under the small right-excess comparison, m(a, v) ≥ Rj′ (v) almost everywhere. At typical
classical j with Crate > 0, the following improvements are available:

  (i) If p∗ (j) > b1 (j), with the extra prediction persisting immediately to the left, replace Lraw by
      max{1, (1 + Q)/2}.

 (ii) If Q ≤ 1 and 0 < Crate < 1, an additional lower bound is k0 + 2Γ − Crate ν(j), where ν is

Proof. Pull a proposed angular list back to λj using its canonical prediction list. Apply (mem)
with time gap h = z/Lraw , then transfer the resulting rarity to the final law by (Sp). This proves
the prefix statements and their derivative consequences. Conditioning additionally on λj , X (v)
proves the right-excess inequality.
    For the prediction variant of (mem) on [j − h, j], only a list for the slope at depth b1 (j) is
needed, given y0 , tj−h . If strict extra prediction persists, this list has arbitrarily small exponent
in sh . In case (ii), its cost is at most Crate ν(j) + o(1): the score at B(j − h) vanishes, and
the remaining interval is above the graph at the earlier conditioning time. Apply Lemma 5.6.
Restrict the final event to successful slope predictions, whose failure is negligible at a fixed strict
tolerance. Their lists are then available on every tested completing path. The prediction memory
bound and (Sp) prove both enhancements.

5.4    Common side variables and orientation marks
We now arrange for several observations to share a common phase, to the accuracy of the tangent
queries. The side variable below specifies this common frame; independence will hold after
conditioning on that variable.
    Write Dend = 1 for the quadratic-equality and pure-linear displays, and Dend = D for the
strict-curvature display of Definition 5.1. We now work at (a, v), with a > 0 strictly below the
chosen endpoint, and set δ = sh . A quadratic branch has l = b + v and rate c′ = b′ > 0. A
linear branch has l = b1 < b + v, or is pure linear, and has rate c′ = b′1 > 0. The following side
constructions are used only on patches where their strict inequalities hold.

Definition 5.8 (Admissible side variable). Choose one of the following variables Z.

  (i) Coarse quadratic side. Suppose l(a, v) < min[a,Dend ] b1 , and, in the hybrid case, w(a, v) < 0.
      Fix S = C(A, V ) on the same unclipped branch, with A < a, V > v, and

                                    V − v ≫ (1 + c′ + |q| + J)(a − A) > 0.

      Retain all strict domain inequalities and put Z = (S, ta , Ra , ka ). When label recovery is
      needed for c = 1, also require v, V < u(a) with slack.

                                                   65
 (ii) Exact quadratic side, classical. Let S = (y0 , k̄, [P ⋄ ]x∗ ), omitting k̄ when absent, with
      x∗ > l(a, v). The raw coefficient bin must have zero conditional exponent, or arbitrarily
      small-cost covering lists, given y0 , ta . One may choose x∗ < p∗ (a), and also x∗ < b1 (D) in
      a virtual comparison. Put Z = (S, ta , Ra , ka ).
 (iii) Coarse pure linear side. Take S = C(A, V ), with V − v ≫ (1 + c′ )(a − A) > 0, and put
       Z = (S, ta , L). Maintain w < 0 in the hybrid case.
 (iv) Exact linear side, classical. Put Z = (y0 , ta , k̄, Ra , ka , L), omitting unused entries. In a
      clipped branch require v > b1 − b, and remain short of a virtual endpoint. The additional
      bins have zero exponent given y0 , ta , by ownership and (A).
The anchors and static coefficient depths are fixed on a patch. Countably many such patches
cover all the stated admissible roots.
Lemma 5.9 (Common phase). Given an admissible side Z, choose a representative point of its
support and let C∗ be its root canonical name. Subtract the displayed root shear, center and boost
in that root cell, and dilate duration, horizontal width, and normal width by sa , sv , sw , respectively.
In this common frame there is a bounded phase
                  F = (X, B, Y, N, D0′ ),       with also P on a quadratic branch,
measurable in Z, which differs from the corresponding actual phase of every conditional observation
by O(sε ) for some positive patch slack. In particular the error is smaller than every fixed δ-power
when h is sufficiently small. The actual root name and C∗ determine each other to bounded lists.
The sampling scheme and the representative can be chosen independently of v on a smaller patch.
Proof. For an exact side, the phase coordinates are fixed by the particle. In the quadratic case
x∗ > l also resolves the residual derivative to finer precision than the root unit.
    For a coarse side first subtract the S-shear. Its base uncertainties are sV , sA+V , sA+2V , and
its normal uncertainties are sw(A,V ) , sA+w(A,V ) ; the same bounds hold after flow to ta . At a repre-
sentative base point the root and side derivatives differ by O(smin{l(a,v),l(A,V )} ). Their curvature
difference has size O(sb(A) ) in earlier-duration units. Changing to the fixed representative frame
therefore adds velocity error at most
                                 O smin{l(a,v),l(A,V )}+V + sb(A)+2V ,
                                                                      

with an additional factor sA for position; omit the second term in the pure linear case. The
inequalities in Definition 5.8 put all these errors strictly below the root widths. The quadratic
derivative itself varies by at most O(sl(A,V ) ). The change of base tilt fits because A+v+V > a+2v.
    An actual cell may differ from the representative cell at a grid boundary. Guess its boundedly
many neighboring linear-cut and angular-center cells first. A linear-cut difference multiplies
only unresolved base coordinates at their root widths when converting residual names; the
resulting errors are root normal widths. The tilt conversion has the same property. This gives
the two-directional lists without comparing normal coordinates in different frames directly. All
fine phase comparisons above are in the single representative frame.
    A measurable representative can be obtained by realizing the conditional kernel with a
uniform seed and fixing a seed that is valid almost everywhere, by Fubini. The same choice can
be made with the patch parameter under Lebesgue measure, and kept fixed as v varies. Only its
real coordinates and cell data are used. Conditional observations still use the original kernel; no
extra shared mark is conditioned upon.

                                                   66
   For the chosen endpoint Dend , put Tend = TDend , the start time of its endpoint interval. On a
quadratic branch put βobs = βDend (TDend ), in the coordinates of the chosen curvature display.

For the equality case c > 1 also include k = (K1 − ka )sa−b(a) . The endpoint is 1, or the fixed
