particular the linear branch has l = b1 (a), so its cut L is independent of v. Subscripts on marks
below denote ordinary bounded-chart bins at δ-depths. Stored marks always have a specified
finite cutoff depth, chosen before the inner scale limit.
Proposition 5.10 (Conditional angular dimension). Conditional on Z, the time mark has lower
surprise dp′ at every tested positive prefix p′ , to arbitrary strict tolerance. At a typical time root,
the joint mark increment on p′ → p′ + t, where p′ > 0 and t ≪ p′ , has conditional surprise at
least dt, given its parent and Z. More precisely:
  (i) If c′ ≥ 1, the time increment itself has this lower bound given the joint parent. In a linear
      branch with c′ > 1, one may also condition on rp′ +t when p′ + t < c′ p′ .

 (ii) If 0 < c′ < 1, the mixed trace gives ν ∈ [0, 1] such that the r-increment, given Z, θp′ +t , rp′ ,
      has exponent νt, while the time increment given Z, θp′ , rp′ +t has exponent at least (d−c′ ν)+ t.
      Their joint increment has exponent at least dt.
In the classical exact linear case with Q ≤ 1 and 0 < b′1 < 1, ν is the raw final derivative trace
with side y0 and graph b1 ; this also applies strictly before a virtual endpoint with b′1 > 0.
Proof. At the gate i = a + p′ h, every predecessor on a tested completing path predicts the static
side and the other root data to small lists. For a coarse side this uses exact base flow, the strict
normal slack for hybrid backflow, and (A). For an exact predicted coefficient, use its arbitrarily
small-cost list and discard its negligible final failure event. The row predicts θp′ , and it predicts
r to depth p′ if c′ ≥ 1, or depth c′ p′ otherwise. In a linear branch the latter accuracy always
holds. When c > 1, the predicted curvature trajectory is evaluated at the endpoint; its bounded
                                                                              ′
normalized slope makes replacing that time by the gate time cost only δ p −o(1) .
    Here is the causal counting step. Use Lemma 3.19: take a Borel inner version of the prediction-
success event, mark rows at a later gate j ′ that have a continuation to it, and retain a Borel inner
version of that analytic mark under the full analyzed j ′ -row energy law, inside the current end-
state support. For each of their times, restrict the i-inputs to a Borel outer version of the listed
predecessor set. It includes all predecessors under the unrestricted truncated i-to-j ′ geometric
correspondence, not just predecessors on successful completions, and has the same full analyzed
input energy. A path from a complementary input to a marked row would concatenate with its
completing path, and so would contradict the definition of that predecessor list. Correspondence
locality therefore makes complementary transfer negligible. Ordinary contraction, summed over
the listed next time cells, costs their multiplicity times the raw mass at i. Applying (Sp) onward
to the readout prohibits lists of exponent strictly below d(j ′ − i), including the small predictor
multiplicities. Testing popular cells proves the stated time lower surprises. The same proof starts
at p′ = 0.

                                                   67
    For c′ < 1, use Lemma 5.6 with V∗ = β ⋄ , B = b for constant quadratic cuts, or with the raw
slope and B = b1 in a linear branch. The side is the specified static side. Its additional root data
have zero exponent beyond S, ta , [V∗ ]B(a) . At the later time, p′ > c′ (p′ + t) for sufficiently small
t/p′ , so the r-increment lies above the graph and has rate ν. Adding the coefficient bins between
B(i) and the tested depth to the causal time bound loses at most c′ (ν − ν− )t: this is precisely
the mixed coefficient information exposed by the new time bits. The r-increment before those
bits has rate at least ν. Chain rule gives joint rate at least

                                       d − c′ (ν − ν− ) + ν ≥ d,

and also the separately stated time bound. Known shifts of bin origins and virtual raw coefficient
comparisons cost small lists only.

5.5    Separation forced by a positive minimal cost
Proposition 5.11 (Angular separation). In an equality quadratic family with c > 0, the
conditional (θ, r) law for an admissible side avoids arbitrary affine lines with a positive power. At
c = 1 this assertion requires the coarse side with the additional left label slack in Definition 5.8;
it is not asserted for a general exact side. More precisely, for a line chosen measurably per Z, its
δ λ -neighborhood has total probability at most δ ελ in the exponent limits, for some ε > 0, each
tested λ > 0, and sufficiently small h. The constants are uniform on bounded lists of positive λ.
     In a pure linear family, the analogous statement holds for predicting a value of r. In a
clipped classical linear branch with p∗ (a) = b1 (a), two conditionally independent marks satisfy
|ri − rj | ≥ δ λ with probability tending to one for every fixed λ > 0; no uniform positive avoidance
exponent is asserted in this case.

Proof. For the clipped assertion, root additions to y0 , ta have zero cost and the offset in (xi) is
then known. Every strictly finer raw slope bin has positive deterministic score by the definition of
p∗ . After trimming exceptional heavy bins, conditional independent sampling gives the separation.
In a virtual use keep the query below b1 (D).
     We prove the power statements by contradiction. Failure gives a predicted tube of width
s (1−o(1))z , where z = λh > 0, carrying mass so(z) ; all previous errors are taken to zero in z-units
first. A nearly vertical line confines the time coordinate to a power-small interval and is excluded
by Proposition 5.10. Hence, after an arbitrarily small loss, the line is the graph of an affine
function with slope s−o(z) in mark coordinates. In physical units it predicts an affine trajectory
β∗ with
                                  |β1 (T1 ) − β∗ (T1 )| ≤ sb(a)+(1−o(1))z .
Exact extra-prediction sides are restricted to their successful lists, taking their costs and failure
tolerances sufficiently small in z-units.
    We shall extract a segment [i, j]. By Lemma 3.19, take a Borel inner version of the retained
final event, mark j-rows having a completion to it, and then take a Borel inner version of this
analytic mark under the full analyzed j-row energy law, inside the current end-state support.
Every retained marked row still has a completion. Their mass is at least so(z) Ej by (Sp); the
                                                       √
count bound gives a cube-sum lower bound so(z) Ej κj . Group starting rows by the binned
polynomial subtraction and their starting interval. Disjointize the finite possible-bin relations and
retain their Borel inner versions, so each marked end row has a bin witnessed by a completion.
Use the Borel outer predecessor set for that bin, formed from the unrestricted truncated i-to-j


                                                  68
geometric correspondence. It includes all its correspondence predecessors without changing
full analyzed input energy; its Borel inner partner carries the same full analyzed state inside
the original starting-bin partition. Each end label has s−o(z) chosen bins, as checked in the
cases below. Path concatenation and locality then justify restricting synthesis to those starting
sets. Input energy summed over groups is at most s−o(z) Ei , each group’s regularity is at most
s−o(z) κi , and the end counts gain only the same multiplicity. Hölder preserves the cube-sum lower
bound over groups. After rescaling, these grouped experiments realize the preceding extremal
parameters in the closure. Thus a fixed improvement in the minimal cost is impossible.
     Suppose first 0 < c < 1. Set i = a+z/2, j = a+3z/4, and choose 0 < ω < min{(1−c)/2, c/4}.
Subtract the prediction at the i-time start, rounded at depth b(i) + ωz. Starting rows know
it from the side. Moving the prediction to the endpoint time costs at most sb(a)+z/2−o(z) , and
comparing the end label with the final coefficient costs sb(j)−o(z) . Both are finer than the chosen
bin, so the end label knows few bins. The residual end coefficient is at most sb(i)+ωz−o(z) , while
the duration-scaled own slope already has size sb(j)−o(z) . In segment units the curvature cost is
at most c − 4ω + o(1) < c.
     For c > 1, set i = a, j = a + γz, with 1/c < γ < 1, and choose γ < τ < 1. On the part where
sj |K1 − β∗′ | > sb(a)+τ z , the end trajectory has the same slope discrepancy to within a factor two,
since cγ > 1 > τ . Its meeting the predicted trajectory within the tube confines the terminal
time, per end row and predicted line, to length sj+(1−τ −o(1))z . The causal time count excludes
this part by a power. On the remainder, extrapolation to ta gives coefficient and slope errors
bounded by
                                 sb(a)+(τ −γ)z−o(z) ,  sb(a)−a+(τ −γ)z−o(z) .
Subtract the predicted pair at those base depths improved by ωz, with 0 < ω < τ − γ. It is
jointly list-recoverable, and both residual curvature terms at the end have cost strictly below c.
     For c = 1, use i = a, j = a + 2z. The additional left slack makes λj predict the coarse side:
its base width has u(j) > V , its derivative comparison fits l(A, V ), and its normal comparison
fits by the same Taylor and hybrid-drift bounds as in Proposition 5.4. It also predicts the root
curvature bins. Subtract the predicted pair at depths b(a) + ωz, b(a) − a + ωz, where 0 < ω < 1/2.
The residual end coefficient is bounded by sb(a)+ωz−o(z) ; the end-duration times the residual slope
is bounded by sb(j)−o(z) . Hence the end curvature cost is strictly below one. This case does not
require the slope of the prediction to be inferred from the end curvature alone: it is predicted by
the recovered side.
     Finally, in the pure linear case choose i = a, j = a + γz with c1 γ > 1. A value prediction for
r predicts the terminal linear coefficient at depth b1 (a) + z − o(z). Subtract its bin at b1 (a) + ωz,
0 < ω < 1. The end label knows the bin by its finer coefficient comparison. The curvature cost
remains zero and the linear cost becomes at most c1 − ω/γ + o(1), a contradiction.
     For clarity, the recovery margins behind the group count are as follows. When 0 < c < 1,
the prediction-transport and end-label errors have depths b(a) + z/2 and b(j) = b(a) + 3cz/4,
both finer than the subtraction depth b(i) + ωz = b(a) + (c/2 + ω)z by the two restrictions on ω.
When c > 1, both errors in the displayed value–slope pair exceed their subtraction depths by
(τ − γ − ω)z > 0. At c = 1, the recovered coarse side supplies the slope bin to a small list, and
the end coefficient has depth b(j) = b(a) + 2z > b(a) + ωz. In the pure linear case the terminal
coefficient has depth b1 (j) = b1 (a) + c1 γz > b1 (a) + ωz. Thus the pre-existing side and rounding
lists leave only s−o(z) bins per fixed end label in each case.




                                                  69
5.6    Replica budgets and conditional fibers
Draw finitely many observations independently given Z, and store their marks Ξi = (θi , ri ),
including ki when required, to fixed finite depths. Let F = F (Z) and C∗ = C∗ (Z) be the
representative phase and name from Lemma 5.9. The cutoff for each stored mark is chosen
sufficiently large for the finitely many ensuing queries; it is never replaced by an exact mark
inside an information estimate without a new justification.
    For the normalized phase and angle, F≤p′ and X≤p′ denote their coordinatewise dyadic names
at δ-depth p′ ; these names also determine their coarser dyadic bins.

Proposition 5.12 (Replica information budgets). At almost every interior admissible root there
are arbitrarily small tangent gaps with sufficiently accurate preceding experiments such that, for
any fixed finite collection of queries,

                                        Iδ (F≤p′ ; Ξ | C∗ ) = o(1).                                  (vb)

Define a fiber read Qi , at a sufficiently large finite depth relative to still finer stored marks, by
binning
                                                   (
                                                       P − 2ri X, N − P X + ri X 2 ,    quadratic,
        B + θi X,             2
                     Y + θi X ,    D0′ + θi N,
                                                       N − ri X,                        linear.

Then
                      Iδ (X≤p′ ; Qi , Ξ | C∗ ) = o(1),
                                                                                                     (fib)
                             iδ (X≤p′ | C∗ ) = m(a, v)p′ + o(1)       in probability.

At a fixed boundary root with one observation, only the consequence of (ng) for adding that
observation and its fiber read is asserted; neither (vb) nor an interior angular trace is asserted
there.

Proof. The marks and their law before evaluation of the representative phase are independent
of v on the patch. Their total entropy is O(h log(1/s)). For sufficiently large fixed K, the
pair C∗ (v), C∗ (v + Kh) determines F≤p′ up to bounded lists. In a quadratic template the new
derivative bin reads P to depth K. The new residual phase can be converted to the old shear
frame using the known shear difference and finer base bins; every old coordinate is then known
to depth at least K. In a linear template the linear cut is unchanged. Consequently telescoping
the decreasing function Hs (Ξ | C∗ (v)) over a translation of size Kh bounds the integral of
Is (F≤p′ ; Ξ | C∗ ) by O(h2 ) + o(1). Here Is = hIδ , since log(1/δ) = h log(1/s).
    Integrate also in a on compact patches when needed. For fixed dyadic h, take lower limits
of the sum of the finitely many nonnegative errors through the earlier approximations. Fatou
preserves the integral bound. After division by h, the dyadic integrals are summable, so almost
every root admits accurate experiments with all the required normalized errors tending to zero.
Countably many finite cutoffs and tuple sizes are handled by a diagonal. This establishes existence
of the stated tangent laws; it does not assert stationarity for arbitrary sampling schemes that
change with v.
    Consider next observation i and its actual child name C(a + Kh, v), with K larger than all
required query depths, also after multiplication by c′ and, for nonconstant quadratic cuts, by
c′ − 1. It predicts C∗ and reads Ξi , Qi to bounded lists. Here are the details for the last assertion.

                                                    70
The child time θ′ approximates θi arbitrarily finely. Write W ′ = B + θ′ X, V ′ = Y + θ′ X 2 . The
difference between child and root shears, in the common frame, is

                               cobs + LX + RX 2 + b(V ′ − 2XW ′ ),
                                      dobs + LW ′ + RV ′ − b(W ′ )2 .

All coefficients and offsets are determined by the two names. In the quadratic case R, b
approximate ri , ki , and L + 2RX − 2bW ′ approximates P . Thus L − 2bW ′ reads P − 2ri X;
adding back the known bV ′ to the residual velocity reads N − P X + ri X 2 . The base reads
supply W ′ , V ′ , and the residual position supplies D0′ + θ′ N . For a linear branch the same
derivative expression reads ri , while R, b are negligible because b + v > l, or identically zero. It
therefore reads N − ri X. Changing the curvature basis in a clipped child costs sb+v , smaller than
                      ′
every required sl δ M by the fixed branch slack. Nonconstant quadratic cuts read ri with error
            ′
O(δ K + δ c K ) and the slope to depth (c′ − 1)K. Large constant offsets are converted at the exact
child-bin time before comparison. Finally the common phase error is negligible by Lemma 5.9.
    Equation (ng) bounds the information that the child name gives about the fine angle,
conditionally on the root. Thus adding (Qi , Ξi ) costs o(1). To add the other marks, first include
a sufficiently fine phase bin. Given it and Ξi , the fiber read has only bounded ambiguity. The
remaining conditional phase information is bounded by (vb), using chain rule. This proves the
first line of (fib). The second line is the deterministic angular profile and the root comparison
from Lemma 5.9.

Corollary 5.13 (Stationary conditional walks). One may resample the common side and its
phase successively from their joint law conditional on C∗ , Ξ, Qi , optionally retaining an angular
prefix bin. Each leg preserves the joint marked marginal. Fine angular increments of the new
endpoint have negligible normalized information from the preceding history beyond that already
present in C∗ and the retained prefix. For a leg with A = ∆X, its increments satisfy, up to
arbitrarily fine specified errors,

                    ∆B = −θi A,     ∆Y = −θi (2XA + A2 ), ∆D0′ = −θi ∆N,
                    ∆P = 2ri A,     ∆N = P A + ri A2                (quadratic),                (leg)
                                    ∆N = ri A                       (linear).

For a finite walk without a retained prefix, the new angular draws have joint negligible information
relative to independent draws given C∗ , including relative to the starting phase and all marks.

Proof. Conditional resampling preserves the conditioning data and their joint marginal. Given
those data it is independent of the rest of the history. Apply (fib) to this unchanged marginal;
chain rule shows that retaining an angular prefix only decreases the information budget for the
remaining angle bits. Summing the finite number of budgets gives the joint assertion. The first
three identities in (leg) follow from equality of the first three fiber reads. The derivative read
gives ∆P = 2ri A. Substitution in the remaining quadratic read gives ∆N = P A + ri A2 ; the
linear read gives the last identity. Increase the finite cutoff to make all rounding errors smaller
than the queried scales.

   These walk assertions are information estimates, not total-variation assertions. In particular
they do not permit retaining a set of merely subpower probability and reusing the old budget
without a separate argument.

                                                 71
5.7   Random scores and diagonal traces
The walk estimates provide small information budgets, while the projection tests to come use
scores of individual outcomes at several depths. We next justify passing from finite-name scores
to local rates and specify which changes of probability law preserve those budgets.
    Throughout this subsection, ρ ↓ 0 and scores are divided by Lρ = log(1/ρ). Side data D may
take values in an arbitrary standard Borel space. All conditional laws are regular conditional
laws. For a finite name A, write

                             iρ (A | D) = −L−1
                                            ρ log P(A = A(ω) | D).

Every assertion about several names is made first for a fixed finite list of queries. Countable
limits below are limits of these joint score laws; they do not assert a coupling of experiments at
different scales.

Lemma 5.14 (Pathwise score limits). Suppose a finite collection of name arrays Aq , with depths
in a bounded interval, has the following properties. Coarser names are determined from finer
names up to lists of size ρ−o(1) , and, conditionally on Aq , D, the name Aq′ , q ′ ≥ q, has at most
      ′
ρ−K(q −q)−o(1) possible values. The same bounds hold for the joint arrays under consideration,
and their total range sizes are at most a fixed power of ρ−1 . Then, after a subsequence, the scores
at countably many fixed queries have a joint distributional limit with these properties:

  (i) Cumulative scores are nonnegative, nondecreasing, and Lipschitz in each named-variable
      refinement argument, with conditioning held fixed and with the corresponding branching
      constant. They extend from rational queries to real queries.

 (ii) The chain rule holds, and adding conditioning can only decrease a conditional score. In
      particular, for r ≤ r′ ≤ a ≤ b, whenever Fr is a nested conditioning array,

                               i(Ab | Fr′ , D) − i(Aa | Fr′ , D)
                                         ≤ i(Ab | Fr , D) − i(Aa | Fr , D).                    (5.1)

      Here and below i without a scale denotes a limiting random score.

 (iii) Mutual determination with exponent-zero lists identifies the corresponding limiting scores.
       All these statements hold simultaneously almost surely for the countable family of queries.

Proof. If a conditional distribution is supported on a list of size N , then the total probability
of values of conditional probability less than ρϵ /N is at most ρϵ . Apply this observation to
each refinement and each list comparison. It bounds the refinement score between zero and
K(q ′ − q) + ϵ + o(1), except on a set of probability tending to zero. Bounded total range gives the
same control on whole scores; values exceeding the range exponent by ϵ have probability at most
ρϵ , also conditionally on D. Thus every finite vector of scores is tight. A diagonal subsequence
gives a consistent joint limit on the countable query set.
     For exactly nested names the log-probability chain rule is an identity. For list nesting,
insert the coarser name into the finer name. Its additional conditional score tends to zero by
the preceding list bound, so the same identities survive in the limit. Equation (LP) gives the
conditioning inequality at each fixed query. Apply it to the increment name with the coarse
name already conditioned upon to obtain Equation (5.1). A countable union of null sets gives all
inequalities simultaneously. The loss from refining a conditioning name Fr to Fr′ is nonnegative

                                                 72
and at most i(Fr′ | Fr , D), by the chain rule and nonnegativity of conditional scores. It is
therefore at most K(r′ − r) with the appropriate branching constant. Thus the required mixed
score functions are Lipschitz also in their conditioning depths. The Lipschitz estimates extend
the limiting scores continuously from rational depths. Comparisons with neighboring rational
depths also give the claimed limit at any additional fixed real query. The same reasoning applies
when several arrays are refined together.

   The following elementary trace statement is useful because differentiating a two-variable
Lipschitz function on its diagonal is not justified by the usual almost-everywhere differentiability
theorem alone.
Lemma 5.15 (Monotone diagonal trace). Let H(q, r) be continuous for 0 < r ≤ q < T , with
H(r, r) = 0. Assume that H(·, r) is nondecreasing and K-Lipschitz, uniformly in r, and that

                     H(b, r′ ) − H(a, r′ ) ≤ H(b, r) − H(a, r)              (r ≤ r′ ≤ a ≤ b).

There is a measurable function g : (0, T ) → [0, K] such that, for almost every p, uniformly over
p ≤ u ≤ v ≤ p + t,
                           H(v, u) = g(p)(v − u) + op (t)       (t ↓ 0).                     (5.2)
Proof. For rational r, choose the derivative fr (q) = ∂q H(q, r) outside one common null set.
Increment monotonicity gives 0 ≤ fr′ (q) ≤ fr (q) ≤ K for rational r < r′ < q. Put

                                                g(q) = inf fr (q).
                                                          r∈Q
                                                         0<r<q

For any fixed real u, differentiate the increment inequalities comparing H(·, u) with all rational
conditioning depths. For almost every q > u and each rational r < u, they imply

                                           g(q) ≤ ∂q H(q, u) ≤ fr (q).

For the first inequality, compare with a rational depth between u and q. Absolute continuity
therefore gives, for every u < v and rational r < u,
                                 Z v                              Z v
                                          g(q) dq ≤ H(v, u) ≤           fr (q) dq.                          (5.3)
                                      u                            u

Choose p to be a Lebesgue point of g and of every fr defined in a neighborhood of p, and outside
the common derivative null set. These conditions exclude only a null set. For fixed rational
r < p, the lower and upper errors in Equation (5.2) are bounded, uniformly over the indicated
triangle, by
           Z p+t                                 Z p+t                        Z p+t
                   |g(q) − g(p)| dq       and            |g(q) − g(p)| dq +           (fr (q) − g(q)) dq,
            p                                     p                             p

respectively. The first is o(t). The limsup of the second divided by t is at most fr (p) − g(p).
Taking the infimum over rational r < p makes it zero and proves the assertion.

Corollary 5.16 (Prefixes with future conditioning). Let Fq , Uq , Vq satisfy Lemma 5.14, and
suppose Fq , D determines Uq , Vq to exponent-zero lists. For almost every fixed p, almost surely in
the limiting score law, there are rates gU (p), gV (p), gU V (p) such that

           i(Up+t | Fp , D) = gU (p)t + o(t),              i((U, V )p+t | Fp , D) = gU V (p)t + o(t).

                                                          73
Moreover, for every fixed 0 ≤ λ ≤ 1,
                                                                      
                        i(Up+λt | Fp , Vp+t , D) = λ gU V (p) − gV (p) t + o(t).                (5.4)

The error can be taken uniform in λ. Tuples may replace V .

Proof. For A = U, V, (U, V ), apply Lemma 5.15 pathwise to HA (q, r) = i(Aq | Fr , D). Its diagonal
vanishes by the list hypothesis, and its increment inequality is Equation (5.1). Put u = p + λt
and v = p + t. The chain rule first gives

             i(Uu | Fp , Vu , D) = HU V (u, p) − HV (u, p) = λ(gU V (p) − gV (p))t + o(t).

Write i(A; B | C) = i(B | C) − i(B | A, C) for limiting conditional information. These quantities
are nonnegative by Equation (LP). Since Uu is determined by Fu , D, the additional loss from
the future part of V satisfies

                            0 ≤ i(Uu ; Vv | Fp , Vu , D)
                              ≤ i(Fu ; Vv | Fp , Vu , D)
                              = HV (v, p) − HV (u, p) − HV (v, u) = o(t).

The second inequality follows by expanding the information involving (Uu , Fu ) in both orders and
using nonnegativity. The last equality uses nesting and determination of Fp , Vu from Fu , D; its
o(t) bound follows from the uniform triangular trace. This proves Equation (5.4). Finally, Fubini’s
theorem converts the pathwise almost-everywhere statement into an almost-sure statement at
almost every fixed p.

Lemma 5.17 (Changes of probability law). Let Pρ , Qρ be probability laws with KL(Pρ ∥Qρ ) =
o(Lρ ). At samples drawn from Pρ , their scores for any fixed finite collection of names, including
conditional scores with arbitrary common side data, differ by o(1) in probability. If Pρ (E) ≥ c > 0,
then Qρ (E) ≥ ρo(1) .
    Restricting a law to an event of probability at least c > 0 preserves its finite-name scores up to
o(1) in probability. It also preserves any conditional mutual-information bound o(Lρ ) between two
variables given common side data. The latter assertion is not made for events whose probabilities
merely have exponent zero.

Proof. Let R = dPρ /dQρ . The elementary estimate Pρ (log R < −u) ≤ e−u implies EPρ (log R)− ≤
1, and hence
                           EPρ (log R)+ ≤ KL(Pρ ∥Qρ ) + 1 = o(Lρ ).
Markov’s inequality proves log R = o(Lρ ) in probability. Relative entropy decreases under
measurable maps, so this also applies to the likelihood ratios for each joint name and its
conditioning field. Subtracting those two log ratios proves the conditional-score assertion.
Applying the log-sum inequality to E, E c gives
                                                             1
                              KL(Pρ ∥Qρ ) ≥ Pρ (E) log            − log 2,
                                                           Qρ (E)

which proves the event assertion.




                                                    74
    For restriction to E, use KL(Pρ (· | E)∥Pρ ) = log(1/Pρ (E)). To verify the information
assertion, let X, W, D denote the variables in question and set Q = PD PX|D PW |D . With
cρ = P (E), the mixture identity for relative entropy gives

                           cρ KL(PXW D|E ∥Q) ≤ IP (X; W | D) + h(cρ ),

where h(c) = −c log c − (1 − c) log(1 − c) ≤ log 2. This identity remains valid if E also depends on
auxiliary variables: apply the mixture identity to the marginal laws of (X, W, D). The relative
entropy on the left is at least IP (·|E) (X; W | D), by decomposition against the product of the
restricted conditional marginals. Division by cρ ≥ c proves the result.

    These results are used without selecting rare limiting profiles. A strict violation is tested at
a fixed admissible p, on an event of positive probability in its random score law, using finitely
many queries and slightly relaxed tolerances. The joint convergence of those scores transfers
positive probability to sufficiently advanced experiments. Their information errors are then taken
sufficiently small for this fixed event and layer, before the layer shrinks.
Remark 5.18 (Freezing coefficients). Suppose a local projection has smooth coefficients depending
on the phase point, such as the contact differential dY − 2X dB. Fix their values to accuracy
O(ρp0 ) using a name already in the conditioning, where 0 < p0 < p. Inside an Fp cell, freezing
changes an increment by O(ρp+p0 ); Taylor expansion of the original coordinate contributes O(ρ2p ).
Both are smaller than ρp+t when 0 < t < min(p, p0 ). The two descriptions therefore give bounded
lists for one another at this layer, conditionally on the parent and the coefficient descriptions.
The values at the parent representative must be translated exactly: they are known offsets and
are not increment errors. The same argument applies to a mark coefficient frozen to accuracy ρϵ
when t < ϵ. Any cost of adding this coefficient information is a separate information budget; the
geometric comparison does not supply it.
    For a Heisenberg recentering, the error left by changing horizontal cell representatives is
bilinear in unresolved horizontal displacements. If their scale is ρa , this error is O(ρ2a ). Thus a
vertical name at scale ρb is predicted up to bounded lists whenever b ≤ 2a, giving the required
nesting and branching in that wedge. The score calculus applies in its interior; it supplies no
derivative trace across the boundary b = 2a.

5.8   A projection rule with conditional records
The record in the next proposition may contain more information than the direction it determines.
In particular, different records with the same direction may carry different lists of projection
values.

Proposition 5.19 (Conditional projection rule). Let 0 ≤ S ≤ 2, 0 < b ≤ 1, and ∆ ↓ 0. Let Z be
a bounded planar source, W a record determining a direction g(W ), and D common side data.
Assume
                                I(Z; W | D) = o(log(1/∆)).                               (5.5)
Suppose there is an event G of probability bounded below such that its unnormalized conditional
marginals obey, for every needed ball and interval of radius ∆ ≤ r ≤ 1,

                                P(G, Z ∈ B(z, r) | D) ≤ ∆−o(1) rS ,
                               P(G, g(W ) ∈ J(r) | D) ≤ ∆−o(1) rb .                            (5.6)


                                                 75
For each D, W , let I(D, W ) be a list of at most ∆−x−o(1) intervals of length O(∆), and suppose
that on G the projection of Z in direction g(W ) belongs to their union, up to O(∆) error. Then

                                      x ≥ min{S, (S + b)/2, 1}.                                     (5.7)

The assertion is a strict-error statement: a fixed strict deficit is impossible if all preceding exponent
losses and the information ratio are sufficiently small. Consequently a sufficiently fine finite
grid of prefix radii suffices, and errors of size ∆1−o(1) in directions, representatives, or output
resolution are allowed. The same conclusion holds with c∆ = P(G) tending to zero, provided

                   log(1/c∆ ) = o(log(1/∆)),        I(Z; W | D) = o(c∆ log(1/∆)).

Proof. The case S = 0 follows from the nonempty output list on G, so assume S > 0. A finite
choice of rotated projective charts allows restriction to a positive-probability part of G where
projections have the form
                                        πu (z) = z1 + uz2 ,
with bounded u, z. Multiplying a projection by a bounded nonzero factor only changes interval
constants. First restrict to G. Lemma 5.17 preserves the vanishing normalized information
budget. If cD = P(G | D) and P(G) ≥ c, then under this restricted law the parents with cD < c/2
have total probability at most 1/2. On the other parents, normalization of Equation (5.6)
costs at most 2/c. The conditional information is nonnegative and has vanishing average after
normalization by log(1/∆). We can therefore choose a parent with these mass bounds and with
conditional information o(log(1/∆)).
    Fix that parent. Write P for the restricted joint law and µ, ν for its source and record
marginals. Let R be the relation that the projected source is in the record’s output list. Since
P (R) = 1, the log-sum inequality gives

                          η := (µ × ν)(R) ≥ exp − KL(P ∥µ × ν) = ∆o(1) .
                                                                    


Thus product sampling still hits the lists, although a fixed positive product probability is not
asserted.
    Fix a small ϵ > 0. For sufficiently advanced experiments, the marginal bounds have constants
at most ∆−ϵ and η ≥ ∆ϵ . Draw M = ⌈∆−b ⌉ independent full records W1 , . . . , WM from ν. With
probability tending to one faster than any fixed power of ∆, their empirical direction law νM
obeys
                              νM (J(r)) ≤ C∆−2ϵ rb      (∆ ≤ r ≤ 1).                        (5.8)
Indeed, for each dyadic interval of radius r, the count is binomial with mean at most M ∆−ϵ rb ;
the asserted threshold is larger by a factor comparable to ∆−ϵ and is at least a constant multiple
of ∆−2ϵ . The binomial upper-tail bound is P(N ≥ k) ≤ (eM q/k)k fora count with M trials and
success probability q: sum over the k successful indices and use M                 k
                                                                       k ≤ (eM/k) . This bound,
                                         −1
followed by a union bound over O(∆ ) dyadic intervals down to scale ∆, proves the claim.
Every other interval is covered by a bounded number of these intervals at comparable radius.
    The expected average relation density of this sample is η. Since the probability that Equa-
tion (5.8) fails is o(η), there is a sample satisfying that equation and
                     Z
                         θ(z) dµ(z) ≥ η/2,      θ(z) = M −1 #{j : (z, Wj ) ∈ R}.



                                                   76
Let E = {z : θ(z) ≥ η/4}. The bounds 0 ≤ θ ≤ 1 imply µ(E) ≥ η/4. The probability
µE = µ|E /µ(E) has planar ball masses at most C∆−2ϵ rS . For each z ∈ E, put the uniform
probability on its successful sampled records. Its direction masses are at most C∆−3ϵ rb , by
Equation (5.8) and θ(z) ≥ η/4.
   Dualize: the source point z parametrizes the line

                                   ℓz = {(u, z1 + uz2 ) : u ∈ R}.

The map from z to the line parameters is bi-Lipschitz on the bounded chart, so this family
supports an S-Frostman probability with the preceding small power loss. On each ℓz , the
successful records give a b-Frostman probability: the direction parameter and distance along the
line are uniformly comparable. Yet all these points lie within O(∆) of a union of at most

                                     CM ∆−x−ϵ ≤ C∆−b−x−ϵ

grid cells, using the output lists separately for every sampled record. Repeated records or repeated
directions cause no problem. Apply Equation (RW) with its point exponent b and line-family
exponent S. For any fixed ω > 0, choosing ϵ small enough gives the lower covering bound

                                      ∆−b−min{1,S,(S+b)/2}+ω .

Comparison with the upper count excludes every strict deficit in Equation (5.7).
    For completeness, suppose mass bounds are supplied only at radii ∆σj on a grid with successive
gaps at most γ, including the two endpoints. Enclose a ball of arbitrary radius by a bounded
number of cells at the next coarser tested radius. The additional loss is at most ∆−2γ , since
S ≤ 2 and b ≤ 1. Choose γ before the other small losses to fit the strict deficit. Finally, errors of
size ∆1−τ are handled by rerunning the proof at that effective final resolution. The output count
exponent becomes x/(1 − τ ), and the small power constants are rescaled by the same factor; the
ball powers S, b are unchanged. The conclusion converges to the displayed one as τ ↓ 0.
    For the last assertion, the restriction estimate in Lemma 5.17 has upper bound I(Z; W |
D)/c∆ + h(c∆ )/c∆ . This is o(log(1/∆)) under the stated assumptions, since h(c)/c ≤ log(1/c) + 1.
Selecting parents with cD ≥ c∆ /2 loses at most half the restricted probability and adds only an
exponent-zero normalization factor to the mass bounds. The rest of the proof is unchanged.

Remark 5.20 (Obtaining the hypotheses from scores). If at a sample the source prefix score at
radius ∆σ is at least Sσ − ϵ, retain only samples with this property. Every occupied source
cell in the retained law then has original conditional mass at most ∆Sσ−ϵ ; restriction can only
reduce its unnormalized mass. Intersecting finitely many such conditions gives the source bound
in Equation (5.6). The same argument applies to direction cells and to unnormalized direction
bounds first proved under finer side data and then integrated to D.
    Conversely, a projection score at most x + ϵ, conditionally on the full record and D, places
the projection in a list of at most ∆−x−ϵ popular cells: each listed cell has probability at least
∆x+ϵ . Thus projection-score inequalities give precisely the lists required by Proposition 5.19.
Bin the proposed rates on a positive-probability strict violation before making these finite tests.
Corollary 5.16 supplies the needed linear prefix rates also when the source is conditioned on a
future reading of another array. No bound on the total number of source cells is assumed.




                                                 77
5.9   Radial spreading and pin products
Projection tests whose directions come from two marks require information about the lines joining
those marks. The estimates below turn the time marginal bounds into a dichotomy for tube
masses, and provide a product-coordinate estimate for the low-exponent alternative. We begin
with a finite-scale radial argument. It is useful to keep the measure unnormalized: no assertion
of conditional independence will be made after restricting to a relation of pairs. All measures in
this subsection are supported in a fixed bounded subset of R2 . Constants may depend on that
subset. We write l(x, y) for the unoriented line through distinct points x, y, and NR (ℓ) for its
R-neighborhood.

Lemma 5.21 (Finite radial exclusion). Assume the discretized Furstenberg estimate (RW). For
0 < A < d ≤ 1 there are η0 > 0 and ∆0 > 0 with the following property. There do not exist
0 < ∆ < ∆0 , a Borel measure µ of mass at most one, and a symmetric measurable relation E of
distinct pairs such that

                µ(B(x, R)) ≤ ∆−η0 Rd                    (∆ ≤ R ≤ 1),                               (5.9)
                                 η0
                (µ × µ)(E) ≥ ∆ ,                                                                  (5.10)
                                 A+η0
           µ(N∆ (l(x, y))) ≥ ∆                          ((x, y) ∈ E),
           µ(NR (l(x, y))) ≤ ∆−η0 RA                    ((x, y) ∈ E, ∆ ≤ R ≤ 1).            (rad-assume)

Replacing fixed constants in the radii or bounds by other fixed constants does not change the
conclusion, after reducing η0 and ∆0 .

Proof. Put
                 F (A, d) = A + min{1, d, (A + d)/2},              g = F (A, d) − 2A > 0.
Choose κ, ϵ0 > 0 so small that κ + ϵ0 < g/4 and κ < (1 + A/d)/2. We choose a separation
parameter λ > 0 in the clustered case below. We prove the assertion with η0 = η, where η is
chosen last, sufficiently small relative to these parameters. The exact requirements on η are
collected at the end of the proof.
    Cover the bounded space of lines meeting the support by finitely many slope–intercept charts
and partition each chart into ∆-squares. Assign each witness line to one such square c, and let
Tc be a fixed C∆-tube containing the ∆-neighborhoods of every line assigned to c. Define
                                      ZZ
                             Jc =                   1{|u−v|≥∆κ } dµ(u) dµ(v).
                                           Tc ×Tc

A pair at separation r ≥ ∆κ belongs to at most C(1 + r−1 ) ≤ C∆−κ such pairs of footprints.
Indeed its allowable line directions occupy intervals of total length O(∆/r), while the intercept
has only O(1) choices at width ∆ for each direction cell. Consequently

                                                     Jc ≤ C∆−κ .
                                              X

                                                c

Call a cell spread if Jc ≥ ∆2A+ϵ0 . There are at most

                                                C∆−2A−ϵ0 −κ                                       (5.11)

spread cells.

                                                        78
    For spread cells, this upper count will contradict the Furstenberg lower count ∆−F (A,d)+g/4 .
In a nonspread cell, the small mass of separated pairs instead forces most of the tube’s mass into
one ball of radius ∆κ . We treat these two cases in that order.
    Suppose first that spread cells account for at least half of the mass in (5.10). Pass to one
line chart, losing a fixed factor, and discard pins whose partner mass in this relation is less than
∆2η . The retained pins have mass at least ∆2η . Their normalized distribution has ball bounds
∆−3η Rd , with a further fixed constant harmless. For each retained pin x, push its restricted
partner measure to the line-parameter points l(x, y) and normalize. This probability has ball
bounds
                                     C∆−3η RA ,      ∆ ≤ R ≤ 1.
To check this, choose one witness line in a parameter ball which meets the support of the
pushforward. Every partner belonging to that ball lies in a CR-neighborhood of this witness line,
so (rad-assume) applies. Values CR > 1 are covered by the mass bound and a fixed constant.
    Under planar duality the parameter points for a fixed pin lie on its dual line. In the chosen
bounded chart the map from pins to dual-line parameters is bi-Lipschitz. Thus (RW) applies
with point exponent A and line exponent d, giving at least ∆−F (A,d)+g/4 parameter cells. This
contradicts (5.11), since ϵ0 + κ < g/4 and ∆ is sufficiently small.
    It remains to treat witness pairs assigned to nonspread cells. For any such occupied cell put
mc = µ(Tc ); then mc ≥ ∆A+η . Averaging over z ∈ Tc gives a point zc ∈ Tc such that

                             µ(Tc \ B(zc , ∆κ )) ≤ Jc /mc ≤ ∆A+ϵ0 −η .

Hence the cluster
                                       Kc = Tc ∩ B(zc , ∆κ )
has mass at least 12 ∆A+η , and therefore at least ∆A+2η when ∆ is sufficiently small.
   Choose
                               0 < λ < κ/10,       10λ < κ(d − A).
Discard witness pairs at distance less than ∆λ . Their total mass is at most ∆dλ−η , which is
negligible compared with ∆η . Since λ < κ, at least one endpoint of every remaining pair has
distance at least 14 ∆λ from its cluster. Symmetry permits orienting a relation of mass at least a
fixed multiple of ∆η so that its first endpoint x has this property. Retain pins whose partner
mass is at least ∆2η ; the set of retained pins again has mass at least ∆2η .
    For each retained pin take a maximal separated collection of its witness directions, with
angular separation
                                            H = ∆1−2λ .
A ball of directions of radius CH accounts for partner mass at most C∆−η H A ≤ ∆A(1−2λ)−2η ,
by the tube upper bound centered at one witnessing line. It follows that the collection has at
least
                                        ∆−A+2Aλ+4η                                      (5.12)
members, with harmless rounding of this lower bound. Assign to each member the cluster of its
witness cell. These clusters are disjoint: every point of such a cluster has distance ≳ ∆λ from
x, so its direction from x differs from the witness direction by O(∆1−λ ), which is much smaller
than H.




                                                79
   Let E ′ consist of the new pairs (x, y) with y in one of these chosen clusters. The choices can
be made measurably by a fixed finite grid and a deterministic greedy ordering. Using (5.12) and
the cluster mass gives
                                 (µ × µ)(E ′ ) ≥ ∆2Aλ+8η ≥ ∆3λ .
Retain with each new pair its original witness line ℓ0 and cluster K. We have

                      K ⊂ B(y, C∆κ ) ∩ NC∆ (l(x, y)),      µ(K) ≥ ∆A+2η .                   (5.13)

Here x ∈ ℓ0 , y ∈ NC∆ (ℓ0 ), and |x − y| ≳ ∆λ . The angle of the two lines is O(∆1−λ ). On the
cluster, whose diameter is O(∆κ ), rotation about y costs at most O(∆1+κ−λ ) = O(∆). This
proves (5.13) without thickening its final tube by a power of ∆.
    Set q = (1 + A/d)/2. By (5.9),

                            µ(B(y, ∆q )) ≤ ∆(d+A)/2−η = o(∆A+2η ).

Pigeonholing the dyadic annuli between radii ∆q and C∆κ therefore assigns to every pair of E ′
a radius D in this range for which its cluster contributes at least ∆A+3η mass in the annulus
D ≤ |z − y| ≤ 2D. There are O(log(1/∆)) choices of D. For one common D, the corresponding
relation has mass at least ∆4λ . Choose y so that its set of pins has mass at least ∆5λ , and put

                                           R = ∆/D.

This pin submeasure has angular ball bounds

                        µ{x : (x, y) ∈ E ′ , ∠(l(x, y), ℓ) ≤ CR} ≤ ∆−2η RA                  (5.14)

whenever the angular ball meets the retained pin set; increasing the exponent loss by η absorbs
any additional fixed constant. In fact choose one pin in that ball and use its original witness
line ℓ0 . The new line differs from ℓ0 by O(∆1−λ ), whereas R ≳ ∆1−κ and λ < κ. Every pin in
the angular ball is consequently in NC ′ R (ℓ0 ). The original upper bound in (rad-assume), not an
upper bound asserted for a newly conditioned measure, proves (5.14).
    A maximal collection of pin directions separated by a sufficiently large constant times R has
at least ∆5λ+3η R−A members. Their C∆-tubes in the annulus of radius D about y are disjoint.
Each contains mass at least ∆A+3η from its assigned cluster. Consequently

                          µ(B(y, 2D)) ≥ ∆5λ+6η R−A ∆A = ∆5λ+6η DA .

The ball upper bound, with its fixed-radius constant absorbed, is at most ∆−2η Dd . These
inequalities imply
                              ∆5λ+8η ≤ Dd−A ≤ C∆κ(d−A) ,
contrary to the choice of λ, η for small ∆.
   To meet all the losses used above, after choosing κ, ϵ0 , λ in that order choose η > 0 with

                   100η < min{λ, dλ, ϵ0 , d − A},     10λ + 100η < κ(d − A),

and smaller than the loss allowed by (RW) with conclusion loss g/4. These are finitely many
compatible requirements. Finally choose ∆0 small enough to absorb the fixed constants and
logarithmic factors in both cases.


                                               80
Proposition 5.22 (Radial dichotomy). Let ξi = (θi , ri ) and ξj = (θj , rj ) be conditionally
independent, identically distributed planar marks given Z. Suppose their bounded-depth records
obey the preceding score calculus, and their time marginals have prefix mass exponent at least d > 0
given Z, with d ≤ 1. More precisely, at every prescribed finite collection of depths, discarding
a set of vanishing average probability makes the unnormalized conditional time-interval bounds
hold with exponent d and arbitrarily small losses. Assume (RW).
    Trim at the required depths to measures µZ dominated by the conditional mark laws. Pass to
a joint subsequential limit of the needed scores and of

                       e(t) = − log1/ρ µZ (Nρt (l(ξi , ξj ))),   t > 0 rational.

Then, almost surely,
                                                  e(t)
                                  e∗ := lim inf        ∈ {0} ∪ [d, ∞].                        (rad)
                                            t↓0    t
If, in addition, the conditional tube supremum satisfies a uniform affine-line avoidance estimate
of some positive exponent in these depth units, then e∗ ≥ d almost surely.

Proof. Discard at each tested depth the time bins which violate the asserted mass bound. Their
average mass tends to zero; finitely many such trimmings have the same property. Each time
interval is covered by a bounded number of comparable bins, so the trimmed time marginal, and
hence every planar ball, has the required unnormalized bound. Increasing finite grids can be
chosen diagonally. Only grids fixed before the ρ-limit are used.
    The displayed tube masses give legitimate limiting random variables. Indeed an endpoint ball
of a sufficiently smaller constant radius lies in the joining tube. Among O(ρ−2t ) planar cells, the
total trimmed mass of those having mass less than ρ2t+α is O(ρα ). The probability that either
endpoint was trimmed away tends to zero. Thus the exponents can be clipped on exceptional
sets and jointly subsequenced at countably many rational queries. Nearby depth queries handle
constant changes of width.
    If 0 < e∗ < d on a set of positive probability, choose an essential value A ∈ (0, d). Given
the tolerance in Lemma 5.21, restrict to |e∗ − A| < ϵ, with ϵ sufficiently small. At each such
sample the lower inequalities e(u) ≥ (A − 2ϵ)u hold for every sufficiently small rational u, while
e(t) ≤ (A + 2ϵ)t holds at arbitrarily small rational t. A countable union therefore supplies a fixed
small t and a positive-probability event on which these inequalities hold at t and at every point
of a prescribed finite grid σt, 0 < σ ≤ 1.
    Choose that grid with mesh small relative to the tolerance of Lemma 5.21, including a first
point so small that the trivial mass bound fills the gap to radius one. Monotonicity fills all
intervening radii. Transfer the strict inequalities to the approximating laws and put ∆ = ρt .
The time bounds give (5.9); the tube bounds give (rad-assume) with any prescribed sufficiently
small loss. The event has fixed positive probability at this stage. Since both marks were sampled
independently given Z, its surviving pair mass is the average of the corresponding µZ × µZ
relation masses. One can consequently choose Z with pair mass bounded below by a fixed positive
constant, in particular by ∆η0 for small ∆. The event and its transpose may be combined to
make the relation symmetric. This contradicts Lemma 5.21.
    A uniform positive tube-avoidance exponent gives e∗ > 0. The dichotomy then gives the last
assertion.

   The radial dichotomy bounds tubes under the original conditional mark law. In the interior
projection tests one also fixes the parent cell of a moving mark and auxiliary angular data. The

                                                    81
next lemma carries the tube bounds to directions from that parent, keeping the source law and
the full pin record explicit.

Lemma 5.23 (Parent-centered directions and comparison laws). Let P be a law of a side variable
Z, finitely many full records V , and a finite auxiliary name A. Suppose that the record marginal
is conditionally iid given Z = z, with law νz . Each record has a bounded planar projection πz (Vi );
write µz = (πz )# νz for its law. The full record may retain additional entries, including a stored
curvature slope. Let S be a function of Z, and set

                              R(dZ, dV , dA) = P (dZ, dV )P (dA | S).

Assume KL(P ∥R) = o(log(1/δ)). Choose distinct records Vx , Vy , and write x = πz (Vx ), y =
πz (Vy ). Let K be the depth-b parent cell of y, with representative cK , where b > 0. For parents
of positive mass put µKz = µz |K /µz (K). The common data are

                      D = (Z, K, A, the held full records other than Vx , Vy ).

Fix t > 0 sufficiently small compared with b, and a finite grid of radii r = δ σt , 0 < σ ≤ 1. Let Tz
be a measurable trimming set in the full record space and put µTz = (πz )# (νz |Tz ). In particular
the trimming may depend only on the planar projection. Let Wy = ψD (πz (Vy )) be a finite Borel
source name in the tested prefix family. Under R, its conditional law given D is the image of
µKz under ψD ; in applications it is a bin of the rescaled child increment of the moving mark in
K. On an event G, suppose that Vx ∈ Tz , that |x − y| ≥ δ χ with b − χ > t, and that the original
witness lines satisfy the tested bounds

                                  µTz (NCr (l(x, y))) ≤ Cδ −εt rd−ε .

Here the fixed enlargement constants needed below are included among the finite tests. Then, with
gK (x) the direction from cK to x,

                               R(G, gK (x) ∈ J(r) | D) ≤ Cδ −εt rd−ε                            (5.15)

for every tested direction interval J(r). A fixed swap or sign change of its coordinate representation
preserves the assertion.
    After deleting a set of P -probability tending to zero, the corresponding unnormalized con-
ditional bound under P holds with factor Cδ −3εt . Source prefix bounds for Wy supplied by the
original conditional law µz (· | K), with their own source trimming when needed, transfer in the
same way. Moreover,
                             IP (Wy ; Vx | D) ≤ KL(P ∥R) = o(log(1/δ t )).
All assertions fix b, t, the finite grid, and the tested positive-probability event before sending the
information error to zero. No lower bound for µz (K) is required.

Proof. Since S is known from Z, conditioning on A does not change the full record kernel under
R. Conditional iid sampling also allows the other full records to be held. On parents of positive
probability, the exact conditional pair law is consequently

                                                                         νz |πz−1 (K)
                    R(dVx , dVy | D) = νz (dVx )νzK (dVy ),      νzK =                  .
                                                                          µz (K)


                                                  82
Its moving planar marginal is µK               K
                                  z = (πz )# νz = µz |K /µz (K). This factorization holds before
selecting G. Take the jointly Borel inner version of G from Lemma 3.19 under the sum of the P -
and R-laws on the full record tuple. It preserves their masses and the pointwise witness bounds;
all conditional assertions here are for almost every common datum. Its direction marginal on G
is
                        Z                                    Z

where WD,J is the set of planar projections of surviving pin records in that direction interval
having some full-record witness Vy with projection in K. The inner probability is at most one.
In particular the bound does not contain a factor µz (K)−1 .
     If WD,J is nonempty, choose one full-record witness and denote its planar projections by
(x0 , y0 ). Since y0 is within O(δ b ) of cK and the pair is separated, the directions of l(x0 , y0 ) and
l(x0 , cK ) differ by O(δ b−χ ) = o(r). Every participating pin lies in a fixed bounded region and
makes angle O(r) with that witness direction about cK . It is therefore in NCr (l(x0 , y0 )). The
original trimmed tube bound proves Equation (5.15). Notice that the source law is still µK           z ; it
has not been replaced by the conditional law of the trimmed measure.
     For the change of law use the full finite tuple carrying the event, the common data, and all
tested source and pin names. Its relative entropy is at most the stated budget by data processing.
Write f = dP/dR on this tuple and fD = dPD /dRD . The likelihood estimates in Lemma 5.17
show, for fixed ε, t > 0, that outside a set of vanishing P -probability,
                                          f ≤ δ −εt ,        fD ≥ δ εt .
The conditional likelihood on the retained event is at most δ −2εt . Integrating it over either the
source or the pin proves the claimed unnormalized marginal bounds under P . Intersecting further
with a popular-output event only decreases these masses.
    Finally, decomposition of relative entropy conditional on D, followed by its decomposition
against the product kernel νz × νzK , bounds IP (Vy ; Vx | D) by KL(P ∥R). The name Wy is a
function of Vy , D, so data processing gives the asserted bound on IP (Wy ; Vx | D), also with the
full pin record retained in the projection lists. The scale conversion is valid because t is fixed.
If a strict violation is retained on an event of probability c(t) > 0, take the earlier information
error negligible compared with c(t)t log(1/δ) before invoking the positive-event restriction rule.
This uses no uniform assertion as t ↓ 0.

     For the linear-fiber estimates, the projection direction is parametrized by the scalar (θj −
θi )(rj − ri ). We next control this scalar on witness sets whose joining tubes have unusually large
mass, then combine that estimate with the high radial alternative.
Lemma 5.24 (A low-exponent pin estimate). Fix 0 < d ≤ 1. There is a constant Cd with the
following property, for every fixed ϵ > 0 and all sufficiently small ∆ > 0 depending on ϵ. Let µ
have mass at most one and time marginal
                              µ{(θ, r) : θ ∈ I} ≤ ∆−ϵ |I|d          (∆ ≤ |I| ≤ 1).
Fix a pin x = (θx , rx ) in the same fixed bounded set, and let Wx be any subrelation of points
y = (θy , ry ) for which
                         |θy − θx |, |ry − rx | ≥ ∆ϵ ,           µ(N∆ (l(x, y))) ≥ ∆ϵ .

                                                        83
For fx (y) = (θy − θx )(ry − rx ), every interval I of length ∆σ , 0 ≤ σ ≤ 1, satisfies

                               µ{y ∈ Wx : fx (y) ∈ I} ≤ Cd ∆dσ−Cd ϵ .                           (5.16)

Finite grids of prefix hypotheses suffice, with their mesh added to the exponent loss.
Proof. It suffices to treat 3ϵ/d < 1: by increasing Cd , the conclusion is immediate from the
total mass bound for larger ϵ. The time bound also bounds planar balls. Put a = ∆3ϵ/d . A
ball of radius a about x has mass at most C∆2ϵ , so at least half the mass of each witness tube
is outside that ball, for small ∆. Choose a maximal family of witness directions separated by
C∆/a. Outside B(x, a) their ∆-tubes are disjoint when C is sufficiently large. The number of
chosen directions is at most C∆−ϵ . Maximality places all witness points within O(∆/a) of one
of the representative lines.
    Each representative is a witness, so its slope m satisfies c∆ϵ ≤ |m| ≤ C∆−ϵ . On its enlarged
tube,
                                                                 ′
                                fx (y) = m(θy − θx )2 + O(∆1−Cd ϵ ).
For the points under consideration, |θy − θx | ≥ ∆ϵ . On each of the two possible signs of this
difference, the inverse of the quadratic has derivative at most C∆−2ϵ . Thus fx (y) ∈ I restricts
                                                    ′′
θy to at most two intervals of total length C∆σ−Cd ϵ . Apply the time-marginal bound and sum
over at most C∆−ϵ representative lines. This gives (5.16); intervals longer than one are handled
by the total mass bound. Rounding the queried lengths to a finite grid merely adds its mesh to
the loss.

Proposition 5.25 (Pin-product alternatives). In addition to the hypotheses of Proposition 5.22,
assume the following. The score calculus and the preceding conditional projection rule apply to
all fixed finite queries used below. At almost every parent depth p, the joint mark increment,
conditional on Z and its own parent ξj,p , has prefix mass exponent at least d on every fixed finite
grid of sufficiently small layers p → p + t. Finally, for every χ > 0,

                              P |θi − θj | ≥ ρχ , |ri − rj | ≥ ρχ −→ 1.
                               


Put f = (θj − θi )(rj − ri ) and G+ = {e∗ ≥ d}, G0 = {e∗ = 0}. Then the limiting cumulative
scores satisfy
                                    i(fp | Z, ξi ) ≥ dp on G+                        (5.17)
at the tested depths. Here ξi denotes a record stored to a sufficiently large fixed depth, chosen
before the ρ-limit.
    On G0 the following local substitute holds. Any strict violation of a differentiable layer rate on
a set of positive probability can be tested at a fixed small layer t on a positive-probability subset
where e(t) < ϵt. With ∆ = ρt , the unnormalized witness sublaw of f , given Z, ξi , then has prefix
interval bounds of exponent d with loss Od (ϵ), as in (5.16). The assertion is for a fixed selected
layer before earlier approximation and information errors are sent to zero.
Proof. We first record precisely how a high radial bound produces a direction bound. On any
finite grid of sufficiently small depths u = σt, a high witness satisfies

                                    µZ (Nρu (l(ξi , ξj ))) ≤ ρ(d−ϵ)u ,

with a further arbitrarily small absolute loss when transferring to the approximating laws. For
fixed Z, ξi , an angular bin which meets the witness set has all its witnessing partners inside a

                                                   84
constant enlargement of one witness tube. This gives the same bound for the unnormalized
partner-direction sublaw. One may instead fix Z and a j-parent bin at depth p ≫ t. Use its
representative as the center. After discarding pairs closer than a sufficiently small fixed power,
the change of center is negligible at every queried angular width. An angular bin with a witness
again places all its participating pins inside an enlargement of that witness tube. Thus the
pin-direction sublaw, unnormalized and with this parent held, has the same exponent d. These
are bounds on a restricted measure, not claims that the two marks remain independent after the
witness restriction.
    The cumulative score of f given Z, ξi is Lipschitz, starts at zero, and is differentiable at almost
every depth. If (5.17) fails on a set of positive probability, Fubini’s theorem and the fundamental
theorem of calculus provide a fixed differentiability depth p > 0 and a positive-probability high
set where the incremental rate is strictly less than d. Choose t ≪ p small enough to test this
strict deficit, together with a finite prefix grid on which the preceding high tube bounds hold.
    Adding ξj,p to the conditioning can only lower the tested limiting incremental score, by the
score calculus. The coarse product is known to bounded lists from this parent and the pin. After
translating and dilating the j-parent, the product increment is, up to O(ρp ) in dilated units, the
linear form with coefficient vector

                                        (rj,ctr − ri , θj,ctr − θi ).

Its norm is bounded below by an arbitrarily small power with probability tending to one. Since
t ≪ p, both its quadratic remainder and the finite-record errors fit the tested resolution, with
arbitrarily small exponent loss. The source increment has prefix exponent at least d by hypothesis.
The gradient directions, given Z, ξj,p , have unnormalized exponent at least d on the high relation:
they are the joining directions just estimated, with the two coordinates interchanged. Before
restriction, the pin is independent of the j-increment given this parent, since the two marks
were independent given Z. The conditional projection rule applies and gives projected rate at
least min{d, (d + d)/2, 1} = d, contradicting the strict deficit. Integrating the almost-everywhere
rate bound proves (5.17). Popular-cell trimming converts it to the corresponding unnormalized
interval-mass bounds whenever needed.
     On G0 , the definition of a pointwise liminf supplies arbitrarily small rational t with e(t) < ϵt at
each sample. A strict violation of a differentiable limiting rate persists at every sufficiently small
layer at that sample. A countable union therefore selects one fixed such t on a positive-probability
violating set. Impose the two scalar separations at threshold ρϵt ; their exceptional probability
tends to zero in the earlier limits. The trimmed time marginal has the required bounds at the
finite queried depths. At ∆ = ρt , the condition on e(t) gives the tube lower bound of Lemma 5.24,
with an arbitrarily small additional loss. That lemma proves the asserted interval bounds at all
the necessary prefixes.
     Finally, these conditional bounds can be averaged over Z and independently sampled auxiliary
records when the parent conditioning keeps the other pin fixed and permits that mixture. A
different conditioning requires its own justification. In particular the selected low-set event may
have a small positive probability depending on t. One first fixes this t and its finite queries, and
only then makes the earlier information errors negligible relative to that probability. Nothing
here permits transferring an information budget through a restriction whose probability is merely
a power of the fine scale.




                                                    85
6     Interior phase inequalities
This section derives the local inequalities used in the cylinder argument. Its input is the tangent
construction of the preceding section, including the information estimates (vb) and (fib), the
angular profile (ang), and the projection calculus. We keep these inputs explicit: an artificial
quadratic branch does not acquire an angular spreading hypothesis merely by having the same
phase coordinates as an equality branch.
   The output is the list of admissible time–width directions in Proposition 6.12. We first
express changes of canonical entropy through local phase-reading rates; the remaining arguments
bound those rates and the quadratic width defect.

6.1     Tangent data and contact readings
Fix a differentiability point (a, v) in an interior patch for which the replica construction is valid.
Write
                        c = c′ ,    m = m(a, v),       k0 = 1 − d,     δ = sh ,
where 0 < d ≤ 1, c > 0, and 0 ≤ m ≤ 1. On a linear branch c = b′1 . The calculations below have
g = 0. All auxiliary cutoffs and all finite lists of depth queries are chosen before the inner scale
limits. Errors in information, divided by log(1/δ), tend to zero before a microlayer length tends
to zero. Statements with an arbitrarily small loss mean that this order is respected.

Definition 6.1 (Admissible interior tangent data). The common root is C∗ , the side is Z, and
the phase F = F (Z) is either

    F = (X, B, P, Y, N, D)   in the quadratic case,        F = (X, B, Y, N, D)    in the linear case.

Here D denotes the normalized coordinate called D0′ in the tangent construction. Conditional on
Z, finitely many observations are independent with their prescribed common distribution. Their
stored marks are Ξi = (θi , ri ), together with ki in the nonconstant quadratic case. Marks are
stored to a sufficiently large finite depth. Set D = (C∗ , Ξ).
    We use the following properties of this construction.

    (i) The information estimates (vb) and (fib) hold for every fixed finite set of phase, angular,
        and mark queries. In particular the angular score at depth p is mp + o(1).

 (ii) The nested phase and projection names satisfy the score calculus: limiting scores have
      nonnegative Lipschitz increments; the chain rule, conditioning inequalities, and comparisons
      by negligible lists hold at actual samples; local mixed-prefix scores are linear at almost
      every layer. The projection rule stated in the preceding section applies with its specified
      finite-prefix Frostman hypotheses.

 (iii) The time mark has dimension at least d. Given a joint mark parent at depth p > 0, the
       joint mark increment on p → p + t has score at least dt, up to strict losses. For c ≥ 1 the
       time increment itself has this lower bound. For a linear branch with c > 1, this remains
       true after conditioning on rp+t , provided p + t < cp. If 0 < c < 1, the mixed mark profile
       supplies a number ν ∈ [0, 1] with

                                iδ (rp+t | Z, θp+t , rp ) = νt + o(t),
                                iδ (θp+t | Z, θp , rp+t ) ≥ (d − cν)+ t − o(t).

                                                   86
      These assertions are made on the finite queries allowed by that profile; t is chosen small
      enough relative to p.

 (iv) Resampling the side and phase conditionally on (C∗ , Ξ, Qi ) preserves the marked marginal.
      On a resampling leg, with A = ∆X, the increments obey (leg) to any fixed tested accuracy.
      The finite-walk likelihood comparison supplied by (fib) remains available after conditioning
      on a common angular prefix when one is used.

In the quadratic case, radial spreading means that the high alternative of (rad) has e∗ ≥ d almost
surely for the replicas. For conclusions involving Gi when c > 1, we also use the stated uniform
avoidance of affine lines. In the linear case, crossing means that both |θi − θj | and |ri − rj | exceed
δ χ with probability tending to one for each fixed χ > 0.

   Subpower separation in this section always means the last type of statement, with each
positive exponent fixed before the inner limits. It permits inversion with an arbitrarily small
exponent loss. It does not give a scale-independent lower bound.
   Let Fp be the isotropic phase name at depth p. On a layer p → p + t, t ≪ p, use the contact
forms
                 H = dY − 2X dB,          J = dN − P dX,      K = dD − P dB                (6.1)
in the quadratic case; only H is used in the linear case. These are linear readings of increments,
not differentials of the corresponding products. For example, J is not d(N − P X).
    For nested names, freeze X, P at a fixed positive depth p0 < p. After translation at the known
parent Fp , changing this freeze changes an increment by O(δ p+p0 ). Genuine quadratic remainders
are O(δ 2p ). Both errors are negligible on a layer with t < p0 and t < p. Mark coefficients may be
frozen in the same way, at a fixed positive depth greater than t, keeping the known translation
at the parent.
    Put Wi = dB + θi dX. The readings needed below are

  xi = K + θi J − ri H,      πi = (Wi , H, xi ), Gi = (Li , J − ki H, K − βi H),
  Li = dP − 2βi dX + 2ki dB, βi = ri − θi ki ,   quadratic,
  xi = dD + θi dN − ri Wi ,       πi = (Wi , H, xi ), Gi = (dN − ri dX, dD − ri dB),          linear.
                                                                                                  (6.2)
The quadratic Gi is used on the equality branch with c > 1.
     Write |U |(p) for the limiting random rate of the score of U at depth p + t, conditional on
Fp , D. Set
                 |U | V | = |(U, V )| − |V |, α(p) = |F |(p),   w(p) = α(p) − m.
The conditioning in |U | V | includes the child reading of V . Rates are defined simultaneously
at typical layers for any finite or countable collection of readings. Relative capacities and the
exponential bound for tiny cell probabilities identify their expectations with entropy derivatives.
    A bar denotes a common subsequential expected logarithmic Cesàro mean at zero. Thus one
averages against dp/p over intervals whose logarithmic lengths tend to infinity and whose lower
endpoints tend to zero. Constant dilations have the same mean. We take one subsequence for
the finite list of averages used in an argument. Exchangeability gives equal expected means for
different row indices.

Proposition 6.2 (Differential cost). For admissible interior tangent data,

   In the quadratic case define, for p ≤ z ≤ 2p, the Heisenberg name Fp,z by reading X, B, P to

                         Y − 2[X]p B,       N − [P ]p X,     D − [P ]p B

Proof. Let O be the actual child name at (a + λh, v). Given C∗ , its limiting entropy divided by

                                        Iδ (Fp+t ; O | C∗ , Fp )                              (6.4)

    We will lower-bound the phase information by integrating the expected values of the following

                                   phase-depth interval local rate
