The middle interval is absent when c = 1. The first rate comes from recovering the phase after
paying for its angle; the other rates come from readings recoverable from the output.
    For p + t < min(1, c)λ, the output together with C∗ and Xp+t reads Fp+t up to negligible
lists. Its positions give the two base coordinates at the child time; undoing its displayed shear
gives the common-frame coordinates and, in the quadratic case, the residual derivative. The
extra angular increment has conditional cost at most mt by (ang). Consequently Equation (6.4)
pays the expected w-rate on this interval.
    For the remaining intervals, we must identify the projected readings and show that their
coefficient information is paid for. Let

                   θ′ = (ta+λh − ta )/sa ,      W ′ = B + θ′ X,           V ′ = Y + θ′ X 2 .

The child-time error is O(δ λ ). The converted base positions read W ′ , V ′ to depth λ. In common
parent units, the child shear difference has known constant offsets and coefficients L, R, b at time
θ′ . Its residual velocity and position are displayed to depths cλ and (1 + c)λ. The derivative

                                             L + 2RX − 2bW ′

                                                      88
approximates P in the quadratic case, or ri in the linear case, to depth cλ.
   In a linear branch the curvature coefficients vanish, or are negligible at every tested fixed
power by the branch slack. For constant quadratic cuts, R = ri + O(δ cλ ) and b = 0. For the
quadratic equality branch with c > 1,
                                  |b − ki | + |R − θ′ b − βi | ≲ δ (c−1)λ .                      (6.5)
This is the own-time coefficient comparison, extrapolated back to normalized time zero after
subtraction of the root trajectory. All its nonconstant normalized coefficients are bounded;
constant offsets are retained exactly in the translations.
    In the quadratic case the own-time algebra can be checked without an implicit change of
origin. Put
                           a = R − θ′ b,     ϵ0 = P − L − 2aX + 2bB.
The derivative comparison gives |ϵ0 | ≲ δ cλ . With actual displayed coefficients held fixed, the
residual velocity has gradient
                                       J − bH + ϵ0 dX,
and the residual position has gradient
                                  K + θ′ J − RH + ϵ0 (dB + θ′ dX).
Subtracting θ′ times the former from the latter gives K − aH + ϵ0 dB. These identities are
evaluated at the actual phase and follow directly by differentiating the shear polynomials; the
known constant offsets have zero variation. At a parent representative the derivative error is
O(δ p + δ cλ ). Multiplication by the parent width gives O(δ 2p ) + O(δ p+cλ ). The second term is
negligible on a microlayer with t < cλ, and fits a binary layer whenever p ≤ cλ. The derivative
display itself has gradient dP − 2a dX + 2b dB. Equation (6.5) therefore gives the three claimed
Gi readings with the stated precision.
    In the linear case the shear is linear, with |ri − L| ≲ δ cλ . The displayed residual velocity and
the backflowed residual position have respective gradients
                                     dN − L dX,           dD − L dB.
They differ from the two components of Gi by (ri − L)dX and (ri − L)dB. Before backflow
the position gradient is dD + θ′ dN − L(dB + θ′ dX), which gives xi with the additional O(δ λ )
time-coefficient error.
    Center each displayed quantity by its value at Fp , evaluated with its actual displayed
coefficients and offsets. Taylor’s formula has error O(δ 2p ). The residual-position gradient
converges, with a positive power error on an interior layer, to xi . Below depth λ, the base-
position gradients additionally give Wi , H. If c > 1, subtract θ′ times the displayed velocity from
the position. In the quadratic case the two resulting gradients give K − βi H and J − ki H, and
the derivative equation gives Li . In the linear case they give the two components of Gi . Thus
the intermediate interval gives πi when c < 1 and Gi when c > 1, while the final interval always
gives xi .
    For completeness, the projected entropy is indeed paid by Equation (6.4), even though its
coefficients may depend on phase. Freeze the needed angular and curvature coefficients into a
name Ω at a depth q with
                        (
                            min(p, λ, cλ),          linear or constant quadratic cuts,
               t<q<
                            min(p, λ, (c − 1)λ),    nonconstant quadratic cuts, c > 1.

                                                     89
Shrink the microlayer first so that such a fixed q exists. On a compact interior interval of depths,
the preceding output comparisons recover Ω from O, C∗ , Fp up to negligible lists. The centered
reading U is also determined, up to such lists, by both (Fp+t , C∗ , Fp , Ω) and (O, C∗ , Fp , Ω). For
finite tested names A, B, Ω, U and arbitrary conditioning data C, the chain rule and the two
reconstruction bounds give

                        Iδ (A; B | C) ≥ Hδ (U | C, Ω) − Hδ (Ω | B, C)
                                               − Hδ (U | A, C, Ω) − Hδ (U | B, C, Ω).

To obtain this inequality, first adjoin Ω to B, at cost at most Hδ (Ω | B, C), retain the conditional
information given Ω, and apply the chain rule to the two reconstructions of U . Use A = Fp+t ,
B = O, and C = (C∗ , Fp ). All three error terms tend to zero at the fixed layer, before division
by t. Conditioning further on the stored marks lowers this entropy. Translation at the parent
and the coefficient-freezing comparison then identify the relevant contact-reading rate.
    Tile compact subintervals of the three depth ranges by smaller layers. Bounded convergence for
the score derivatives and nonnegativity for the information sums permit passage to their integrals.
Truncate near the endpoints, then remove the truncations using the uniform relative capacities.
Finally average in λ logarithmically. Dilation invariance of this mean gives Equation (time-rate).
    For width variation, increasing v by λh in a linear branch reads at most Fλ and one additional
λ-depth scalar in vertical position. This proves the linear upper bound. In a quadratic branch
the same change refines X, B, P by λ and the three recentered coordinates by 2λ. In changing
the linear shear, the new binned coefficient differs from the true normalized P by O(δ λ ). After
translation, its effect on unresolved horizontal widths is O(δ 2λ ). Hence the new width name
and Fλ,2λ determine one another up to bounded lists, given C∗ . Equation (vb) allows marked
conditioning in these finite-depth entropy comparisons. Since F0 has zero normalized entropy,
logarithmic averaging of Equation (6.3) proves Equation (width-rate).

6.3   Angular modes and projection amplification
Lemma 6.3 (Angular and commutator modes). The angular fiber direction is
                        (
                            (1, −θi , 2ri ; 0, 0, 0),     (dX, dB, dP ; H, J, K) quadratic,
                 ℓi =                                                                           (6.6)
                            (1, −θi , ri , −θi ri ; 0),   (dX, dB, dN, dD; H) linear.

Any nondegenerate scalar component in this direction has conditional rate at least m after
adjoining finitely many simultaneous readings that annihilate ℓi . A conditional angular-coordinate
rate is at most m.
    In the quadratic case, if m > 0, the center also has the mode

                         bij = (θi − θj , ri − rj , θi rj − θj ri )     in (H, J, K).           (6.7)

A nondegenerate component of this mode has residual rate at least m after conditioning on center
readings that annihilate it. Both xi and xj annihilate bij .

Proof. Adjoin Qi , Xp to the conditioning. Along this fiber the phase is determined by X to every
fixed tested accuracy. Differentiating the fiber equations gives Equation (6.6). Their quadratic
remainder on an angular parent cell is O(δ 2p ). Thus Fp and the annihilating child readings have
negligible list cost with the enlarged conditioning. A nondegenerate component recovers Xp+t up


                                                           90
to negligible lists. For a subpower lower bound on its derivative, fix the separation exponent
much smaller than the strict loss in the test, apply one-dimensional inversion, and then let that
exponent decrease. Equation (fib) gives the residual rate m. The reverse upper bound for angular
rates follows from (ang) and conditioning.
    For the center mode, walk successively along fibers i, j, with endpoints F0 , F1 , F2 and angular
coordinates X0 , X1 , X2 . Adjoin the starting side, very fine X2 bits, and X1,p . The finite-walk
likelihood comparison and (fib) leave rate m for X1,p+t . The separation |X1 − X2 | ≥ δ χ holds
with probability tending to one for every fixed χ > 0: conditional on the previous history, a new
angular prefix has positive score at each positive depth, so it cannot lie in a bounded list of
prescribed bins with nonvanishing probability.
    Apply (leg) on the two legs and differentiate with X2 fixed. The resulting center derivative
at F2 is
                                        2(X2 − X1 )bij dX1 .
In particular its last component is 2(X2 − X1 )(θi rj − θj ri )dX1 . The same parent-cell Taylor and
inversion argument applies. The endpoint has the original marked marginal, so the result is a
statement about its original conditional rates. Substitution in the formula for xi or xj verifies
the last assertion.

Lemma 6.4 (Projection amplification). Let a planar pair have joint local rate S, possibly after
conditioning on common finer readings. Suppose that a scalar projection of rate x leaves a residual
rate at least M > 0. Suppose also that its direction has Frostman exponent d on the finite prefixes
required by the projection rule, and that its full varying record has negligible information with the
planar source. Then
                                        x ≥ min(1, d + M ).
Proof. The chain rule gives S ≥ x + M . The projection rule gives x ≥ min(1, S, (S + d)/2). If
x < 1, then S > x, so the only remaining possibility is 2x ≥ S + d ≥ x + M + d. This gives
x ≥ M + d.
    For random rates these deductions are applied to a positive-probability set with a strict
deficit. Bin the rates to sufficiently short fixed intervals, use mixed-prefix linearity to obtain
the finite-prefix source bounds, and use popular projection cells to obtain the output lists. The
information error vanishes at this fixed layer. The projection rule excludes the deficit. This
procedure also allows the high-set radial witnesses and the low-set pin-product witnesses of the
preceding section; it never conditions on an unspecified subpower-probability event.

Proposition 6.5 (Basic phase rates). Put l∗ = min(1, d + m). For quadratic data with m > 0
and radial spreading, write Cz = (H, J, K). Then
                |Wi | Cz | ≥ l∗ ,       |Cz | − |xi | ≥ m + l∗ ,    α − |Cz | ≥ 2m + l∗ .      (modes)
When the quadratic Gi is used and line avoidance holds,
                               |Gi | ≥ 2m + |xi |,        2E|Gi | ≥ Eα − 1.                       (Gq)
For linear data with m > 0 and crossing,
        |Wi | H| ≥ l∗ ,      |xi | ≥ l∗ ,     |Gi | ≥ m + l∗ ,     2E|Gi | ≥ Eα − 1.        (modes-lin)
For linear data, without a positivity assumption on m,
                                               w − |πi | ≤ 1.                                    (miss)

                                                     91
Proof. In all uses of Lemma 6.4, the planar source and any common child conditioning depend
only on phase and the held records. Equation (vb), followed by the conditional chain rule, permits
removal of the varying record. Its directional sublaw bounds, initially conditional on Z and the
held records, integrate to the tested conditioning. This verifies the information requirement
without asserting independence after a witness restriction.
    For Wi , use the source (X, B), conditional on the center in the quadratic case and on H in
the linear case. The direction parameter is θi , with exponent d, and ℓi gives residual m.
    For the quadratic center inequality, condition on xi to child depth and use (H, J). It
determines all center coordinates with xi . Vary j and project with kernel the image of bij . The
direction is a nonsingular linear transform of the joining-line direction in mark space. Radial
spreading and Lemma 6.3 give a projected rate at least l∗ and residual m. Their sum gives
|Cz | − |xi | ≥ m + l∗ .
    For the remaining horizontal rate, condition on the whole center and quotient (dX, dB, dP )
by ℓi . One may use the quotient coordinates (Wi , dP − 2ri dX). The missing coordinate has
rate at least m. On the quotient, vary j and project with kernel the image of ℓj . Its direction
is another nonsingular transform of the joining line, and its residual is m. Thus the quotient
rate is at least m + l∗ , proving the third bound in (modes). Time separation suffices for the
nondegenerate coordinate comparisons.
    Next, xi is a combination of the two center components of quadratic Gi . Given xi , the form
J − ki H sees bij . Given the center, Li sees ℓj . Both tests are nondegenerate because

                                       |rj − ri − ki (θj − θi )|

has subpower separation by line avoidance. They contribute two residual copies of m. The pair
Gi , Gj determines all three center coordinates and two independent horizontal combinations.
Indeed the two coefficient pairs (βi , ki ) and (βj , kj ) have subpower separation by the same line
test. One scalar coordinate remains, with rate at most one. Subadditivity and exchangeability
prove the second bound in (Gq).
     For the linear xi bound, use the planar source (dX, xi ) and put f = (θi − θj )(rj − ri ). Both
projections xi and xi − f dX have residual rate exactly m: ℓi and ℓj , respectively, give the lower
bound, and the angular upper bound gives equality. Consequently their projected rates agree.
Vary j in the second projection and use the pin-product direction alternatives with Lemma 6.4.
This proves |xi | ≥ l∗ . For Gi , its image of ℓj is nondegenerate by crossing and has direction θj .
The same argument gives a projected rate l∗ and residual m. Two G readings determine every
coordinate except Y , so the last expected inequality follows.
     Finally (πi , X) misses only one additional scalar coordinate by rank. In this comparison all
inversions have bounded coefficients. Its missing angular rate is at most m, and the other scalar
costs at most one. Thus α ≤ |πi | + m + 1, which is (miss).

Iterating this monotone map from zero converges to min(1, m + d): below that value each of
its three entries exceeds the current value, and its least positive fixed point is min(1, m + d).
Adding the auxiliary data can only lower the original endpoint score, whose parent was already
predicted. Given any strict desired error, choose a finite n making the iterate sufficiently close to
the limit, and only then choose the common replica experiment and cutoffs.

6.4   Orientation motions
The preceding estimates varied the angular coordinate with a mark held fixed. We now vary the
mark in the equations relating two phases with shared fiber data. Holding one phase and the
target angle fixed determines the resulting motion of the target phase. These motions make the
time and orientation innovations available as further phase readings.

                                                 93
Lemma 6.7 (Mark-motion comparison). Assume m > 0. Fix a record j and resample one or
two sides Rs independently from the conditional kernel with the same Qj , D as a target F . Put
As = XF − XRs . Each As , and A1 − A2 when two draws are used, has subpower separation. With
Rs , XF held to sufficiently fine tested precision, variation of the mark ξj = (θj , rj ) has differential

                dX = 0, dB = −A dθj , H = A2 dθj ,                  both cases,
                                   2              2
                dP = 2A drj , J = A drj , K = A (rj dθj − θj drj ), quadratic,                    (trans)
                dN = A drj , dD = −rj A dθj − θj A drj ,            linear.

Here A = As , and the forms are frozen at the target. In particular xj annihilates the full two-
component motion. These motions give conditional lower-score tests with the mark innovation
bounds of Definition 6.1, when the held readings annihilate the tested innovation.

Proof. The target and the resampled sides are exchangeable conditionally on the shared fiber
data. Their angular information bounds give the stated separations. Holding Rs and XF fixes A.
In the quadratic case the leg formulas give

                    BF = BR − θj A,                         YF = YR − θj (2XR A + A2 ),
                    PF = PR + 2rj A,                        NF = NR + PR A + rj A2 ,
                    DF = DR − θj (PR A + rj A2 ).

Differentiating and substituting XF = XR + A and PF = PR + 2rj A into the contact forms
gives Equation (trans). In the linear case use NF = NR + rj A and DF = DR − θj rj A. Direct
substitution proves the assertion about xj .
    The conditioning order is essential. First freeze every coefficient involving the moving mark
at a depth below p and above t. With the full record known, translate at Fp to compare the
original and frozen readings. Equation (vb) then removes the remaining fine record from the
list test. Only after this removal do we adjoin ZRs and the motion data. At a mark parent of
depth p, these data predict Fp and every held annihilating child reading up to negligible lists. An
informative component recovers the scalar innovation by inversion, with arbitrarily small power
loss. Its Taylor error is O(δ 2p ). Holding the other mark scalar to depth p + t, when permitted by
its innovation law, adds only an error of that order of accuracy.
    Auxiliary angular bits do not invalidate removal of the moving record. For example, for two
draws and fixed sufficiently fine names,
                                         ∗      ∗
                                   Iδ ((XR 1
                                             , XR 2
                                                    ); ZF , Ξ | C∗ ) = o(1).                         (6.9)

Indeed let B = (Qj , D) and Ys = XR ∗ . The variables Y , Y are conditionally independent given
                                     s                  1 2
B, and their joint conditional law depends on (ZF , Ξ) only through B. Since B is determined by
these latter data, the conditional chain rule gives

                         Iδ ((Y1 , Y2 ); ZF , Ξ | C∗ ) = Iδ ((Y1 , Y2 ); B | C∗ )
                                                          2
                                                          X
                                                      ≤         Iδ (Ys ; B | C∗ ) = o(1).
                                                          s=1

The inequality subtracts the nonnegative term Iδ (Y1 ; Y2 | C∗ ); the last equality is (fib) for the
stationary marginals. The target bits XF∗ may be included in the finite phase budget in (vb).
At Rs , its own angular bits are known, and the other angular bits satisfy the corresponding

                                                       94
version of Equation (6.9). Finally, (ZRs , Ξ) has the original marked marginal, so the replicas are
conditionally independent there and the stated mark innovation law applies. Conditional chain
rules permit the specified mark parents and child scalar bins. This proves the score comparison
without asserting independence conditional on all the exact motion data simultaneously.

Proposition 6.8 (Orientation rates). For quadratic data with m > 0 and radial spreading,

                                              |xi | ≥ d.                                          (cross-q)

If also c < 1, set a∗ = (d − cν)+ and n∗ = min(1, d + ν). Then

     |xi | ≥ n∗ ,   |H | xi | ≥ a∗ ,    |Cz | − |xi | ≥ a∗ + ν,        |πi | ≥ l∗ + a∗ + |xi |.    (mix-q)

For linear data with crossing, m > 0, c < 1, and ν > 0,

                         |xi | ≥ n∗ ,   |πi | ≥ 2l∗ + n∗ ,         w − |πi | ≥ ν.                  (mix-l)

The many-leg term in this assertion is obtained with an arbitrary strict small loss using finitely
many replicas.

Proof. For the quadratic xi bound, move a record j ̸= i. Its gradient in mark space is

                                        A2 (rj − ri , θi − θj ).

Keep ZR , the moving mark parent ξj,p , the needed target angular bits, and other records except
i, j. Use i as the varying projection record. The input mark increment has dimension at
least d. By the parent-representative version of radial spreading, the gradient directions over i
have unnormalized Frostman exponent d on the high witness relation. Swapping and signing
coordinates does not change that assertion. Pair separation makes the gradient magnitude
nondegenerate up to subpower loss.
     To verify the output and independence hypotheses, start from a proposed low-score list for xi
conditional on Fp , D−j . The removal of record j is allowed by (vb). Given its parent and the
motion data, Fp has negligible lists. Taylor expansion at the phase predicted by the mark parent,
followed by dilation by δ −p , turns this into a list for the stated linear projection. The law of
the angular bits has negligible likelihood cost relative to retaining (ZR , Ξ) and sampling those
bits independently given C∗ . Under this comparison kernel the planar source and the projection
record are independent given the test data. Trim likelihood and direction failures with fixed
small power slack; the marked marginal is unchanged. Thus the source and direction dimensions
are both at least d, and the projection rule gives |xi | ≥ d. Precisely, Lemma 5.23 applies with
side ZR , auxiliary name XF∗ , S = C∗ , moving mark j at parent depth b = p, and pin record i. It
supplies both the unnormalized parent-direction bound and its conditional likelihood transfer,
without dividing the pin bound by the moving parent’s probability.
     For the mixed quadratic bounds, move i itself. Hold ri to child depth. The H component
reads the time innovation, of rate at least a∗ , and xi annihilates it. Next hold θi to child depth.
The J component reads the ri innovation, of rate ν, while both H and xi may be held. The
chain rule gives the two center bounds. If ν > 0, use the planar pair (J, K) conditional on H
and the projection K + θi J. Holding θi to child depth, this projection and H annihilate the ri
innovation, while J reads it. Remove the full i record from the planar source by (vb), then use
the time direction and Lemma 6.4 with residual ν. The resulting projected rate is n∗ . Restoring

                                                  95
the record and H identifies it with xi . When ν = 0 use (cross-q). Finally Wi , even conditional
on the whole center, costs at least l∗ by (modes).
    In the linear case condition on (X, B, H) to child depth. The planar source is (dN, dD)
and the projection is dD + θi dN . With θi held to child depth, the ri innovation has rate ν, is
annihilated by this projection, and is read by dN . Lemma 6.4 gives rate n∗ , also for xi with
this finer conditioning. Add |Wi | H| ≥ l∗ and |H| ≥ l∗ from (H-walk). This proves the first two
claims. Given πi , the angular mode gives rate m for X; after those readings are held, dN still
reads the ri innovation with residual ν. Consequently α ≥ |πi | + m + ν.

Proposition 6.9 (Two time innovations). For linear admissible data with m > 0 and crossing,

                                   |πi | ≥ 2d + l∗ , c < 1, ν = 0,
                                   w − |xi | ≥ 2d, c = 1,                                   (double)
                                   w − |Gi | ≥ 2d, c > 1.

Proof. In the first case move j ̸= i, holding rj to child depth. The time innovation still has rate
at least d. The reading
                                      V = dD + θi dN − rj Wi
annihilates that motion. It is a projection of Gj with direction θi . The image of ℓi in Gj is
nondegenerate by crossing and is annihilated by V . Lemma 6.4 therefore gives |V | ≥ l∗ .
     For c = 1 move i and set V = xi . Do not hold the refined ri ; the joint-parent time innovation
still has rate d, and xi annihilates the full motion. For c > 1, move i, set V = Gi , and hold ri to
child depth. The allowed depth condition p + t < cp gives the time rate d, and Gi annihilates
this remaining motion.
     In all cases use two conditional draws R1 , R2 as in Lemma 6.7. The motion of (Wi , H) has
components (−As , A2s )dθ. After adding the auxiliary angular bits, first test H + A1 Wi with the
second draw. Its coefficient is A2 (A2 − A1 ), with subpower separation, and V is held. Next
hold both V and H + A1 Wi , and test Wi with the first draw. The held combination annihilates
this motion and the coefficient of Wi is −A1 . The chain rule gives |(Wi , H) | V | ≥ 2d. The
conditioning comparison in Lemma 6.7 justifies each test before dropping the auxiliary bits.
     In the first case (Wi , H, V ) and πi are equivalent with the marked data, since their last
components differ by (ri − rj )Wi . In the other two cases the held readings also annihilate ℓi ,
leaving angular rate m. These observations prove all three inequalities.

6.5   Quadratic width and binary time layers
Two estimates remain before we can collect the admissible directions. The first bounds the
entropy cost of refining the horizontal width in a quadratic phase. The second uses binary time
layers to improve the time bound at large quadratic cost.

Proposition 6.10 (Horizontal width). For quadratic admissible tangent data, ē ≥ 2m. If m > 0
and radial spreading holds,
                                       ē ≥ 2m + d.                                      (e-q)

Proof. Fix p < z < 2p and refine the horizontal depth from p to p + t, leaving z fixed. Given
Fp,z , this increment is equivalent to the new horizontal bits: recentering contributes only O(δ 2p ).
For every angular mode, Qi , Xp , D predict Fp,z up to negligible lists. Indeed the contact-center
derivative on the fiber is O(δ p ) on the parent, and its variation is O(δ 2p ).


                                                 96
   Two time-separated angular modes are linearly independent. First test a horizontal scalar
annihilating ℓi and seeing ℓj ; then test a scalar seeing ℓi . These tests remain valid after holding
the scalar annihilating both modes. Lemma 6.3 therefore gives horizontal increment rate at least
2m. Integrating in p from λ to 2λ with z = 2λ fixed yields e(λ) ≥ 2m in the averaged sense.
Boundary endpoints are handled by the Lipschitz continuity of the array on the wedge.
    For the additional rate, a horizontal scalar annihilating both modes is

                      U = (θi − θj )(dP − 2ri dX) − 2(rj − ri )(dB + θi dX).                   (6.10)

Set q0 = z − p and u = p − q0 > 0. Resample R on the i fiber while sharing Xq0 . Then
A = XF − XR is O(δ q0 ) and, for every fixed χ > 0, at least δ q0 +χ in magnitude with probability
tending to one. This follows from the conditional version of (fib). The marked marginal is
stationary, and the target angular bits beyond the common prefix retain negligible information
with ZR , Ξ given C∗ , Xq0 . To see the exact comparison, set S = (C∗ , Xq0 ) and B = (Qi , D, Xq0 ).
The resampling kernel is the conditional side law given B. The conditional Markov property and
(fib) give

                          Iδ (XF∗ ; ZR , Ξ | S) ≤ Iδ (XF∗ ; Qi , D | C∗ , Xq0 )
                                                ≤ Iδ (XF∗ ; Qi , D | C∗ ) = o(1).

The second inequality uses that Xq0 is a prefix of XF∗ . Thus the comparison law keeps (ZR , Ξ)
unchanged and samples XF∗ independently with its conditional law given S. The common prefix
is already determined by ZR .
    Start the moving mark at depth u. Given ZR , fine XF bits, and ξi,u , the name Fp,z
has negligible lists. Horizontal uncertainty is O(Aδ u ) = O(δ p ). Guess the boundedly many
corresponding horizontal bins. With these bins fixed, the recentered uncertainty is

                                     O(A2 δ u ) + O(δ 2p ) = O(δ z ).

The mixed quadratic mark remainder is O(A2 δ 2u ), which is smaller.
   Freeze the i coefficients in Equation (6.10) at their u-parent representatives. Indeed its
expanded form is
                      U = (θi − θj )dP − 2(rj − ri )dB − 2(θi rj − θj ri )dX.
Each coefficient changes by O(δ u ). On a horizontal parent its centered increment has size O(δ p ),
so the change is O(δ p+u ), negligible at depth p + t when t < u. Absolute shifts at the parent are
retained during this comparison with the full record. Remove the remaining fine i mark by (vb)
before adjoining motion data. With XF fixed, the resulting mark-plane projection is
                                                                         
                                 2A (rj − ri,u ) dθi + (θi,u − θj ) dri .

After dilation of the mark parent, a low-rate list at depth p + t becomes a projection list at
depth t, up to arbitrarily small power losses. Its source has joint dimension at least d. More
explicitly, the common test data are ZR , XF∗ , ξi,u and the held records other than i, j. The source
is the child increment of ξi and the projection record is j. Under the comparison law they are
independent given these data: the marks are independent given ZR , and XF∗ is independent
of them given S. Conditional chain rules retain a vanishing information error at each fixed
u, t. The original low-score list can be unioned over the bounded parent possibilities described
above, giving a list for each j record. The mark innovation bounds apply at ZR , ξi,u . Radial

                                                    97
spreading at this same parent gives the unnormalized direction bound of exponent d over j on
the high witness relation. Finite-prefix likelihood trimming, with fixed small power slack, verifies
the projection hypotheses at the actual samples. This is Lemma 5.23 with side ZR , auxiliary
name XF∗ , S = (C∗ , Xq0 ), moving mark i at parent depth b = u, and pin record j. The required
separation exponent can be chosen so that u − χ > t, since t ≪ u. Thus U has incremental rate
at least d in the strict-error sense.
    This last argument does not assume homogeneous horizontal increments under the finer
center conditioning. More explicitly, an expected increment rate strictly below 2m + d along a
sequence t ↓ 0 would, by bounded relative capacities and the two residual angular rates, give a
positive-probability strict deficit for the U list. Radial witnesses provide the required finite-prefix
bounds with probability tending to one as t decreases. Select one sufficiently small fixed t, take
the tangent information errors to zero first, and apply the projection rule to exclude this deficit.
Integration over the open wedge, followed by Lipschitz endpoint control, proves (e-q).

Proposition 6.11 (Binary time estimate). Assume quadratic admissible data, m > 0, c > 3,
and line avoidance. At a binary layer p → 2p, freeze contact coefficients at Fp , and let

                            bmiss (p) = p−1 Hδ F2p | Fp,2p , D, (Li , Lj )2p
                                                                               


in the limiting entropy law. Then 0 ≤ bmiss ≤ 1 and e(p) ≥ bmiss (p) + 2m. Put r0 = c 2−⌊log2 c⌋ ∈
[1, 2). For the time lower bound, the term (c − 1)|Gi | in (time-rate) may be replaced by
                             1h                                          i
                               (c − r0 )(ᾱ − b̄miss ) + (r0 − 1)(ᾱ − 1) .                      (bin)
                             2
Proof. The center name and two independent horizontal forms leave only one scalar coordinate;
line avoidance supplies subpower singular-value bounds. Relative capacities therefore give
0 ≤ bmiss ≤ 1. The joint increment of Li , Lj given Fp,2p costs at least 2mp. Indeed Li annihilates ℓi
exactly and Lj sees it, and conversely. Given Qi , Xp , D, the center bins to depth 2p have bounded
lists by the fiber Taylor estimate. The horizontal invariance is linear, so these two comparisons
apply on the whole binary layer. The conditional chain rule yields e(p) ≥ bmiss (p) + 2m.
     On (λ, r0 λ) use the microlayer estimate and 2E|Gi | ≥ Eα−1. Tile [r0 λ, cλ] by binary intervals
[p, 2p], where p = c2−j λ. On each such interval,

                                     2p ≤ cλ,       p ≤ (c − 1)λ.

The output comparison in Proposition 6.2 then reads Gi to depth 2p in the contact basis at
Fp . The Taylor remainder is O(δ 2p ), and the coefficient error in (6.5) times the parent width is
no larger. Freeze the true (βi , ki ) at depth p as output-predicted auxiliary data before adding
marked conditioning. Subtract the velocity using the actual child bin time θ′ ; this avoids a
λ-depth error from an incorrect terminal-time backflow.
    The pair Gi , Gj at this accuracy recovers the three recentered center readings and Li , Lj ,
up to subpower losses. Hence symmetry and subadditivity lower-bound the information from a
single output by half the isotropic binary increment minus pbmiss (p)/2. Logarithmic averaging of
that isotropic increment divided by p gives ᾱ: integrate its expected derivative on [p, 2p] and use
dilation invariance. The binary interval lengths sum to (c − r0 )λ, and the initial interval has
length (r0 − 1)λ. Their sum proves (bin).

6.6   Interior action bounds
We now combine the phase rates into directions of decrease for the canonical excess. Put t = 1 − a.
Along a path with dv/dt = k ≥ 0, the inequality below gives dE(1 − t, v(t))/dt ≤ −2Γ wherever
it applies. We call k = 0 freezing: the horizontal resolution is held fixed. Recall that c = c′ is the
rate of the active template branch, so c = b′1 in the linear cases, including a clipped branch.

Proposition 6.12 (Admissible local actions). On a valid branch of (canon), the actions listed
below satisfy
                                    (∂a − k∂v )E ≥ 2Γ.                              (surplus)
Except where explicitly allowed, assume m > 0, radial spreading in the quadratic case, crossing
in the linear case, and line avoidance for the quadratic c > 1 estimates.

  (i) Quadratic freezing: k = 0 is admissible if m ≥ k0 /(1 + c) for 0 < c < 1, or m ≥ k0 /2 for
      c ≥ 1.

 (ii) Quadratic motion: for c ≥ 1, k = (c + 1)/4 is admissible.

(iii) At quadratic cost c = 1, without radial spreading and allowing m = 0, k = 1/2 is admissible
      when d ≤ 1/4.

 (iv) Linear freezing: k = 0 is admissible if m ≥ k0 . For c < 1, it suffices more generally that
      m ≥ k0 − cν, with m > 0 still required.

 (v) Linear motion with 0 < m ≤ k0 : for c ≥ 1, use k = (1 + c)/2; for c < 1 and m ≥ k0 /(1 + c),
     use k = c.

 (vi) Linear motion with 2d ≤ c < 1: k = 1 is admissible for every m ∈ [0, 1], including zero.
      Crossing is needed only when m > 0.

All assertions are stable under sufficiently small errors in their hypotheses and inequalities, with
the corresponding additive error in (surplus).

Proof. Write x, π, G, w, α, e for the expected barred rates. Equations (time-rate), (width-rate),
and (canon) reduce the claim to S ≥ 0, where

                        T = min(c, 1)(w + x) + (1 − c)+ π + (c − 1)+ G,

                        S = T − 1 − 2d − c + k
                                                   3 − α,      linear.

For c > 3 in the quadratic case we may replace the last term in T by (bin). The omitted favorable
η term is ηc in the linear case and η(c − k) in the quadratic case. It is nonnegative for the stated
speeds.
    Quadratic freezing. Suppose first c < 1. Equations (modes) and (mix-q) give

                  T ≥ l∗ + (1 + c)n∗ + (1 − c)a∗ + cm + c max(m + l∗ , a∗ + ν).                (6.12)

                  T ≥ (1 + c)l∗ + 2d + 2cm + (1 + c2 )ν ≥ (1 + c)l∗ + 2d + 2cm.

for c ≤ 1. If ν ≥ k0 , then n∗ = 1 and the maximum is at least a∗ + ν. Equation (6.12) gives
                           T ≥ 1 + c + l∗ + cm + a∗ + cν ≥ 1 + c + 2d,
because l∗ ≥ d and a∗ + cν ≥ d.
   For c ≥ 1, the center and horizontal bounds give w ≥ 2m + 2l∗ + x and G ≥ 2m + x. Hence
                                    T ≥ 2cm + 2l∗ + (1 + c)d.
If m ≤ k0 , subtracting 1 + 2d + c gives (1 + c)(2m − k0 ) ≥ 0. If m ≥ k0 , substitute l∗ = 1 and

    Quadratic motion. For 1 ≤ c ≤ 3, use 2G ≥ α − 1, x ≥ d, e ≥ 2m + d, and k = (c + 1)/4.

                              4S ≥ (3 − c)(1 − d) + 2(c − 1)m ≥ 0.
For c > 3, set b = b̄miss ∈ [0, 1]. The two width bounds imply e ≥ 2m + max(b, d). Substitution

b = d, 1 need be checked. The respective values are
                  (c + 3 − 2r0 )(1 − d) + 2(c − 1)m,      4(1 − d) + 2(c − 1)m,
both nonnegative since r0 < 2.
   At c = 1, using only e ≥ 2m and x ≥ 0 gives

This proves the third assertion, including m = 0.
   Linear freezing. If c < 1 and ν > 0, Equation (mix-l) gives T ≥ 2l∗ + (1 + c)n∗ + cν. Its

                       2 min(m, k0 ) − (1 + c)k0 + (1 + c) min(k0 , ν) + cν.
For ν ≥ k0 this is nonnegative. For ν ≤ k0 , if m ≥ k0 it is also immediate. Otherwise

T ≥ π + cx ≥ 2d + (1 + c)l∗ . The threshold gives l∗ = 1. For c = 1 use w ≥ x + 2d and x ≥ l∗ = 1.
For c > 1 use w ≥ 2d + G, G ≥ m + l∗ , and x ≥ l∗ . These yield T ≥ 2d + c(m + 1) + 1 ≥ 1 + 2d + c.
   Linear motion. For c ≥ 1, put k = (1 + c)/2 and use 2G ≥ α − 1. Cancellation gives
S ≥ 1 + x − 2d − m. Since m ≤ k0 , l∗ = d + m; the bound x ≥ l∗ leaves S ≥ k0 . For c < 1, put
k = c. The α terms cancel directly, leaving
                            S = −cm + cx + (1 − c)π + 2c − 1 − 2d.
Use x ≥ l∗ and π ≥ 2d + l∗ . The latter follows from (double) if ν = 0; if ν > 0, (mix-l) implies it
because l∗ + n∗ ≥ 2d. Thus

                                                100
Finally take k = 1 and 2d ≤ c < 1. Equation (miss) gives

                                         S ≥ 1 − 2d − m + cx.

When m > 0, crossing and ℓj give x ≥ m; when m = 0 the same inequality is trivial. Since
m ≤ 1, this lower bound is at least c − 2d ≥ 0.
   Every calculation involves finitely many linear inequalities with bounded coefficients on a
compact parameter range. Strict-error versions therefore give the claimed stability; changing the
speed costs the corresponding Lipschitz error.


7      Completion of the cylinder estimate
We now finish the proof of Theorem 3.6. The canonical excess E is nonnegative and vanishes at
the equality points specified in Proposition 5.4. Proposition 6.12 supplies directions in which it
decreases when followed backwards in time. The final contradiction comes from a path starting
at an equality boundary along which this decrease remains valid. The remaining difficulties are
the unit-cost quadratic boundary and intervals on which the angular trace vanishes.
    All entropy limits in this section use the ordered limits of the canonical construction. In
particular, a finite list of macroscopic queries is fixed before the configuration, mesh, and scale
limits; a tangent gap is subsequently allowed to tend to zero.

7.1     A line estimate at unit quadratic cost
At unit quadratic cost the needed fixed-width time estimate is 2 + 3d. On an equality boundary
it contradicts the canonical entropy upper bound; in the strict-curvature case it gives the missing
lower bound for ∂a E. The next lemma isolates this estimate when the orientation marks lie close
to a line determined by the side data. Its hypotheses record time uncertainty, angular uncertainty
along a fiber, and a mismatch between the target line and the line obtained by resampling with
the same fiber label. Its conclusion concerns the entropy of one observation output.

Lemma 7.1 (Fixed-width line estimate). Let δ → 0. Let C be a common root name and let
side data Z determine C, a quadratic phase F = (X, B, P, Y, N, D), and coefficients (βF , κF ).
The normalized phase, coefficients, and marks have size δ −o(1) . Conditional on Z , draw two
independent observations with the same conditional law, whose marks satisfy

                                     ri = βF + κF θi + O(δ 10−o(1) ).                            (s-line)

Suppose the following hypotheses hold, with arbitrary fixed strict resolution slack and with
exceptional probabilities tending to zero.

    (i) The conditional score of θi,p+t given Z , θi,p is at least dt, for the finitely many queries used
        below through depth 3, including queries starting at 0, where d > 0.

 (ii) Each observation carries a fiber label Si such that (C, Si ) determines its marks and the
      quadratic entries of Qi in the fiber formulas to single-valued error δ 10−o(1) .

 (iii) Each observation has an output Oi with these recovery properties. The pair (C, Oi ) reads
       θi to depth 1, and together with Xp+t reads Fp+t whenever 0 < p < p + t < 1, up to lists of



                                                   101
       vanishing exponent. If 1 < p < p + t < 2 and t ≪ ε, p, it also predicts (θi , ri )ε and, using
       these coefficients and Fp , reads
                                          K + θi,ε J − ri,ε H
       to depth p + t. Here H, J, K are the contact forms, evaluated in a fixed positive-depth prefix
       frame and translated at Fp .

 (iv) For each i individually,

                           Iδ (X6 ; Si | C) = o(1),           iδ (Xp | C) = M (p) + o(1)

       in probability at the tested prefixes, where M is deterministic, M (0) = 0, and M (p) ≥ g∗ p
       for some g∗ > 0.

 (v) Draw R independently from the posterior law of the side and observation given (C, Si ). Its
     line satisfies

                 |κR − κF | ≥ δ χ    with probability tending to one, for every fixed χ > 0.          (mis)

Then
             Hδ (Oi | C) ≥ d + g∗ + 2 min(1, 2d) + d + min(1, d + g∗ ) − o(1).                   (line-time)
In particular, g∗ ≥ 1 − 2d implies the lower bound 2 + 3d − o(1). The finite accuracy constants 6
and 10 may be increased.

Proof. The angular capacities make M Lipschitz. Put m(p) = M ′ (p) at its Lebesgue points.
Fix the tested observation i, its posterior draw R, and the other target observation j. Write
A = XF − XR and, for a small fixed ε > 0, condition on

                                      Ω = (βF , κF , θi , ri , θj , rj , A)ε .

Its normalized entropy given C is O(ε). In the contact frame of the current prefix define

                     L = dP − 2βF,ε dX + 2κF,ε dB,
                     J ′ = J − κF,ε H,         K ′ = K − βF,ε H,
                     G = (L, J ′ , K ′ ),      x = K ′ + θi,ε J ′ ,         y = J ′ − 12 Aε L.

Unless specified otherwise, rates are conditional on (C, Ω, Fp ); an additional held reading is held
to child depth p + t. First restrict p to compact subintervals of (0, 1) and (1, 2). After ε and a
positive contact-prefix depth are fixed, take t smaller than these depths and than p.
    We first account for the cost of Ω. For each of the nested refinement families
                                X beyond p | C, Si , Xp or C, Sj , Xp ,
                                θe beyond p | C, ZR , XF,6 , θep ,                                     (7.1)
                                F beyond p | C, Fp , Ω0 ,

where Ω0 is any fixed subtuple needed below, the integrated expected information loss on adding
Ω is at most Hδ (Ω | C) = O(ε). This is the conditional chain rule applied along the refinement
family. The cumulative losses have nonnegative Lipschitz limits, so their derivatives have the
same integral bound.


                                                       102
    Before this addition the first family has rate m(p) by stationarity. For the second, the
posterior draw has the original side-and-observation marginal. Conditional independence given
(C, Si ) and the first stationarity hypothesis give

                           Iδ (XF,6 ; ZR , observation at R | C) = o(1).

Its time innovations therefore retain the lower rate d, by (LP). The same information comparison
and M (z) ≥ g∗ z show that |A| ≥ δ χ with probability tending to one for each fixed χ > 0: the
contrary event puts XF in a bounded list of depth-χ bins predicted by XR .
    These budgets justify all subsequent rate errors as follows. Fix a strict proposed deficit and a
positive lower bound for its probability. Let ε ↓ 0, discard layer points where one of the finitely
many loss densities is too large, and then choose typical remaining points for the pathwise and
mixed-prefix differentiations. At each such point let t ↓ 0. The expected loss divided by t tends
to zero along this procedure; the likelihood inequalities (LP) turn it into a vanishing score error
on all but a vanishing probability. The same argument gives a vanishing product-likelihood cost,
measured relative to t log(1/δ), for each planar projection test. Conditioning additionally on a
child reading which does not use the removed subtuple costs no more: the chain rule bounds the
remaining information by that of the full source, with the negligible list entropy of that reading
added. All rates have bounded capacities, so discarded probabilities and layer sets contribute
vanishing errors to the integrals. This procedure requires no uniform differentiability in ε.
    The output chain rule now gives
                                Z 1                         Z 2
                                                     
            Hδ (Oi | C) ≥ d +          α0 (p) − m(p) dp +         E|x|(p) dp − o(1),      (L-chain)
                                 0                           1

where α0 is the expected isotropic phase rate before adding Ω. Indeed, conditional on fine phase,
the time contribution is at least d after conditioning further on Z . Below depth 1, adding the
angular bits recovers the phase bits and costs at most their rate m. Between depths 1 and 2,
first add the coarse mark coefficients predicted by the output and then add Ω. Translate the
displayed reading at Fp and use (s-line) to obtain x. Tiling compact interior intervals and taking
limits proves (L-chain); bounded capacities allow the omitted endpoints to tend to 0, 1, 2.
    Both angular fiber modes ℓi , ℓj annihilate G to the required accuracy. They give

                                       |F | ≥ |G| + 2m(p) − o(1).                         (L-mode)

For completeness, add Si , Xp for the first mode, or Sj , Xp for the second. The fiber equations
predict the parent and all annihilating readings; replacing line coefficients by their depth-ε
representatives introduces error O(δ p+ε−o(1) ). First use dB + θi,ε dX, which sees ℓj , and then
dX, which sees ℓi . The two times are separated by every fixed power threshold, by conditional
independent sampling and their time-score bound. Each inversion thus costs an arbitrarily small
power, and the first budgets in (7.1) supply the two residual rates m(p).
   Next vary θe at R, holding ZR and XF,6 and starting inside a depth-p time parent. The
shared fiber label identifies the target mark with (θ,
                                                    e βR + κR θ)
                                                               e to the fine prescribed accuracy.

                                      (κR − κF )(2A, A2 , −θi A2 ) dθ,

up to the coarse-coefficient error. Here βR − βF = −θi (κR − κF ) to the same accuracy. This
response annihilates x, y, whereas its J ′ and L components are separated from zero by arbitrary

power slack, using (mis) and the separation of A. The parent is predicted by the fiber formulas.
The remaining Taylor error is O(δ 2p−o(1) ), which is finer than δ p+t . The contact forms are frozen;

                             |J ′ | x| ≥ d − o(1),  |L | x, y| ≥ d − o(1).                     (L-res)

initially retain only the line coefficients from Ω, and vary the time slope θi,ε . Conditional on
Z , the trimmed direction sublaw has exponent d; integrating this unnormalized bound supplies
the required direction bound at the source parent. The last budgets in (7.1) justify reinstating
the remaining records for the score and list tests. If S is the joint source rate, (L-res) gives
S ≥ |x| + d − o(1), and the projection bound |x| ≥ min(1, S, (S + d)/2) − o(1) therefore gives
|x| ≥ min(1, 2d) − o(1).
    For the second application condition on x to child accuracy, retain the target entries of Ω,
and vary Aε in the projection y of (L, J ′ ). Here is the exact information comparison for this
conditioning. Write T for the target subtuple of Ω, B∗ = Aε , and D∗ = (C, Fp , T ). Let W∗ be
the child bin of the frozen reading x, and let Z∗ be the planar child source (L, J ′ ). Both are
determined to negligible lists by a sufficiently fine relative child phase name V∗ and D∗ , without
using B∗ . The name V∗ may be taken at depth p + (1 + ξ)t with arbitrarily small fixed ξ > 0;
the extra capacity is O(ξt). The conditional chain rule gives

                Iδ (Z∗ ; B∗ | D∗ , W∗ ) ≤ Iδ (V∗ ; B∗ | D∗ ) + Hδ (Z∗ , W∗ | D∗ , V∗ ),           (7.2)
                Iδ (V∗ ; B∗ | C, Fp , T ) = Iδ (V∗ ; B∗ | C, T ) − Iδ (Fp ; B∗ | C, T ) + o(1).   (7.3)

For the first inequality, adjoin V∗ to the source, and use I(V∗ ; B∗ | D∗ , W∗ ) ≤ I(V∗ ; B∗ |
D∗ ) + H(W∗ | D∗ , V∗ ). The second equality uses nested phase bins, with the negligible list
error if their grids are only list-compatible. The cumulative mutual information on the right is
nondecreasing and bounded by Hδ (B∗ | C, T ) = O(ε). Consequently its loss density is one of
the integrated budgets already treated above. Divide (7.2) by t, use that budget at the retained
layer points, and then let the strict resolution slack ξ tend to zero. This proves the needed
vanishing source-record information after the child conditioning. In particular, W∗ uses the
frozen target coefficients and no A; coefficient replacement is by a known translation at Fp and
an error O(δ p+ε−o(1) ), not by an A-dependent reading.
    For the direction bound, let the full target data include its side and its two observations.
The names D∗ , W∗ are functions of these data. Conditional on the full target data, R still
has the posterior kernel given (C, Si ). Stationarity and M (z) ≥ g∗ z trim its angular law to
unnormalized ball bounds of exponent g∗ . More explicitly, for each required fixed depth and
strict tolerance retain only posterior angular cells whose conditional mass given (C, Si ) is at most
the corresponding δ g∗ z−tolerance . Their union has probability tending to one by the stationary
scores, and their unnormalized mass in any constant-multiple depth-z ball has the same bound
up to a fixed neighbor factor, for every conditioning value. Translation and reflection give the
same bound for A for every full-target value. Integrating these restricted kernels over the full
target conditional on (D∗ , W∗ ) preserves the bound. No normalization of the retained sublaw is
used in this step. The projection uses layers t ≪ ε, so rounding the direction is harmless. The
last budget, now with mixed-prefix linearity for the source conditioned on x, again permits the
full record in the test. Its residual beyond y is at least d − o(1). Thus

                |x| ≥ min(1, 2d) − o(1),          |G| − |x| ≥ d + min(1, d + g∗ ) − o(1).         (7.4)

                                                     104
Each assertion means that a fixed strict deficit of positive probability contradicts the corresponding
finite projection test with the Rloss order specified above.
    Finally α0 ≥ E|F |, and 01 m(p) dp = M (1) ≥ g∗ . Substitute (L-mode) and (7.4) into
(L-chain), and then send all the budget and endpoint errors to zero. This gives (line-time). To
check the last claim, if d ≤ 1/2, use g∗ ≥ 1 − 2d and min(1, d + g∗ ) ≥ 1 − d; if d ≥ 1/2, use g∗ > 0
and min(1, d + g∗ ) ≥ d. In either case the stated lower bound is at least 2 + 3d.

7.2    Boundary applications of the line estimate
The line estimate has two uses. At the stationary equality boundary it forces the radial spreading
needed by the interior estimates. In the strict-curvature case it gives a fixed-width time bound
directly. Both applications must verify the posterior-line mismatch in (mis).

Lemma 7.2 (The stationary equality boundary). In the classical equality configuration q = c2 = 1,
J = 0, the exact-side quadratic replica construction has the radial spreading property at almost
every strong time a, where p∗ (a) > b1 (a). This holds on interior patches v > 0 with a common
static depth b(a) + v < x∗ < p∗ (a).

Proof. Here u = 0 and b1 = b2 = a − 1. Use the unclipped quadratic template and request the
canonical profiles also on the fixed boundary v = 0, where E(a, 0) = 0. Countably many depth
queries suffice. Choose a typical strong a at which the prediction slack persists immediately to

    We prove the remaining mismatch property while keeping a, j, D, h fixed. Suppose a δ χ
interval for κF is predicted from (C, Si ), where 0 < χ < 1 is fixed and small. Choose 0 < ω < χ
and group on [j, D] by bins of Kj and βj (Tj ) of widths

                                         sζ+ωh ,      sb(j)+ωh .

Starting rows know these bins. The end label knows the final canonical names at the used times
below j at width v0 , hence their j-versions, C, Si , and k̄j , to small lists. It therefore predicts the
slope bins on success paths. Given one such slope, it guesses the value bin at Tj from βD (TD ):
Equation (A) and time transport apply because min[j,D] b2 > b(j). Include the ordinary time
and start-rounding choices. After subtraction the residual end value is bounded by sb(j)+ωh−o(1) ,
and the residual slope times the terminal duration by sb2 (D)−o(1) . Since b2 (D) − b(j) = D − j,
the curvature cost on [j, D] is at most 1 − ωh/(D − j), a fixed strict improvement.
    Classically, failure of (mis) yields a prediction with nonvanishing final mass. Use the
Borel inner success and completion marks of Lemma 3.19 at D, inside the current end-state
support; Equation (Sp) and the count bounds supply the group lower bound, using outer hulls of
unrestricted geometric predecessors. Exact particle and time starts fix the earlier gate ownership,
so these are actual path groups. Group extraction contradicts minimality of the curvature cost.
    In hybrid the working law is Pj , and a prediction-success event there might have little
terminal mass. To address this, discard before j-synthesis all rows whose depth-χ slope bin has
conditional mass at least sτ given (C, Si ), with τ > 0 sufficiently small. These maps use the
single observation on that row and their Borel versions under the full analyzed j-row energy law
as in Lemma 3.19. For each conditioning value there are at most s−τ discarded bins. We use a

                                                   108
closure argument because τ is not yet a vanishing exponent. Hold a, j, D, h, χ, ω fixed, and put
γ0 = ωh/(D − j) > 0. If arbitrarily small τ allowed the discarded synthesis to approach terminal
saturation, choose τn ↓ 0 and genuine approximating experiments whose terminal deficit and
prior mesh, profile, and regularization losses tend to zero faster than τn . For each n group on
the same fixed interval [j, D] by the preceding construction. Each reachable end label has the
expanded list of groups. Finite-overlap inequalities have normalized loss O(τn /(D − j)), while
other list and approximation losses can be made o(τn )/(D − j). Regularity is retained before
propagation, grouped energies sum to the j-energy budget, and counts have the same overlap loss.
The quantitative loss form of group extraction selects genuine experiments whose extremality
and count losses tend to zero in the [j, D] units. Their curvature costs are at most 1 − γ0 + o(1),
since h, j, D, ω remain fixed. They realize the original extremal exponents in the closure with
cost at most 1 − γ0 /2 eventually, contradicting minimality. This applies the vanishing-overlap
clause along τn ; it does not apply a zero-loss extraction statement at fixed τ . Consequently some
sufficiently small fixed τ > 0 leaves a strict positive terminal-exponent deficit on the discarded
part. The triangle inequality for the terminal functional shows that the complementary synthesis
preserves the terminal exponent. Its pre-synthesis row fraction is consequently so(1) .
    Restrict only the j-row probability law to this complement. On retained rows the original
conditional slope-bin mass is below sτ . Except on a vanishing restricted fraction, conditioning
normalization given (C, Si ) multiplies mass by at most s−τ /2 : the excluded conditioning cells have
original retained mass at most sτ /2 , whereas the total retained mass is so(1) . Neighboring-bin
counting therefore makes posterior collisions at the tested precision vanish. The same fixed
representative maps, time bounds, and deterministic scores are preserved by this restriction.
For finitely many separation queries repeat the exclusion with previous errors sufficiently small;
diagonal limits give (mis) for every fixed threshold. This is a restriction of the j-law for the
entropy test, not a claim about the final law of a modified stack. The strict curvature improvement
is needed only for fixed h.
    Lemma 7.1 now gives ∂a H(a, v0 ) ≥ 2 + 3d. On this fixed-width quadratic branch, ∂a W = 1 − η
and p + d = 1 + 3d − 2Γ in (canon); hence ∂a E ≥ 2Γ + η ≥ 2Γ. Finally, the inequalities used
above hold for every q < 1: b2 (j) − j − ζ = (q − 1)(j − D) > 0, and min[j,D] b2 > b2 (D) + j − D
whether q is positive or negative.

7.3    Finite motion when the angular trace may vanish
Throughout the remaining argument write k0 = 1 − d and p + d = 1 + 3d − 2Γ, where 0 < d ≤ 1.
Motion is in backwards time t = 1 − a, at speed k = dv/dt ≥ 0. Thus (surplus) makes E decrease
at rate at least 2Γ. On a fixed compact template domain the limiting profiles are Lipschitz with
a fixed constant, uniformly for small normal enlargements g > 0. The two normal-bin list costs
give
                                         |E g − E| ≤ Cg.                                    (enl)
Original and enlarged names may have deterministic profiles on the same law: first request a
countable sequence g ↓ 0 and dense depth lists; later finite requests are readout refinements.

Lemma 7.4 (Finite off-gap motion). Consider either an unclipped classical quadratic equality
branch with b′ = c > 3, b′1 > 0, and uniform strong-prediction slack p∗ (a) > b(a) + v, or a classical
linear branch with rate c ≥ 1. The latter may be the right part of a clipped equality template, or
a pure linear template. Fix a small g > 0 and let h > 0 be sufficiently small compared with g



                                                 109
and the prediction slack. Set δ = sh . Let K be a sufficiently fine uniform finite grid in [c, c + 1],
including its endpoints, and set

                   kR = k/2      in the quadratic case,          kR = k     in the linear case.

In particular kR ≥ 1. For a fixed K∗ sufficiently large compared with c + 1, define

   C = C g (a, v),      O = C g (a + h, v),      C k = C g (a, v + kR h),     MX = lim Hs (X v+K∗ h | C).

Provided the names stay on the specified branch, some k ∈ K satisfies

                                E g (a + h, v) − E g (a, v + kR h) ≥ Γh − MX .                        (step)

Proof. Use the actual C-shear at ta . Center the residual normal velocity and starting position
in their C-bins, and divide by sw−g , sa+w−g , respectively. The bounded pair V = (Vn , Vd ) is a
function of (y0 , C). The output and root know θ′ = (ta+h − ta )/sa . Given C, let Sk contain

  (i) X v+K∗ h ;

 (ii) Bta to depth a + v + kR h and Yta − 2[X]v+K∗ h Bta to depth a + 2v + 2kR h;

 (iii) in the quadratic case, P − 2Ra X + 2ka Bta to depth l(a, v) + kR h, in the root curvature
       cuts.

These data refine with k, up to zero-cost lists. Request the finite deterministic profiles of
(Sk , Vz ), z, k ∈ K, and their needed joints, without altering the earlier profiles. Write D(z | k) =
lim Hδ (Vz | C, Sk ).
     Two geometric comparisons will be used:

                                  (O, C) predict (Vn )c and (Vd + θ′ Vn )c+1 ,
                                                                                                       (7.5)
                   (C, Sk , Vk ) predict C k ,      lim Hs (Sk | O, C) ≤ MX + h(3kR − 2).

Here and below all prediction lists have vanishing exponent at fixed h. To verify the first line,
the child basis minus the root basis on the actual ray has derivative discrepancy O(sl ) and
curvature discrepancy O(sb ) in sa duration units. In curvature cases b + v ≥ l. Evaluating
this difference at the known root base-bin center instead costs only O(sw ) in normal velocity
and O(sa+w ) in position, including the vertical base tilt. Since (c + 1)h ≪ g, these errors fit
both child accuracies in the enlarged normal units. Evaluate all known offsets at the exact bin
times. Thus the predictions are single centers with the required radii, not merely unspecified
lists. For C k , the Sk data supply its refined base and derivative bins. The same shear-difference
comparison bounds the normal error within its requested flat widths.
     For the entropy bound in (7.5), first give X v+K∗ h . Backflow from (O, C) reads both the
horizontal and tilted vertical positions at normalized depth at least 1. In the tilted coordinate
the velocity is X 2 − 2[X]v+K∗ h X, whose error about the refined center is quadratic. The two
extra position costs are therefore (kR − 1)h and (2kR − 1)h. In the quadratic case the output
displays derivative refinement ch, which suffices because kR ≤ c. Changing back to the root
curvature basis costs O(sb ) in earlier-time units; multiplying by the unresolved base errors in the
guessed position bins costs at most O(sl+kR h ). No further derivative entropy is required.
     Conditionally on (y0 , C, Sk ), time bins at δ-depth z ∈ [0, 1] have typical score at least dz.
Indeed (C, Sk ) is predicted to small lists from (y0 , ta ) on all paths outside the profile exceptions.

                                                         110
Root curvature predictions follow from (A), (F), and cut ownership. The quadratic derivative
query is strictly below p∗ (a); in a clipped linear branch it uses only l = b1 (a), and in pure
linear form it uses the center slope comparison. Exact classical flow predicts the phase entries.
Expanding any popular time list by these predicted lists and applying the causal time count
through a + zh gives power-small failures at each fixed strict tolerance. Quantizing the child bin-
start time changes only bounded factors. Likewise, deterministic profiles and the neighboring-bin
likelihood bound give conditional V -ball mass at most δ D(z|k)−o(1) about typical samples, for
z ∈ K. These are bounds for the unrestricted conditional mass of those balls. The trimmed time
law has unnormalized interval bounds of exponent d.
    We claim

                 Hδ (O | C, Sk ) ≥ d + min{D(z | k) + d(c + 1 − z)} − O(mesh K).                 (sh)
                                       z∈K

The first term is supplied by Hδ (O | y0 , C, Sk ). For the information with y0 , draw an independent
reference y0′ given (C, Sk ), and let V ′ be its normal pair. If it is admissible for (O, C), the
first comparison in (7.5) forces agreement in Vn to O(δ c ) and in Vd + θ′ Vn to O(δ c+1 ). Hence
|V ′ − V | ≲ δ c . If its distance lies between consecutive grid widths δ z+µ and O(δ z ), where
µ = mesh K, the velocity difference is comparable to the full difference unless the latter is already
at final width. Consequently the time projection confines θ′ to an interval of length O(δ c+1−z−µ ).
At the final width no time gain is charged.
     Average reference admissibility over actual samples satisfying both typicality bounds. Given
the actual (y0 , C, Sk ), use the conditional ball bound for V ′ and, for each fixed V ′ , the trimmed
time interval bound. Summing the finitely many annuli bounds this average by the power in
(sh) minus its first d, with strict tolerance and the O(µ) loss. Markov’s inequality makes the
reference admissibility probability at most that power with slightly more tolerance for most
actual (O, C, Sk ). The actual posterior given (O, C, Sk ) is supported on the admissible set. The
relative entropy bound for a measure supported on a reference set of mass q, namely log(1/q),
therefore gives the information term in (sh). All trimming was on the actual sample in this
averaging argument; no exceptional-set transfer to the independent product law is used.
     For z2 > z1 , the increment D(z2 | k) − D(z1 | k) is nonincreasing in k, because Sk refines.
Thus the largest minimizer z(k) in (sh) is nondecreasing. A nondecreasing self-map of a finite
ordered set has a fixed point: iterate from its least element. At such a point z(k) = k, use (7.5)
and refinement of C by O to obtain

              lim{Hδ (O) − Hδ (C k )} ≥ d + d(c + 1 − k) + 2 − 3kR − MX /h − O(µ).

The weight exponent difference, later minus moved, divided by h, is −(3 + η)kR + (1 − η)(c − k).
Subtracting this and p + d as in (canon) gives

   E g (a + h, v) − E g (a, v + kR h)
                                      ≥ (1 − d)(1 + k − c) + 2Γ + η(kR + c − k) − MX /h − O(µ).
                   h
Both extra displayed terms are nonnegative: k ∈ [c, c + 1], d ≤ 1, and kR + c − k ≥ 0 in the stated
branches. Choose µ small enough to retain Γ. The finite refinement proof can be applied at any
macroscopic path node to the same profiles E g , MX ; it does not select microscopic configurations
adaptively. This proves (step).

7.4    The off-gap path contradiction
The finite step loses only the angular information MX . We compare that loss with angular scores
at later backward times, so that its accumulated contribution vanishes across widths where the
trace is zero. This lets us join finite steps to the differential motions from the interior estimates.
    Write Etg (v) = E g (1 − t, v), Et (v) = Et0 (v), and mt (v) = m(1 − t, v). Denote the cost in
(step) with earlier root (1 − t − h, v) by MX (t, v; h, g). Thus a permitted speed k satisfies

                           Etg (v) − Et+h
                                      g
                                          (v + kh) ≥ Γh − MX (t, v; h, g).

On a fixed compact canonical domain there is a constant A0 such that, if t′ > t + h + A0 g and
0 ≤ v − h ≤ y ≤ v, then

                           MX (t, v; h, g) ≤ U y + (K∗ + 1)h; 1 − t′ , y .
                                                                         
                                                                                               (shift)

Indeed the enlarged name at (1 − t − h, v) predicts the ordinary name at (1 − t′ , y). Nesting
handles time, curvature, derivative, and base data. The positive forward rate of the normal-width
exponent absorbs g in this time shift; backflow in the fine shear costs its enlarged velocity
width and that width times the longer duration, and the shear-difference estimate on the
base cell fits the ordinary coarse widths. This remains valid across a clipping switch. Finally
y + (K∗ + 1)h ≥ v + K∗ h, so conditioning and refinement prove (shift). For each fixed rational t′
the angular profile properties give

                  Fh (t′ , y) := h−1 U (y + (K∗ + 1)h; 1 − t′ , y) −→ (K∗ + 1)mt′ (y)           (7.6)

for almost every y, with 0 ≤ Fh ≤ K∗ + 1.

Lemma 7.5 (Off-gap path contradiction). Suppose that on an open time interval and in a
sufficiently narrow right neighborhood of v = Jt the following hold. The profiles E, E g are
nonnegative and uniformly Lipschitz on the compact domains used, satisfy (enl), and Et (Jt) = 0.
Equations (step) and (shift) apply, with bounded step speeds whose minimum is at least 1 and
strictly greater than J; step admissibility is uniform on compact subsets for h/g small enough.
The angular rate has its nondecreasing-in-t version. There is a speed k+ ≥ 1, k+ > J, for which
(surplus) holds almost everywhere where mt (v) > 0. Alternatively, that motion need only hold
for 0 < mt (v) ≤ k0 provided the vertical estimate ∂t Et (v) ≤ −2Γ holds almost everywhere where
mt (v) > k0 , throughout the constant-width continuations used, including after crossing the seam.
The normal-width exponent has positive forward rate on all branches needed for (shift). These
conditions are incompatible with Γ > 0.

Proof. Fix compact domains and all Lipschitz and speed bounds before choosing density scales.
Let C0 denote constants depending only on these bounds and K∗ . Outside a null set of widths
set
                              σ(v) = inf{t ∈ Q : mt (v) > 0},
with the terminal endpoint convention if the set is empty. Take rational queries in a containing
interval and truncate this threshold to its closure if necessary. Monotonicity implies positivity at
almost every relevant point above σ, whereas mt′ (v) = 0 at each rational t′ < σ(v) outside its
null set of exceptional widths.



                                                 112
   We first justify integration on a prescribed positive segment. Fix a slab [s, T ] and a measurable
width set P where mt (v) > 0 for almost every t in that slab. Consider a proposed segment of
speed k+ , duration ℓ, and width interval W . At its initial point one has either
                                Es (v) ≥ 2Γℓ + Es+ℓ (v + k+ ℓ) − C0 |W \ P |,                     (7.7)
                        or      Es (v) ≥ 2Γ(T − s) − C0 |W \ P |.                                 (7.8)
To see this without assuming the proposed line is generic, perturb its initial width. For almost
every perturbation, Fubini’s theorem and the Lipschitz chain rule give the moving estimate almost
everywhere and valid vertical estimates at almost every encountered width. Time spent outside
P is bounded by |W \ P |/k+ , with an error tending to zero under the perturbation. If freezing is
allowed and the high-rate set m > k0 on that line has positive time measure, choose a regular
point arbitrarily close to its first essential occurrence. The measure of earlier high-rate times
can be made arbitrarily small. Integrate the moving estimate to that point, using the Lipschitz
bound on those earlier high-rate times, and then freeze to T . Monotonicity retains m > k0 on the
vertical continuation, and endpoint nonnegativity proves (7.8). If there is no positive-measure
high-rate set, integrate to the moving endpoint to get (7.7). Let the earlier-high-time tolerance
and then the perturbation tend to zero. A subsequence either always freezes or always reaches the
proposed endpoint, and Lipschitz continuity gives the corresponding alternative at the original
starting point.
    Suppose J > 0 and put T (v) = v/J on an interior seam-width interval. It is covered by
                     Z0 = {σ(v) ≥ T (v)},            {σ(v) ≤ T (v) − 1/n},             n ≥ 1.

                                                         114
7.5    Exhaustion of the affine configurations
Proof of Theorem 3.6. First suppose a classical extremal configuration has Γ > η/2. After it is
excluded, suppose a hybrid configuration has Γ > η/2. It then has the strict gap Γ > max(Γcl , η/2)
required for (Pl). The affine reduction (F) and the zero-cost arguments leave precisely the positive-
cost cases treated below. All uses of (surplus) are on valid branches with the indicated crossing
or spreading hypotheses.
    We describe once the integration conventions. Almost-everywhere estimates on affine rays
are integrated on generic arbitrarily nearby rays and passed to the desired one by Lipschitz
continuity. A speed with surplus 2Γ may be decreased slightly while retaining surplus at least
Γ. Rays from the terminal corner can start at a small positive backward time, with initial E
tending to zero. If a high-rate linear portion is encountered, the freezing alternative in the proof
of Lemma 7.5 applies.
    We also require a short-slab observation. Suppose b′1 > 0 and choose a typical j with
E(j, u(j)) = 0. Work in a short slab ending at j, omitting a small fixed fraction next to its
final edge. For v > u(j) sufficiently close to u(j), the pair (λj , X v ) recovers C(a, v) to small lists
throughout the shortened slab. In the unclipped quadratic case, choose it so that b(a) + v < b1 (j).
If Rj is the angular profile given λj , conditioning gives

                          m(a, v) ≥ Rj′ (v)   for almost every such (a, v).                        (7.9)

An integrated lower slope for Rj through u(j) supplies positive-measure sets of arbitrarily close
widths with derivative at least that slope minus any prescribed strict error. Otherwise integration
would contradict the lower slope. A bound above capacity is already impossible. Choose these
widths generically for all almost-everywhere assertions. If the supplied derivative exceeds a
freezing threshold, (surplus) holds throughout the slab, except for any stipulated small time-
density error. Its positive integral contradicts nonnegativity: the terminal width perturbation
and the omitted edge cost arbitrarily little by Lipschitz continuity and E(j, u(j)) = 0. When a
threshold depends on the mixed trace ν, choose j to be a Lebesgue point of that trace. The strict
margin at j then survives off a time set of arbitrarily small relative length. All extra j-profile
queries are readout refinements after j has been selected.
    Finally, if p∗ > b1 on a positive-measure set of interior times, monotonicity of p∗ and continuity
of b1 give a smaller open interval with uniform strict prediction slack. Otherwise p∗ = b1 at almost
every time under consideration. We call these the strong and weak alternatives, respectively.

Equality curvature: q = c2 > 0. Put c = c2 and C1 = c − J = b′1 . If J > 0, consider left
quadratic points 0 < v < u(a) with l < min[a,1] b1 and, in hybrid, w < 0. Write v = u(j) for a
later j < 1. Left memory gives

                 k0 + 2Γ + η(Lraw − 1)

                         Lraw
The coarse-side construction and avoidance supply radial spreading, including the extra left-slack
version when c = 1. If 0 < c ≤ 1, then Lraw ≤ 1 + c, so (7.10) exceeds the quadratic freezing
threshold k0 /(1 + c). On every compact interior time slab all sufficiently small v > 0 meet the
hypotheses. Integrate at those widths, let v ↓ 0, and then let the trimmed endpoint tend to 1.
The equality E(1, 0) = 0 contradicts the resulting positive drop. The same argument applies for
c > 1 when Lraw ≤ 2, using the threshold k0 /2.

    If c > 1 and J ≥ (c + 1)/4, use a ray from the terminal corner at a speed just below (c + 1)/4.
It stays in the left region, has 2k < c, and satisfies the derivative prediction condition; if b1
decreases forward, l < 0 suffices. The positive quadratic motion in (surplus) is contradictory.
This covers hybrid equality configurations because (Pl) gives J = (c + 1/2)/2 there.
    The remaining configurations are classical and have b′1 > 0: either J = 0, or c > 1 and
0 < J < (c+1)/4, with C1 > 1. Indeed if C1 ≤ 1 in the latter range, then Lraw ≤ 2, a case already
excluded. In a strong interval use the unclipped quadratic template near the seam with exact

Lemma 7.2. If c ≤ 3, the strengthened memory bound (mr) uses L = max{1, (1 + c)/2}. For
c < 1 it exceeds k0 /(1 + c), and for 1 ≤ c ≤ 3 it exceeds k0 /2. Equation (7.9) therefore gives the
short-slab freezing contradiction. Shrink the slab to retain l < p∗ throughout. If c > 3, apply
Lemma 7.5: its positive speed is k+ = (c + 1)/4 > J, and the finite-step speeds have minimum
c/2 > J. They are all at least 1, the normal-width exponent increases forward, and no high-rate
switch is needed.
    In the weak alternative use the clipped template. Its right branch v > u(a) is linear of rate
C1 , with exact construction and crossing at almost every weak time. If c < 1, necessarily J = 0
here and C1 = c. At a typical mixed-trace time j, use both memory inequalities (mr). Together
with (7.9), their strict margin gives positive m above k0 − cν(a) outside an arbitrarily small
relative set of times. The low-rate linear freezing bound contradicts the short-slab observation.
In all other weak cases C1 ≥ 1. For 0 < m ≤ k0 use k+ = (1 + C1 )/2, and finite-step speeds have
minimum C1 ; both are strictly greater than J in the remaining range. For m > k0 , freeze on the
right by the linear bound. A continued freeze crossing left uses the quadratic freezing bound, the
same monotone clipped-template trace, coarse side, and line avoidance. The forward width rates
are C1 on the right and c on the left, both positive. Lemma 7.5 applies and gives a contradiction.

Pure linear cost: c2 = 0 < c1 . Write c = c1 . The linear construction has crossing. If classical
and c < 1, use both inequalities (mr) at a typical mixed-trace time and the short-slab argument
just given; there is now no side-of-seam restriction. In hybrid, (Pl) gives J = c + 1/2. Use a ray
from the corner at a speed slightly below c for c < 1, and slightly below (1 + c)/2 for c ≥ 1. It
lies in v < u(a), w < 0, and left memory gives

                                           k0 + 2Γ + ηc

                                               1+c
For c < 1 this exceeds the threshold k0 /(1 + c) for linear motion at speed c; for c ≥ 1 use the

point to freezing. Its hypotheses remain valid backwards. These motions contradict nonnegativity
and terminal equality. The same argument, with exact side, works classically for c ≥ 1 and
J ≥ (1 + c)/2. For the remaining classical case c ≥ 1, J < (1 + c)/2, use Lemma 7.5 on the pure
linear template: k+ = (1 + c)/2 > J, finite-step speeds are at least c > J, and any high-rate
freeze stays in a linear branch.

If C1 ≤ 0, then Lraw = 1, so this is positive and at least 1 − 2d. This explicitly includes every
negative q allowed by the affine reduction. If C1 > 0, the same conclusion follows whenever
d ≥ C1 /(1 + 2C1 ), since (1 − d)/(1 + C1 ) ≥ 1 − 2d is exactly that inequality. Take D = 1,
integrate Proposition 7.3, and contradict E(D, v0 ) = 0.
    It remains that C1 > 0 and d < C1 /(1 + 2C1 ). If C1 ≤ 1/2, then d < 1/4. From the terminal
