Take the spacetime position at the finer ancestor and its time, expressed in the moving frame of
C. Its discrepancy from the later row position is at most O(ρK ), since both the time discrepancy
and the centered velocity are bounded at that scale. Given C, the unit-depth name O1 and
C(a + h, v) determine one another to bounded lists: move between their times by at most O(ρ)
with bounded centered velocity. Thus

                                                       H(O1 | C)
                           iρ (O1 | C) −→ oT ,                   −→ oT .                     (9.11)
                                                        log(1/ρ)

Here H(· | ·) denotes Shannon conditional entropy. The angular statements give, for every needed
finite 0 < z ≤ 1,
                                    iρ (Zz | C) ≥ mz − o(1).                               (9.12)
For waves the expected score has limit mz. Moreover

                                      I(ZK ; C ′ | C)
                                                      = o(1).                                (9.13)
                                        log(1/ρ)

Indeed C ′ determines C to bounded ambiguity, ZK is equivalent given the roots to Av+Kh ,
and the two conditional angular entropies differ by the left side of Equation (stat), in s-units.
Both profiles remain valid on the residual law by Lemma 9.7. Equation (LP), including the
small-information version, therefore preserves Equation (9.12) after conditioning on C ′ .

Lemma 9.14 (Width derivative). At almost every interior wave point,

                                     ∂v D ≤ m + oT − d + 1.                                    (rv)

Proof. Given C, the bins (Z, W )1 determine C(a, v + h) except for at most O(ρ−1 ) possibilities.
The extra factor is exactly the further vertical precision: the new vertical grid is ρ2 in the old
units, while W1 has width ρ. The new angular center and the known horizontal bin determine
its change of tilt at the required accuracy.
    On the other hand, adjoining θ1 to (Z, W )1 adds conditional entropy at least (d−o(1)) log(1/ρ).
This follows from Lemma 9.10, since Y# is a finer name than (Z, W )1 , and from entropy
monotonicity. Equation (9.10) shows that (Z, W, θ)1 is determined to bounded lists by (Z1 , O1 )
given C. Its entropy is therefore at most (m + oT + o(1)) log(1/ρ). Subtract the time contribution,
add the one vertical branching exponent, and use the tangent increment of D(a, v + h) − D(a, v).
This proves Equation (rv).




                                                 147
9.6    The outgoing entropy inequality
Proposition 9.15 (Outgoing entropy). Use the exponent data d, p, Γ of Equation (9.1). For
almost every classical depth a ∈ (0, 1) at which D(a, 0) is differentiable and Lemma 9.13 applies,
put oT = ∂a D(a, 0) and m = 3 − p. For waves, let (a, v) be almost every interior point with
0 < a < 1 and 0 < v < (1 − a)/2 at which D is differentiable and Lemmas 9.12 and 9.13 apply,
and put oT = ∂a D(a, v) and m = m(a, v). In either case,

                                    oT ≥ 4d + min(1 − d, m/2).                          (round-out)

Proof. Fix the finite incidence queries required by Equations (inc)–(cond) for a prescribed strict
final loss. Later we choose the geometric and score tolerances small compared with these queries.
Given (C, C ′ ), sample M independent copies of (Z, W, θ), all sharing the representative O. Let
Li = (Zi )K be their fine angular names, represented by bounded grid points, and put ei = e(Li ).
The integer M will be fixed before taking the tangent limit.
    The time-spread estimate survives conditioning on all these pins. The own pin is already part
of Y#,i , and conditional independence gives

                              I(L̸=i ; Y#,i , θi | C) ≤ I(L̸=i ; C ′ | C)
                                                                I(Lj ; C ′ | C).
                                                         X
                                                     ≤
                                                         j̸=i

For clarity, the second inequality follows by writing the mutual information as conditional entropy
minus entropy given C, C ′ : the former is at most the sum of the marginal entropies, and the latter
equals their sum by conditional independence. Equation (9.13) makes the whole information
cost o(log(1/ρ)) for fixed M . Thus Lemma 9.10 and Equation (LP) give the required time scores
even with these pins. The angular score also satisfies, typically,

                                iρ ((Zk )z | C, C ′ , L̸=k ) ≥ mz − o(1),                     (9.14)

because the conditional law of copy k is unchanged by the other copies when C, C ′ are given.
     Use the law already obtained from Lemma 9.11, with a sufficiently small ε > 0. With
probability tending to one, every four distinct ei have determinant of magnitude at least ρCε ,
and every pair of angular representatives has separation at least ρCε . To check this, sample
successively in the first-coordinate-one affine slice of R4 . The affine span of the preceding points
is contained in an affine plane in its three remaining coordinates. The probability that the
next point is within ρε of such a plane tends to zero, uniformly in the previously chosen points.
Successive distances and bounded vector lengths give the determinant bound. A plane through
one preceding point also bounds the probability of a close pair; V is Lipschitz on the bounded
angular range. Pin rounding by O(ρK ) is negligible. There are finitely many tuples, so all the
conditions hold simultaneously. We call this the transverse event.
     Four transverse pins already give the lower bound 4d, for every m ≥ 0. Choose any four
of the sampled pins and apply Gram–Schmidt in their order to produce an orthonormal basis
(f1 , f2 , f3 , f4 ), renumbering these pins from 1 to 4 within this calculation. Reveal the bins of
O · fi in reverse order. At step i, adjoin Y#,i to the conditioning data. It predicts all the
previously read components to bounded ambiguity, since their directions are perpendicular to ei
and Equation (9.10) eliminates their time dependence. The new component determines θi to
at most ρ−Cε cells: its coefficient ei · fi is bounded below by ρCε . The time score with all pins
consequently contributes at least d − Cε − o(1) at each step. The chain rule, the list bounds,

                                                   148
and Equation (LP) show that the score of O1 is at least 4d − Cε − o(1). The change from the
orthonormal bins to ordinary O1 bins has bounded multiplicity given the pins. Drop the pins by
Equation (LP) and use Equation (9.11); then let ε ↓ 0. This proves Equation (round-out) when
m = 0.
   When m > 0, we sharpen two of these four time contributions by projecting onto a plane.
For i < j let πij be orthogonal projection onto the two-dimensional quotient by span(ei , ej ), with
measurable orthonormal coordinates, and define the projective direction

                                             gij (Z) = [πij e(Z)].

Only values on the transverse event matter, so the definition at zero may be arbitrary. Color
each triple i < j < k by the vector of quantized scores

                                        iρ ((gij (Zk ))z | C, C ′ , L̸=k )

at the finitely many required widths, with color intervals of length ε. Use one overflow color. It
is avoided with probability tending to one, since a bounded projective chart has at most O(ρ−z )
cells and counting controls the upper tail of its surprise. There are a fixed finite number of colors.
The same finite Ramsey theorem [24, Theorem B, p. 267] therefore supplies, for sufficiently large
fixed M , four indices whose triples all have the same color vector.
    Here is the geometric score constraint on such a quadruple. Let i < j < l < k. Given the
two directions gij (Zk ) and gil (Zk ) to width ρz , and all pins except Lk , the number of possible
good (Zk )z cells is at most ρ−Cε . Fix one compatible transverse witness Z0 . The first directional
constraint places e(Z) within O(ρz−Cε ) of span(ei , ej , e(Z0 )), and the second does the same for
span(ei , el , e(Z0 )). The determinant lower bound controls the inverse linear algebra, so their
intersection confines e(Z) within O(ρz−Cε ) of span(ei , e(Z0 )). In the first-coordinate-one slice
this is the secant line
                                         (1 − t)e(Li ) + te(Z0 ).
Its quadratic null residual is t(1 − t)|Li − Z0 |2 (up to the harmless choice of sign). The roots
0, 1 are separated and their derivatives have magnitude at least ρCε . Boundedness and the pair
separation therefore leave only neighborhoods of radius O(ρz−Cε ) of the two endpoints. Each
contains at most ρ−Cε angular ρz -cells. This proves the list bound, also when z ≤ Cε, by the
trivial ambient angular count.
    Apply this list bound and Equation (9.14) with the same conditioning data C, C ′ , L̸=k . The
joint score of the two directions is at least mz − Cε − o(1). Their marginal score sum is at least
their joint score up to o(1) outside a vanishing exceptional set, by Equation (LP) and the chain
rule. Thus

            iρ ((gij (Zk ))z | C, C ′ , L̸=k ) + iρ ((gil (Zk ))z | C, C ′ , L̸=k ) ≥ mz − Cε − o(1).   (9.15)

On a monochromatic quadruple these two scores differ by at most ε. Hence both are at least
(m/2)z − Cε − o(1) simultaneously at every required width. There are finitely many possible
quadruples; after passing to a subsequence, select fixed indices i < j < k and an event of fixed
positive probability on which all these bounds hold. This restriction has probability bounded
below independently of ρ after the parameters are fixed, so the finite score bounds and the
small-information consequences survive by likelihood comparison. Retain transversality as well.
    Use the side data D∗ = (C, L̸=k ), the point Q = πij O, and the line L through πij (0, Wk ) in
direction πij e(Zk ). They are incident up to O(ρK ). Restrict to a positive-probability bounded

                                                      149
slope chart after a fixed rotation; a finite collection of such charts covers all directions. We verify
Equation (cond) with exponents d and m/2.
    For the point score, adjoin Y#,k . This determines the line at the required conditioning
resolution to at most ρ−Cε choices, by transversality and fine pinning. Conditioning additionally
on its line cell therefore costs at most Cε + o(1) in score. Given these data, the projected point
                            ′
determines θk at width ρz to at most ρ−Cε possibilities, because the projected speed is at least
ρCε . Lemma 9.10, its preservation under the other pins, and Equation (LP) give
                          iρ (Qz ′ | Lz , D∗ ) ≥ dz ′ − Cε − o(1),    z ′ ≤ z.
For the line score, adjoin C ′ . The point is then known, and the line cell determines its direction
at the same resolution, up to the chart’s controlled lists. The directional lower bound from
Equation (9.15), with the same conditioning C, C ′ , L̸=k , gives
                              iρ (Lz ′ | Qz , D∗ ) ≥ (m/2)z ′ − Cε − o(1).
These are exactly the finite-grid hypotheses of Equation (cond). Choose ε and the earlier
tolerances sufficiently small. Equation (inc) yields
                             iρ (Q1 | D∗ ) ≥ d + min(1, d + m/2) − o(1)                          (9.16)
to any prescribed strict loss.
     It remains to recover the other two coordinates of O. Let fi be the unit vector along ei ,
and let fj be the unit vector along the component of ej perpendicular to ei . After Q1 , reveal
(O · fj )1 , then (O · fi )1 . For the former, adjoin Y#,j ; it predicts Q1 to bounded ambiguity because
πij ej = 0, and the new coordinate reveals θj to ρ−Cε choices. For the latter, adjoin Y#,i ; it
predicts both preceding coordinates because they annihilate ei , and this last coordinate reveals
θi . Each conditional score is therefore at least d − Cε − o(1) by the time estimate with the pin
data. All these conditioning variables are available: D∗ includes the own pins Li , Lj , and the
information argument at the start of the proof permits the other pins.
     Combining these two contributions with Equation (9.16) and then dropping D∗ down to C
by Equation (LP) gives
            iρ (O1 | C) ≥ 3d + min(1, d + m/2) − o(1) = 4d + min(1 − d, m/2) − o(1)
on the retained event of fixed positive probability. Its deterministic limit in Equation (9.11)
forces the asserted inequality.
    The order of choices is essential. Fix the desired final strict loss and the finite grids in
Equation (inc); next choose the geometric and color tolerances; next fix a Ramsey-sufficient M ;
then let dyadic h ↓ 0 through the typical point, taking all previous finite-list and inner limits
sufficiently accurate for that h. Every restriction made after the stationarity estimate has a
positive probability bound at these fixed choices. No arbitrary subpower restriction is used to
preserve a mutual-information assertion.

9.7    The differential contradiction
Lemma 9.16 (A two-direction descent). Let Ω = {(t, v) : 0 < t < 1, 0 < v < t/2}. Suppose
that E is locally Lipschitz, nonnegative, and has zero trace on v = t/2. Let m be measurable and
nondecreasing in t at each fixed v, with this condition allowed outside a null set of v. If c > 0
and q ∈ R, the following pair of almost everywhere inequalities is impossible:
                   Et + 12 Ev ≤ −c    where m ≤ q,         Et ≤ −c    where m ≥ q.

                                                  150
Proof. Fix 0 < t0 < t1 < 1, put T = t1 − t0 , and let N be the null set where differentiation or an
applicable inequality fails. Fubini after the affine change (t, δ) 7→ (t, t/2 − δ) shows that almost
every line γδ (t) = (t, t/2 − δ) meets N in a null set. Ordinary Fubini gives the same property for
almost every vertical section. Include exceptional monotonicity sections in the latter null set.
Choose a good δ > 0 sufficiently small that δ < t0 /4 and E0 := E(t0 , t0 /2 − δ) < cT /2; the trace
condition permits this. The function f (t) = E(γδ (t)) is Lipschitz on [t0 , t1 ]; let B be a Lipschitz
constant.
    Set A = {t : m(γδ (t)) > q}. If |A| = 0, the first inequality integrates to f (t1 ) ≤ E0 − cT < 0.
Otherwise let τ be the essential first time in A. Then τ < t1 , A has zero measure before τ , and
every sufficiently short interval immediately after τ has positive intersection with A. Integration
before τ gives f (τ ) ≤ E0 − c(τ − t0 ). Choose t∗ ∈ A arbitrarily close to τ from above, with
v∗ = t∗ /2 − δ a good vertical section. Such a choice exists because this affine map of t∗ preserves
null sets. Monotonicity gives m(t, v∗ ) > q for t ≥ t∗ , so the second inequality integrates along
this vertical segment:

                   E(t1 , v∗ ) ≤ f (t∗ ) − c(t1 − t∗ ) ≤ E0 − cT + (B + c)(t∗ − τ ).

Taking t∗ sufficiently close to τ makes the right side negative, again a contradiction. Each
segment is a compact subset of Ω, so local Lipschitz regularity suffices.

Proof of Theorem 9.1. In the classical contradiction, Equation (9.6) gives m/2 = 1 − d + Γ.
Proposition 9.15 therefore implies ∂a D(a, 0) ≥ 1 + 3d almost everywhere. The profile is Lipschitz
and D(0, 0) ≥ 0 by continuity of the lower bound in Proposition 9.8. Integration gives D(1, 0) ≥
1 + 3d. But the endpoint bound gives

                                  D(1, 0) ≤ p + d = 1 + 3d − 2Γ,

a contradiction. Hence Γcl,round ≤ 100η.
    For waves suppose the exponent is larger than 100η; the classical conclusion just obtained
supplies the strict comparison needed in Proposition 9.3. Put t = 1 − a and use the same letter E
for the excess in these coordinates. It is Lipschitz, nonnegative, and zero on v = t/2. The version
of m from Lemma 9.12 is nondecreasing in t at fixed v. Equations (rv) and (round-out) imply

                        Et = 1 + 3d − 2Γ − oT ,         Ev ≤ m + oT − d − 3.

If m ≤ 2 − 2d, then oT ≥ 4d + m/2 ≥ m + 5d − 1, so

                                         Et + 21 Ev ≤ −2Γ.

If m ≥ 2 − 2d, then oT ≥ 1 + 3d, so Et ≤ −2Γ. Apply Lemma 9.16 with c = 2Γ > 0 and
q = 2 − 2d. This is a contradiction. The free wave exponent is therefore at most 100η. The
finite-stack comparison completes all assertions of the theorem.


10     The physical half-wave and frequency summation
We now deduce Theorem 1.1 from the wave assertion of Theorem 9.1. We use the negative
half-wave                                  Z

Complex conjugation, followed by the change of variable ξ 7→ −ξ, interchanges the two signs.
The same conjugation commutes with every real radial Sobolev multiplier.
   There are two points to verify before the round-cone estimate applies to the physical wave:
the packet state must have the required regularity on the original time slice, and its terminal
masses must control the physical L3 norm. We prove both at one frequency scale, then sum over
spatial pieces, amplitude levels, and frequency annuli.

10.1    The estimate on one annulus
For 0 < H ≤ 1, let aH be a smooth multiplier supported where c ≤ H|ξ| ≤ C. All derivatives of
aH (H −1 ·) are assumed uniformly bounded. Write

Proposition 10.1 (Annular estimate). For every δ > 0,

                              ∥WH g∥L3 (R3 ×[1,2]) ≤ Cδ H −δ ∥g∥L3 (R3 ) .                  (10.2)

                     |KH,τ (x)| ≤ CN H N (1 + |x|)−N          (|x| ≥ R, 1 ≤ τ ≤ 2).         (10.4)

To see this, substitute ξ = H −1 ω. The gradient of the phase in ω is H −1 (x − τ ω/|ω|), whose

over j. Translation reduces these local estimates to input supported in one fixed compact set K
and output in one fixed bounded set.

10.3     Input regularity on the physical initial slice
The physical initial slice τ = 0 is the spacelike plane y = −t. We verify the full regularity
family on this plane, including the nonround tests. After a kernel truncation uniform over all
rows, let E ⊂ K comprise the physical initial points joined to a test by retained kernel pairs.
Equation (10.8) then bounds the test’s row mass by CB 2 |E|, up to an error negligible relative to
∥g∥22 . We therefore need a uniform kernel-localization estimate and a bound for |E| by the test
weight r4 f 1−η , allowing an arbitrarily small power loss.
Lemma 10.2 (Uniform packet localization from the initial slice). Fix γ0 > 0 and put Λ = H −γ0 .
The operator g 7→ V1 v(T ) can be truncated to the following relation between an input point

If r is bounded by a fixed constant, a nonempty compatible set has bounded A∗ , because its

coordinates u in Equation (10.15) is independent of x, so hx = Fx . Choosing the cutoff between
the two ranges once and for all proves a uniform lower bound for the restricted gradient.
    The polynomial h has degree at most two. In a fixed bounded box, the set {|h| ≤ q, |∇h| ≥ c}
has volume at most Cq, uniformly
                            √       in its coefficients: partition according to a coordinate derivative
of magnitude at least c/ 3, hold the other coordinates fixed, and split the one-dimensional
fiber at the zeros of that derivative. There are at most two monotonic pieces, on each of which
the mean value theorem gives length O(q). Applying this with q = CΛC2 f , and then using
Equation (10.16) (or r4 after recentering), gives |E| ≤ CΛC3 r4 f in the small-f case. Since
f ≤ f 1−η , this suffices. In the complementary case the missing factor f 1−η costs only another
fixed power of Λ, as already noted.

Proposition 10.4 (Regularity of the initial analyzed state). Fix 0 < η < 1 as in Definition 8.2.
For every γ > 0, the state in Equation (10.7) has input regularity constant

                                              κ = Cη,γ H −γ B 2                                 (10.19)

at each of the bounded starting times T . Under Equation (10.11), it also satisfies the polynomial-
size and negligible-tail hypotheses of the round-wave experiment with terminal length s = H.

Proof. Choose γ0 > 0 so small that C1 γ0 < γ/2 in Lemma 10.3. For a test T , restrict g to its
predecessor set E. The truncated operator of Lemma 10.2 sends g1K\E to zero on T . The
unrestricted operator on g1E has uniformly bounded L2 norm by Equation (10.8). Thus, for
every N ,
             µT (T ) ≤ C∥g1E ∥22 + CN H N ∥g∥22 ≤ CB 2 H −C1 γ0 r4 f 1−η + CN H N B 2 .
The uncertainty condition gives the uniform weight floor

                                      r4 f 1−η = (rf )4 f −3−η ≥ H 2 .                          (10.20)

Taking N large proves regularity with Equation (10.19), including every admissible width.
    The ratios e/κ and κ/e are bounded above by fixed powers of H −1 by Equation (10.11). The
kernel proof gives negligible phase tails outside a fixed polynomial box: the input is compact,
all velocities and durations are bounded, and arbitrary decay is available. Its pointwise kernel
bound also gives a phase-density bound by a fixed power of H −1 times B 2 , and hence times e.
All these estimates are uniform along any sequence of the present inputs.

assignments with weights bounded above and below by positive constants. For every 0 < σ < 1
and N ,              ZT +1 Z

Proof. At length H, the angular aperture is dH = 1 and both spatial packet widths are H. All
row angles lie in a fixed bounded set. A fixed round test with r ≥ 1, centered at a given cube,
contains that cube and all these angles. Its weight r4 is a fixed constant, and different cube
assignments are disjoint in row space.

10.5    Sobolev summation and the remaining exponents
Proof of Theorem 1.1. Let h = J ε f . Use a smooth radial partition into a low-frequency cutoff
and annuli |ξ| ≍ 2n , n ≥ 0. On the nth annulus put the multiplier J −ε into aH , where H = 2−n .
After factoring out H ε , the rescaled symbols have uniformly bounded derivatives. Proposition 10.1,
applied with δ = ε/2, gives

                           ∥W (Pn J −ε h)∥L3x,τ (R3 ×[1,2]) ≤ Cε 2−nε/2 ∥h∥3 .

The series is summable by the triangle inequality.
    We also record a direct justification at frequency zero. For a smooth cutoff χ supported in
|ξ| ≤ 2, decompose
                             χ(ξ)e−iτ |ξ| = χ(ξ) + χ(ξ)(e−iτ |ξ| − 1).
The first term has an integrable convolution kernel. Split the second into annuli of radius
λ = 2−j ≤ 1. In variables ξ = λω, its symbol and its derivatives through order 4 are O(λ) on
a fixed annulus, uniformly for 1 ≤ τ ≤ 2. Integration by parts using (1 − ∆ω )2 shows that its
inverse Fourier transform has L1 norm O(λ). These norms sum. The smooth low-frequency
factor J −ε preserves the same bounds. Young’s inequality therefore handles the low-frequency
term. The resulting inequality is exactly the negative-sign version of Theorem 1.1; complex
conjugation proves the stated sign.

Corollary 10.6 (The positive-regularity local-smoothing range). For every 2 < p < ∞ and
α > max{0, 1 − 3/p},

                      ∥U f ∥Lp (R3 ×[1,2]) ≤ Cp,α ∥J α f ∥Lp (R3 )         (f ∈ S(R3 )).      (10.24)

Proof. Take a smooth radial annular cutoff Pλ = ϕ(|D|/λ), λ ≥ 1. Plancherel gives

                                           ∥U Pλ ∥L2x →L2x,τ ≤ C,

and Proposition 10.1 gives, for every δ > 0, ∥U Pλ ∥L3x →L3x,τ ≤ Cδ λδ . We first prove the remaining
endpoint bound
                                      ∥U Pλ ∥L∞    ∞ ≤ Cλ.
                                              x →Lx,τ
                                                                                               (10.25)

                                  r   0

Put I(s) =    0 e ρϕ(ρ/λ) dρ. For every N ,

                  |I(s)| ≤ CN λ2 (1 + λ|s|)−N ,          |I ′ (s)| ≤ CN λ3 (1 + λ|s|)−N .

The kernel is a constant times r−1 (I(τ + r) − I(τ − r)). If 0 < r ≤ 1/2, the mean value theorem
and 1 ≤ τ ≤ 2 bound it by CN λ3−N , which also removes the apparent singularity at r = 0. If
r ≥ 1/2, its absolute value is at most

                              λ2

    Interpolation between the L2 and L3 estimates gives an arbitrarily small positive power of
λ for every 2 < p ≤ 3. For p > 3, interpolate the L3 bound with Equation (10.25); the L3
interpolation weight is 3/p, giving

                                ∥U Pλ ∥Lpx →Lpx,τ ≤ Cp,δ λ1−3/p+3δ/p .

Choose δ small enough that this exponent, or its counterpart for p ≤ 3, is strictly below α. The
same estimates hold for uniformly smooth radial annular symbol families, as both the kernel
proof and Proposition 10.1 are uniform for those families. We may thus insert J −α into each
annular symbol, factor out λ−α , and sum the resulting geometric series. The low-frequency
kernels have uniformly bounded L1 norms by the preceding proof, which applies to every Lp .
This proves Equation (10.24).

    The same estimate describes the wave equation with independent initial displacement u0 and
velocity u1 . For Schwartz data its solution is

                                                           sin(t|D|)
                                u(t) = cos(t|D|)u0 +                 u1 ,
                                                              |D|

and for the exponents in Corollary 10.6,

                         ∥u∥Lp (R3 ×[1,2]) ≤ Cp,α ∥J α u0 ∥p + ∥J α−1 u1 ∥p .
                                                                              


At high frequency this follows from the two signs of the half-wave estimate and the order −1
velocity multiplier. At low frequency, retain the combined multiplier sin(t|ξ|)/|ξ|: it is smooth
at zero and, after a smooth frequency cutoff, has a uniformly integrable kernel for 1 ≤ t ≤ 2.
Density extends the solution estimate to the indicated Sobolev data.




                                                   159
10.6    Maximal half-waves and convergence to the initial data
The spacetime estimate also controls the largest value of the wave over a finite time interval,
at the cost of additional regularity. This gives a pointwise interpretation of the evolution for
Sobolev initial data. For s ∈ R and 1 < p < ∞, write W s,p (R3 ) = {f ∈ S ′ (R3 ) : J s f ∈ Lp (R3 )},
with norm ∥J s f ∥p .
Corollary 10.7 (Maximal half-wave and pointwise convergence). Let 3 ≤ p < ∞ and s > 1−2/p.
Then
                  sup |U f (·, t)|     ≤ Cp,s ∥J s f ∥Lp (R3 ) (f ∈ S(R3 )).        (10.26)
                     0<t<1             Lp (R3 )

The family has a bounded maximal extension to W s,p (R3 ), and in that extension every f ∈
W s,p (R3 ) satisfies
                      lim U f (x, t) = f (x) for almost every x ∈ R3 .              (10.27)
                             t↓0

Proof. For p ≥ 3, Corollary 10.6 gives the 1/p− local smoothing gain relative to the fixed-time
loss 1 − 2/p. The established maximal implication [2, Section 3.5, Equation (3.24)] therefore
gives (10.26). At each frequency, Sobolev embedding in time costs arbitrarily more than 1/p
derivatives; together with local smoothing, this requires regularity arbitrarily close to 1 − 2/p
from above. The strict inequality on s absorbs the remaining positive losses. The implication
uses scaling to cover the time intervals approaching zero.
    For Schwartz data, the Fourier integral converges uniformly to the initial data as t ↓ 0.
Schwartz functions are dense in W s,p , and the maximal bound gives the extension and carries
this convergence to every such datum. More explicitly, for a Schwartz approximation g to f , the
measure of the set where lim supt↓0 |U f − f | > η is at most Cp,s η −p ∥J s (f − g)∥pp . Letting g → f
proves (10.27).


11     Bochner–Riesz consequences
We apply the established local-smoothing implications of Beltran–Hickman–Sogge to Corol-
lary 10.6. The maximal result concerns the family in (1.3); the subsequent nonmaximal result
concerns the individual linear means. These means recover a function by gradually admitting
higher frequencies, with order δ smoothing the boundary of the Fourier ball. Uniform Lp bounds
make each approximation stable. A maximal bound is stronger: it controls the error for all radii
at once, which allows the elementary convergence for Schwartz data to pass to every Lp datum
by density.
    For p ≥ 3, the local-smoothing input has an arbitrarily small positive loss beyond 1 − 3/p.
The strict inequality δ > 1 − 3/p absorbs that loss in the established implication. Thus the
conclusion below requires only Lp integrability of the data, in contrast to the Sobolev hypothesis
in the maximal half-wave corollary.
Theorem 11.1 (Maximal Bochner–Riesz bounds and almost-everywhere summation). Let
3 ≤ p < ∞ and δ > 1 − 3/p. Then
                          ∥B∗δ f ∥Lp (R3 ) ≤ Cp,δ ∥f ∥Lp (R3 )   (f ∈ S(R3 )).                  (11.1)
The family has a bounded maximal extension to Lp (R3 ), and in that extension every f ∈ Lp (R3 )
satisfies
                             δ
                        lim BR f (x) = f (x) for almost every x ∈ R3 .                   (11.2)
                         R→∞


                                                    160
In particular, every δ > 0 is allowed when p = 3.

Proof. For p ≥ 3, the sharp fixed-time Sobolev loss is 1 − 2/p. Corollary 10.6 permits every
exponent α = 1 − 3/p + ε, ε > 0, and hence gives the 1/p− local smoothing hypothesis for the
Euclidean half-wave in [2, Proposition 3.3]. That proposition applies for 2n/(n − 1) ≤ p < ∞
and order above
                                            1 1      1
                                                       
                                  max n − − , 0 .
                                            p 2      2
For n = 3 and p ≥ 3 this threshold is 1 − 3/p. The arbitrary positive loss in the local-smoothing
input is absorbed by the strict inequality on δ.
   The cited proposition uses the multiplier (1 − t|ξ|)δ+ . Write LδR for the same means with
t = R−1 and Lδ∗ f = supR>0 |LδR f |. It gives the asserted strong bound for Lδ∗ . Here is the
transfer to the squared-radius convention in (1.3). Choose χ ∈ Cc∞ ((0, ∞)) equal to one on a
neighborhood of [1/2, 1], and put

                   a(ξ) = χ(|ξ|)(1 + |ξ|)δ ,         b(ξ) = (1 − χ(|ξ|))(1 − |ξ|2 )δ+ .

Corollary 11.2 (The full strict nonmaximal range). For 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞, with 1/∞ = 0, set

                                    δ
                                  ∥BR f ∥Lp (R3 ) ≤ Cp,δ ∥f ∥Lp (R3 ) .                    (11.3)


                                                  161
Proof. Proposition 3.2 of [2], with the same local-smoothing input and the same smooth-factor
transfer, gives (11.3) for 3 ≤ p < ∞ and δ > 1 − 3/p; dilation makes the constants uniform in R.
For 2 ≤ p ≤ 3, fix any δ > 0 and interpolate the p = 3 bound at that order with the L2 bound
from Plancherel. This gives the required threshold δBR (p) = 0 in this interval. The multiplier is
real and even, so duality gives the range 1 < p < 2, since δBR (p) = δBR (p′ ). Finally, at p = 1
and p = ∞ the stated condition is δ > 1. The inverse Fourier transform K δ of (1 − |ξ|2 )δ+ obeys
|K δ (x)| ≤ Cδ (1 + |x|)−2−δ and is therefore integrable at these orders. Its dilates have the same
L1 norm, so Young’s inequality supplies both endpoint bounds.
    For 1 ≤ p < ∞ in this strict range, the uniform bounds also give BR  δ f → f in Lp as R → ∞.

One first checks this for Schwartz functions with compact Fourier support and then uses their
density in Lp together with the uniform operator bound.
    The interpolation and duality in this corollary apply to the fixed-radius linear operators; the
maximal conclusion remains the range of Theorem 11.1. For comparison, the separate article
[20, Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 12.4] proves positive-order L3 boundedness and the same strict
nonmaximal range by a different argument. That article is not an input to the deduction above.

11.1    Spherical restriction and Kakeya maximal estimates
The strict multiplier range has further established consequences for Fourier extension and tube
geometry. Let dσ be surface measure on S 2 , and define the spherical extension operator by
                                                Z
                                     Eg(x) =           eix·ω g(ω) dσ(ω).
                                                  S2
Tao’s Bochner–Riesz-to-restriction implication [29], also described in [2, Section 3.1], applies to
Corollary 11.2. By duality it gives
                           ∥Eg∥Lr (R3 ) ≤ Cr ∥g∥Lr (S 2 ,dσ) ,      3 < r < ∞.                 (11.4)
Thus the multiplier bounds also control the physical-space concentration of waves with Fourier
support on the sphere. The strict family of positive-order Bochner–Riesz bounds supplies the
premise; no order-zero multiplier assertion is needed.
    For 0 < h < 1 and ω ∈ S 2 , let Th (ω) be the family of all unit-length tubes of radius h parallel
to ω, at arbitrary spatial positions, and put
                                                       1
                                                          Z
                                Kh F (ω) = sup               |F (x)| dx.
                                            T ∈Th (ω) |T | T

The restriction-to-Kakeya implication of [31, Section 4, Proposition 4.1] applies to the diagonal
extension range (11.4). For r > 3 sufficiently close to 3, it first gives restricted weak type (a, a),
where a = r/(r − 2) < 3, with norm O(h−2(3/a−1) ) for the tubes above. Interpolation with the
trivial L∞ bound gives strong type (3, 3) with loss h−2(1−a/3) . Since a ↑ 3 as r ↓ 3, this yields
the Kakeya maximal estimate, for every F ∈ L3 (R3 ),
                   ∥Kh F ∥L3 (S 2 ,dσ) ≤ Cε h−ε ∥F ∥L3 (R3 ) ,     ε > 0,   0 < h < 1.         (11.5)
This quantitatively controls averages over thin tubes in every direction. In particular, a compact
set containing a unit line segment in each direction has Hausdorff dimension three. Wang–Zahl
already proved the Hausdorff and Minkowski dimension assertions [30, Theorem 1.1]; the maximal
estimate is a stronger quantitative conclusion. The deductions here use the established Bochner–
Riesz-to-restriction-to-Kakeya chain and also apply to the independent Bochner–Riesz result
cited above.
